AdS N N -body problem at large spin
Petr Kravchuk, Jeremy A. Mann

Abstract
Motivated by the problem of multi-twist operators in general CFTs, we study the leading-twist states of the N N -body problem in AdS \mathrm{AdS} at large spin J J . We find that for the majority of states the effective quantum-mechanical problem becomes semiclassical with ℏ = 1 / J \hbar=1/J . The classical system at J = ∞ J=\infty has N − 2 N-2 degrees of freedom, and the classical phase space is identified with the positive Grassmannian Gr + ​ ( 2 , N ) \mathrm{Gr}_{+}(2,N) . The quantum problem is recovered via a Berezin-Toeplitz quantization of a classical Hamiltonian, which we describe explicitly. For N = 3 N=3 the classical system has one degree of freedom and a detailed structure of the spectrum can be obtained from Bohr-Sommerfeld conditions. For all N N , we show that the lowest excited states are approximated by a harmonic oscillator and find explicit expressions for their energies.



1
Introduction

The spectrum of scaling dimensions
\Delta
of local operators in an interacting conformal field theory is, generally speaking, extremely complex. This can be seen from basic thermodynamic considerations. Nevertheless, there are various limits in the spectrum that can be understood analytically. The focus of this work is the large spin limit
J\to\infty
and bounded twist
\tau=\Delta-J
.

In this limit, simple universal structures have been observed first in perturbation theory
[
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
,
5
]
and later in the general non-perturbative context
[
6
,
7
,
8
]
. Specifically, it is now largely a theorem
[
9
,
10
]
that, given a pair of local primary operators
{\mathcal{O}}_{1},{\mathcal{O}}_{2}
with twists
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
,
1
1

1

For simplicity, we only consider traceless-symmetric operators in this paper.
for sufficiently large spin
J
there exists a family of local primary operators
[{\mathcal{O}}_{1}{\mathcal{O}}_{2}]_{J}
of spin
J
, such that as
J\to\infty
, their twist approaches

\displaystyle\tau_{J}\to\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}.

(1)

There also exist subleading families whose twist asymptotes to
\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}+2n
(see
[
10
]
for a recent mathematical proof of their existence); for simplicity, we will focus on the leading family
[{\mathcal{O}}_{1}{\mathcal{O}}_{2}]_{J}
. The operators
[{\mathcal{O}}_{1}{\mathcal{O}}_{2}]_{J}
are referred to as the double-twist operators. A lot of work in recent years (see
[
11
]
for a review) went into studying their properties and, in particular, in computing the large-
J
expansions of the anomalous dimension
\gamma
, which is defined as

\displaystyle\tau=\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}+\gamma.

(2)

\log J
{\mathcal{O}}_{2}
{\mathcal{O}}_{1}
{\mathcal{O}}_{3}
{\mathcal{O}}_{1}
{\mathcal{O}}_{2}

Figure 1
:
A slice of
\mathrm{AdS}
space at constant global time. Left: a two-body state in
\mathrm{AdS}
at large
J
. Right: a hierarchical three-body state in
\mathrm{AdS}
.

The existence of double-twist states can easily be understood holographically. The primary states created by
{\mathcal{O}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{2}
are each dual to localized excitations at rest in the center of the
\mathrm{AdS}_{d+1}
. The primary state created by
[{\mathcal{O}}_{1}{\mathcal{O}}_{2}]_{J}
can be then viewed as the state which contains the excitation created by
{\mathcal{O}}_{1}
and the excitation created by
{\mathcal{O}}_{2}
, diametrically opposed, and both orbiting around the center of
\mathrm{AdS}_{d+1}
, see figure
1
. The geodesic distance between these excitations is proportional to
\log J
, and so in the limit
J\to\infty
the interaction between them can be neglected, explaining the twist additivity. We will review below why it is the twist and not the scaling dimension that is additive. The fact that we are dealing with a two-body problem simplifies the calculation of the twist correction
\gamma
.

A natural question is whether this picture can be extended to
N
-body states or, equivalently, multi-twist operators
[{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\cdots{\mathcal{O}}_{N}]_{J}
. While OPE coefficients of such operators have been a subject of active study in holographic CFT
[
12
,
13
,
14
,
15
]
, much less is known about their anomalous dimensions, especially in theories with finite central charge. An obvious approach is to build “hierarchical” states by iterating the double-twist construction, for example

\displaystyle[[{\mathcal{O}}_{1}{\mathcal{O}}_{2}]_{\ell_{1}}{\mathcal{O}}_{3}]_{J},\quad[[[{\mathcal{O}}_{1}{\mathcal{O}}_{2}]_{\ell_{1}}{\mathcal{O}}_{3}]_{\ell_{2}}{\mathcal{O}}_{4}]_{J},\quad[[{\mathcal{O}}_{1}{\mathcal{O}}_{2}]_{\ell_{1}}[{\mathcal{O}}_{3}{\mathcal{O}}_{4}]_{\ell_{2}}]_{J},\quad\cdots.

(3)

As long as we choose
\ell_{i}
such that all the inner double-twists exist, the above expressions define double-twist families of states labeled by spin
J
. In fact, it is reasonable to expect that all Regge trajectories at large spin have a double-twist description of the above form
[
16
]
.

In order to be able to compute, say, the anomalous dimension of
[[{\mathcal{O}}_{1}{\mathcal{O}}_{2}]_{\ell_{1}}{\mathcal{O}}_{3}]_{J}
in terms of the operators
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
, we have to assume that
1\ll\ell_{1}\ll J
in order for both double-twist constructions to be under analytical control. Indeed, the state
[{\mathcal{O}}_{1}{\mathcal{O}}_{2}]_{\ell_{1}}
has size
\sim\log\ell_{1}
, and the separation
\log J
between
[{\mathcal{O}}_{1}{\mathcal{O}}_{2}]_{\ell_{1}}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{3}
needs to be much larger than the size of either state so that the system can be viewed as a two-body problem, see figure
1
.

The condition
1\ll\ell_{1}\ll J
also guarantees that the pairwise distances between the
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
are all large. This is important for two reasons. Firstly, it makes the interactions weak, and we can hope to understand them in a perturbative fashion. Secondly, it ensures that only the long-distance physics, at scales much larger than the AdS scale, is important. One can therefore hope that the AdS description is not essential and this class of states has calculable properties in any (possibly non-holographic) CFT.
2
2

2

That the hierarchical states are calculable in non-holographic CFTs is of course known and follows from the CFT constructions of double-twist states
[
6
,
7
]
. Our point here is that large AdS distances are necessary for this to be true.
An extension of this regime, where
\ell_{1}\sim\sqrt{J}
has been studied in
[
17
]
using multi-point bootstrap.

In this paper, we study a different and the most numerous class of multi-twist states in AdS. For these states it is still true that the pairwise distances between the
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
are all large. In particular, the interactions between the
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
are still suppressed, and we still expect that our results should be applicable to general CFTs. However, in this class of states there is no hierarchy between the pairwise distances and therefore these states cannot be studied by iterating the solution to the two-body problem.

{\mathcal{O}}_{1}
{\mathcal{O}}_{2}
{\mathcal{O}}_{3}

Figure 2
:
A typical three-body state of the class considered in this paper.

A typical representative of this class of states is illustrated in figure
2
, where the excitations sit at the vertices of an equilateral triangle that rotates around its center, giving rise to the total spin
J
. As we will see, when all
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
are identical, this typically describes the state with the smallest value of
|\gamma|
, where the anomalous dimension
\gamma
is now defined as

\displaystyle\tau=\tau_{1}+\cdots+\tau_{N}+\gamma.

(4)

Our goal will be to understand in detail the state in figure
2
as well as its excitations. We will show that such states can be described by a semiclassical quantum-mechanical problem with
\hbar=J^{-1}
. The fraction of states that can be described in this way tends to
1
as
J\to\infty
.

N=3

) as a function of spin

. In this figure,
\Delta_{\phi}=1.234,\,\Delta_{\sigma}=0.6734
, and the constant
b_{0}
is defined in (
64
).

Same as figure

but for
N=4
.

In figures
3
and
4
we show a typical spectrum of anomalous dimensions for
N=3
and
N=4
(in a model that we describe below), where all
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
are identical scalars
\phi
. The high-lying states form obvious double-twist families which approach constant values of
\gamma
at large
J
. For instance, the states with the largest value of
\gamma/b_{0}
in figure
3
form the family
[[\phi\phi]_{0}\phi]_{J}
.
3
3

3

The states
[[\phi\phi]_{0}\phi]_{J}
form two Regge trajectories: an even-spin and an odd-spin trajectory. Both are shown in figure
3
and appear as separate families due to
(-1)^{J}
terms in the anomalous dimension.
Lower-lying states form families
[[\phi\phi]_{\ell_{1}}\phi]_{J}
with even
\ell_{1}>0
. The limit
J\to\infty
with
\ell_{1}
fixed or growing slowly can be understood using the double-twist construction.

The state illustrated in figure
2
is the lowest-lying state in figure
3
. In section
3
, we will derive for
N=3
states a Bohr-Sommerfeld rule of the form

\displaystyle c_{0}(J^{\Delta_{\sigma}}\gamma)+c_{1}(J^{\Delta_{\sigma}}\gamma)+\cdots=2\pi(k+\tfrac{1}{2}),

(5)

where each
c_{n}(E)
is
J^{1-n}
times a
J
-independent function, and
\Delta_{\sigma}>0
describes the decay of interactions at large distances. Out of the
J/6+O(1)
states at spin
J
, this condition accurately describes the spectrum of
\sim J/6
states above and including the lowest-lying state in figure
3
. We will compute the functions
c_{0}
and
c_{1}
explicitly, and show that this gives a very good agreement with the exact spectrum (see figure
12
in section
3
).

We consider the general case
N\geq 3
in section
4
. We find that unlike in the case of
N=3
, for
N>3
the effective quantum-mechanical description has more than one degree of freedom, and only the “leading-order Bohr-Sommerfeld rule”, i.e. the Weyl law can be written down. We explicitly describe the classical phase space and the classical Hamiltonian in the case of pair interactions, and we verify that this correctly predicts the semiclassical density of states (see figures
16
and
17
in section
4
). We also obtain explicit results for the lowest-lying excitations with
k\ll J
which can be described by an effective harmonic oscillator.

Our methods are quite general and apply to a large class of interactions. In particular, we expect the main conclusions of this work to carry over to general, non-holographic CFTs. However, since we do not know the effective multi-twist interactions in non-holographic CFTs, in this paper we rely on an AdS toy model as an example. Specifically, we will consider a QFT of two scalars
\Phi
and
\Sigma
in rigid
\mathrm{AdS}_{d+1}
with the action given by

\displaystyle S=\int d^{d+1}x\sqrt{-g}\left(-\partial_{\mu}\Phi\partial^{\mu}\overline{\Phi}-m^{2}_{\Phi}\Phi\overline{\Phi}-\tfrac{1}{2}(\partial\Sigma)^{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}m_{\Sigma}^{2}\Sigma^{2}+\lambda\Phi\overline{\Phi}\Sigma\right),

(6)

and where the scalar
\Phi
is complex and carries a
U(1)
charge
1
.
4
4

4

We introduce a conserved charge exclusively to avoid discussing various extraneous processes involving annihilation of pairs of
\Phi
particles.
We denote the dual CFT operators by
\phi
and
\sigma
.

We will then study the leading-twist states with
U(1)
charge
N
at a given spin
J
, which are the states with
N

\Phi
particles with Yukawa interactions mediated by
\Sigma
. Since we expect the interactions to be suppressed at large
J
, we will only focus on the leading
O(g^{2})
contribution to the anomalous dimension
\gamma
. One way to think about this model is that for
N=2
it reproduces the full result for
\gamma
that the Lorentzian inversion formula
[
8
,
18
,
19
]
gives for the
t
-channel exchange of a scalar operator
\sigma
.
5
5

5

Most of the time we will be interested only in the leading term in the large-
J
expansion. In this case, there is no difference between scalar exchanges and spinning exchanges, and our results for this model can be viewed as describing the
t
-channel exchange of any local operator
{\mathcal{O}}
, after replacing
\Delta_{\sigma}
by
\tau_{\mathcal{O}}
and suitably modifying the three-point couplings.
We describe this model in more detail in section
2
.

When most of the results of this paper were in place, the work
[
20
]
appeared which studied a similar class of AdS models. For their models, they found the spectrum of leading-twist multi-twist states numerically and compared it to the data of various CFTs. The analytical analysis of
N>2
states in
[
20
]
was mostly limited to the leading term in the anomalous dimension
\gamma
of the state in figure
2
. Our focus, as mentioned above, is instead on developing a solution theory for a much more general class of states. While we do not construct models as elaborate as those of
[
20
]
, our methods should allow an analytic calculation of many of their numerical results at large spin.

The rest of this paper is organized as follows. In section
1.1
we describe an intuitive classical picture of the dynamics of leading-twist
N
-body states in AdS. In section
2
we study the model (
6
) in detail and verify explicitly that the AdS two-body binding energies agree with the Lorentzian inversion formula. In section
3
we study the
N=3
case and develop a semiclassical picture by relating it to Berezin-Toeplitz quantization. In section
4
we discuss the general case
N\geq 3
and its semiclassical limit. We conclude in section
5
. Appendices contain various details of our calculations, conventions, and a brief discussion of pseudodifferential operators.

1.1
Some classical intuition

To gain some intuition about the dynamics of the lowest-twist states in AdS, consider the following heuristic argument. For simplicity, we first focus on an
\mathrm{AdS}_{3}
subspace by setting some angles to
0
, and generalize to
\mathrm{AdS}_{d+1}
later. In the global coordinates
(t,\rho,\varphi)
the metric takes the form

\displaystyle ds^{2}_{\mathrm{AdS}_{3}}=-\cosh^{2}\rho\,dt^{2}+d\rho^{2}+\sinh^{2}\rho\,d\varphi^{2}.

(7)

Here,
t\in(-\infty,+\infty)
is the global time,
\rho\in[0,\infty)
is the radial coordinate and
\varphi\in[0,2\pi)
is the angular coordinate. The conformal boundary is at
\rho=\infty
. Changing to the coordinates
(t,\rho,\varphi^{\prime})
where
\varphi^{\prime}=\varphi-t
we find

\displaystyle ds^{2}_{\mathrm{AdS}_{3}}=-dt^{2}+d\rho^{2}+\sinh^{2}\rho\,d\varphi^{\prime 2}+2\sinh^{2}\rho\,d\varphi^{\prime}dt.

(8)

The vector field
-i\partial_{t}
in the coordinates
(t,\rho,\varphi^{\prime})
corresponds to
-i(\partial_{t}+\partial_{\varphi})
in the original coordinates
(t,\rho,\varphi)
. Therefore, the Hamiltonian that generates
t
evolution in
(t,\rho,\phi)
coordinates is the twist
\tau
(see appendix
A
and section
2.2
below for a more detailed discussion of our conventions).

Intuitively, the smallest values of
\tau
should correspond to slowly moving particles, and so we can employ a non-relativistic approximation in the
(t,\rho,\varphi^{\prime})
coordinates. For a classical particle, we have the action

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=-m\int|ds|=-m\int dt\sqrt{1-\dot{\rho}^{2}-\sinh^{2}\rho\,\dot{\varphi}^{\prime 2}-2\sinh^{2}\rho\,\dot{\varphi}^{\prime}}

(9)

\displaystyle\approx\int dt\left(-m+\frac{m/4}{2}((2\dot{\rho})^{2}+\sinh^{2}\rho\,\dot{\varphi}^{\prime 2})+m\sinh^{2}\rho\,\dot{\varphi}^{\prime}\right),

(10)

where we have expanded to the second order in velocities. The piece quadratic in velocities can be interpreted as the non-relativistic kinetic term for a particle of mass
m_{\text{eff}}=m/4
in the hyperbolic disk
\mathbb{D}
parameterised by the polar coordinates
(\rho^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime})
, where
\rho^{\prime}=2\rho
and the metric is given by

\displaystyle ds^{2}_{\mathbb{D}}=d\rho^{\prime 2}+\sinh^{2}\rho^{\prime}d\varphi^{\prime 2}.

(11)

The term linear in derivatives can be interpreted as

\displaystyle m\int dt\dot{\varphi}^{\prime}\sinh^{2}\rho^{\prime}=\int A,

(12)

where
A=A_{\varphi^{\prime}}d\varphi^{\prime}=m\sinh^{2}\frac{\rho^{\prime}}{2}d\varphi^{\prime}
. This coincides with the action of a charge-one particle in the electromagnetic field with gauge potential
A
. The corresponding field strength is

\displaystyle F=dA=\frac{m}{2}\sinh\rho^{\prime}d\rho^{\prime}\wedge d\varphi^{\prime}=\frac{m}{2}{\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}}_{\mathbb{D}},

(13)

where
{\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}}_{\mathbb{D}}=\sinh\rho^{\prime}d\rho^{\prime}\wedge d\varphi^{\prime}
is the volume form on the hyperbolic disk. Thus, we find an effective magnetic field of constant magnitude
B_{\text{eff}}=m/2
.

If we formally quantize this particle, we expect to find Landau levels split by the cyclotron frequency
6
6

6

Recall that we are working in dimensionless units since we have set the
\mathrm{AdS}
radius
R
to
1
.

\omega_{c}=B_{\text{eff}}/m_{\text{eff}}=2
. This is valid at least as long as
B_{\text{eff}}
is large so that the particle is localized on scales smaller than hyperbolic curvature.
7
7

7

The exact spectrum of Landau levels in hyperbolic space is given by
n(n+B_{\text{eff}}-1)/m_{\text{eff}}

[
21
]
.
Therefore, this naive picture predicts the low-twist spectrum of one-particle states to be given by

\displaystyle\tau=2n+\text{const}.

(14)

This agrees with the expectation from a
d=2
boundary theory, where different descendants
\partial_{-}^{m}\partial_{+}^{n}{\mathcal{O}}(0)
of a (quasi-)primary operator
{\mathcal{O}}(0)
have twist
\tau=\tau_{\mathcal{O}}+2n
.
However, now we can interpret the lowest-twist states with
n=0
as corresponding to the lowest Landau level (LLL) in the hyperbolic disk
\mathbb{D}
.

Figure 5
:
Spiraling geodesics in AdS.

Two comments are in order. Firstly, the above discussion took place in
\mathrm{AdS}_{3}
. In
\mathrm{AdS}_{d+1}
there are additional transverse degrees of freedom. It turns out that for the leading twist states these degrees of freedom are not excited and the LLL picture is still valid. Secondly, the above discussion is rather heuristic, starting with a non-relativistic limit of a classical particle. Fortunately, as we will show in section
2
, the same LLL Hilbert (sub-)space can identified in the full quantum field theory of free scalars on
\mathrm{AdS}_{d+1}
. In particular, none of our calculations depend on the LLL interpretation, and we only use the LLL picture to motivate our discussion. With these comments in mind, let us discuss the implications of the LLL picture for the large-spin dynamics.

Recall that the LLL is infinitely-degenerate. Physically, different LLL states can be visualized as being localized at different points of the hyperbolic disk
\mathbb{D}
. It is useful to think about
\mathbb{D}
as being the effective phase space for the LLL particle. One then expects one state per
2\pi
phase space volume.

By construction, the Hamiltonian is a constant when restricted to LLL,

\displaystyle H=\tau=\tau_{0}.

(15)

Therefore, in the absence of interactions, the LLL particles are stationary in
(t,\rho,\varphi^{\prime})
coordinates. Using
\varphi=\varphi^{\prime}+t
, we see that these states can be visualized as spiraling geodesics in
\mathrm{AdS}_{3}
, see figure
5
.

Lowest-twist multi-particle states can be visualized as several LLL particles localized at different points on
\mathbb{D}
. We will see in section
2
that at least some
\mathrm{AdS}_{d+1}
interactions can be reduced in LLL to instantaneous pair potentials
U_{ij}
. When such potentials are added, the effective Hamiltonian becomes non-trivial,

\displaystyle\tau=\tau_{0}+\sum_{i<j}U_{ij}.

(16)

Interpreting this classically, Hamilton’s equations imply that the particles move with velocities proportional to derivatives of
U_{ij}
. This slow motion gives a small correction to eigenvalues of
\tau
, which is what we want to calculate.

2
\mathrm{AdS}_{d+1}
toy model

2.1
Summary of the model and first-order perturbation theory

The toy model in AdS
d+1
is a two-scalar field theory with cubic coupling. We denote the bulk scalar fields by
\Phi,\Sigma
and their boundary CFT operators by
\phi,\sigma
, such that the masses and the conformal dimensions are related by
m^{2}_{\Phi}=\Delta_{\phi}(\Delta_{\phi}-d)
,
m^{2}_{\Sigma}=\Delta_{\sigma}(\Delta_{\sigma}-d)
. To slightly simplify the discussion, we take
\Phi
to be a complex scalar charged under a
U(1)
symmetry. The cubic coupling is
\mathcal{L}_{\text{int}}=\lambda\Phi\overline{\Phi}\Sigma
, and the full action is given by

\displaystyle S=\int d^{d+1}x\sqrt{-g}\left(-\partial_{\mu}\Phi\partial^{\mu}\overline{\Phi}-m^{2}_{\Phi}\Phi\overline{\Phi}-\tfrac{1}{2}(\partial\Sigma)^{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}m_{\Sigma}^{2}\Sigma^{2}+\lambda\Phi\overline{\Phi}\Sigma\right)

(17)

To study the multi-particle states of
\Phi
, it is convenient to integrate
\Sigma
out to produce a quartic potential in
\Phi
:

e^{iS_{\text{int}}[\Phi]}=\int D\Sigma\,e^{i\int d^{d+1}x\sqrt{-g}\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}(\partial\Sigma)^{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}m_{\Sigma}^{2}\Sigma^{2}+\lambda\Phi\overline{\Phi}\Sigma\right)}

(18)

The result is

\displaystyle S_{\text{int}}[\Phi]

\displaystyle=\int dt\,V(t),

(19)

\displaystyle V(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\int d^{d+1}x_{1}\sqrt{-g}d^{d+1}x_{2}\sqrt{-g}K_{\Sigma}(x_{1},x_{2}):\mathrel{\Phi\overline{\Phi}(x_{1})\Phi\overline{\Phi}(x_{2})}:\delta(t_{1}-t),

(20)

where
K_{\Sigma}
is the propagator for
\Sigma
.
To the leading order in
\lambda
, the interaction energies can be obtained from the Rayleigh-Schrödinger perturbation theory for
V
in the Hilbert space of the free theory, see
[
22
,
20
]
8
8

8

Note that
V(t)
is only time-dependent through the Heisenberg evolution, it has no “explicit” time-dependence. To stress this, we will write
V
instead of
V(t)
in what follows.
. In the rest of this section we will first describe the free Hilbert space and then compute the matrix elements of
V
.

Note that the above description is of our toy model, in which all approximations are easily controlled from first principles. However, as discussed in more detail in the introduction, we believe that the main results of this work apply more generally. In particular, we expect that neither the assumption of
\lambda
being small, nor the restriction to simple
\Sigma
-exchange interactions are essential for describing the leading-twist states at large spin.

2.2
Symmetries

To simplify the forthcoming calculations, it helps to carefully consider the symmetries of the problem. The
\mathrm{AdS}_{d+1}
isometries coincide with the conformal symmetries of the boundary and are generated in Euclidean signature by the standard generators
D,P_{\mu},K_{\nu},M_{\mu\nu}
, with indices running over
\{1,\cdots,d\}
. These operators satisfy the hermiticity conditions
D^{\dagger}=D,P_{\mu}^{\dagger}=K_{\mu},M_{\mu\nu}^{\dagger}=-M_{\mu\nu}
. When acting on the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
of states on the unit sphere in the boundary
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, these generators have the familiar spectrum given by the operator-state correspondence. It is possible to choose coordinates (see appendix
A
) so that this unit sphere becomes the
t=0
spatial slice of the Lorentzian cylinder
\mathbb{R}\times S^{d-1}
, where
t
is the global time. Therefore,
\mathcal{H}
coincides with the Hilbert space of the AdS theory. Appropriate complex linear combinations of the above generators then span the isometries of the Lorentzian
\mathrm{AdS}_{d,1}
.

The
\mathrm{AdS}_{d,1}
metric in global coordinates is

\displaystyle ds^{2}_{\mathrm{AdS}_{d,1}}=-\cosh^{2}\rho dt^{2}+d\rho^{2}+\sinh^{2}\rho\,ds_{S^{d-1}}^{2},

(21)

where
ds^{2}_{S^{d-1}}
is the radius-1 round metric on the sphere
S^{d-1}
parameterized by a unit vector
n\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Using the conventions in appendix
A
, it is easy to check that the action of
D
on bulk operators is

\displaystyle[D,{\mathcal{O}}(x)]=-i\partial_{t}{\mathcal{O}}(x),

(22)

and thus
D
becomes the Hamiltonian for
t
translations.

We choose a preferred rotation generator
M_{12}
and define the twist generator as

\displaystyle\tau=D+iM_{12}.

(23)

We also introduce the following coordinates on
S^{d-1}
:

\displaystyle n^{1}=\cos\theta\cos\varphi,\quad n^{2}=\cos\theta\sin\varphi,\quad n^{i}=\sin\theta\,\widehat{n}^{i}\quad(i=3,\cdots d),

(24)

where
\theta\in[0,\pi/2]
,
\varphi\in[0,2\pi)
and
\widehat{n}\in S^{d-3}
. In these coordinates, the twist generator acts on local fields as

\displaystyle[\tau,{\mathcal{O}}(x)]=-i(\partial_{t}+\partial_{\varphi}){\mathcal{O}}(x).

(25)

Therefore, if we define
\varphi^{\prime}=\varphi-t
, then in the coordinates
(t,\rho,\varphi^{\prime},\theta,\widehat{n})
the Hamiltonian for
t
translations coincides with
\tau
.

Under the adjoint action of
\tau
, the conformal algebra splits into eigenspaces with eigenvalues
0
and
\pm 1
. To describe these eigenspaces, it is convenient to introduce the components
v^{\pm}
of a vector
v^{\mu}
, defined as
v^{\pm}=-iv^{1}\mp v^{2}
. The
\pm
component has charge
\pm 1
under
iM_{12}
, which implies

\displaystyle[\tau,P^{-}]=[\tau,K^{+}]=[\tau,\overline{\tau}]=[\tau,M^{ij}]=0,

\displaystyle[\tau,M^{\pm i}]=\pm M^{\pm i},\quad[\tau,P^{+}]=2P^{+},\quad[\tau,K^{-}]=-2K^{-},

\displaystyle[\tau,P^{i}]=P^{i},\quad[\tau,K^{i}]=-K^{i}.

(26)

where
i,j\geq 3
and
\overline{\tau}=D-iM_{12}
. The generators commuting with
\tau
generate a
\mathfrak{so}(2,1)\times\mathfrak{so}(d-2)
subalgebra (not counting
\tau
itself).

2.3
Minimal twist Hilbert space

Our first goal is to describe the Hilbert space of the free theory of the field
\Phi
. For simplicity, in this paper we focus on the lowest-twist subspace of a given
U(1)
charge. The free-theory Hilbert space splits into multi-particle sectors, which can be understood as properly symmetrized tensor products of the single-particle subspace. The single-particle subspace (say, containing a single
\Phi
particle) forms a (generically) irreducible Verma module of the conformal algebra, which we denote by
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\text{full}}
. The primary of this Verma module is a scalar operator of dimension
\Delta_{\phi}
.

A useful characterization of a one-particle state
|\Psi\rangle
is given by its wave-function
\Psi(x)=\langle 0|\overline{\Phi}(x)|\Psi\rangle
. The lowest-twist states have to be annihilated by all negative-twist generators, see (
26
),

\displaystyle K^{-}|\Psi\rangle=K^{i}|\Psi\rangle=M^{-i}|\Psi\rangle=0,

(27)

where
i\geq 3
. This implies that the wave-function
\Psi(x)
satisfies a set of differential equations

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{-}\Psi(x)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{K}^{i}\Psi(x)=0,

(28)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{-i}\Psi(x)

\displaystyle=0,

(29)

where
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{M}
denote the vector fields associated to the respective generators. It turns out that the constraint
\mathcal{M}^{-i}\Psi(x)=0
is redundant and follows from (
28
).

The solution is conveniently written in terms of

\displaystyle\alpha=e^{i\varphi^{\prime}}\cos\theta\tanh\rho,

(30)

which is
t
-independent and also satisfies
\mathcal{K}^{-}\alpha=\mathcal{K}^{i}\alpha=0
. In fact, any
t
-independent function which satisfies (
28
) is a function of
\alpha
. Indeed, the vector fields
\mathcal{K}^{-}
,
\mathcal{K}^{i}
, and
\partial_{t}
are linearly-independent and there are
d=\dim\mathrm{AdS}_{d+1}-1
of them. Note that by construction
\alpha
takes values in the unit disk,
|\alpha|<1
.

We know that the lowest twist states in the one-particle Verma module satisfy
\tau|\Psi\rangle=\Delta_{\phi}|\Psi\rangle
, which implies

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\Psi(x)=-i\Delta_{\phi}\Psi(x)

(31)

It is now not hard to find the general solution of (
28
) and (
31
), which we express as

\displaystyle\Psi(x)=C_{\Delta_{\phi},d}^{1/2}\frac{e^{-i\Delta_{\phi}t}}{(\cosh\rho)^{\Delta_{\phi}}}\psi(\alpha),\quad C_{\Delta,d}:=\frac{2\pi^{d/2}\Gamma(\Delta)}{\Gamma(\Delta-d/2+1)},

(32)

where
\psi(\alpha)
is an arbitrary holomorphic function of
\alpha
, and
C_{\Delta_{\phi},d}^{1/2}
is multiplicative constant that we factor out for future convenience. We can therefore identify the states in the lowest-twist subspace
\mathcal{H}_{1}
of
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\text{full}}
with the corresponding wavefunctions
\psi(\alpha)
.

The minimal twist subspace
\mathcal{H}_{1}
naturally forms a representation of the twist-0 subalgebra
\mathfrak{so}(2,1)\times\mathfrak{so}(d-2)
. The quantum number of
\mathfrak{so}(2,1)
is the conformal spin
\overline{h}=\overline{\tau}/2
, while the
\mathfrak{so}(d-2)
representation labels are transverse spins. Since the generators
M^{ij}
spanning
\mathfrak{so}(d-2)
act trivially on
\psi(\alpha)
, the latter form representations with zero transverse spin. The action of
\mathfrak{so}(2,1)
is conveniently expressed in terms of the generators

\displaystyle L_{0}=\tfrac{1}{2}\overline{\tau},\quad L_{+}=\tfrac{i}{2}K^{+},\quad L_{-}=\tfrac{i}{2}P^{-},

(33)

which have the standard commutation relations
[L_{m},L_{n}]=(m-n)L_{m+n}
. Acting on
\psi
, we have

\displaystyle(L_{0}\psi)(\alpha)

\displaystyle=(\alpha\partial_{\alpha}+\Delta_{\phi}/2)\psi(\alpha),

\displaystyle(L_{+}\psi)(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\partial_{\alpha}\psi(\alpha),

\displaystyle(L_{-}\psi)(\alpha)

\displaystyle=(\alpha^{2}\partial_{\alpha}+\Delta_{\phi}\alpha)\psi(\alpha).

(34)

As expected, this defines a lowest-weight representation of
\mathfrak{so}(2,1)
with
\overline{h}=\Delta_{\phi}/2
, where the lowest weight state satisfies

\displaystyle L_{+}\psi=0,\quad L_{0}\psi=\overline{h}\psi,

(35)

and is given by
\psi(\alpha)\equiv 1
. The states with definite
L_{0}
weights
\overline{h}+n
are simply the monomials
\psi(\alpha)=\alpha^{n}
, where
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

The Hermiticity conditions
L_{n}^{\dagger}=L_{-n}
fix the inner product in
\mathcal{H}_{1}
up to normalization. In terms of
\psi(\alpha)
it is given by

\displaystyle\langle\psi_{1}|\psi_{2}\rangle:=\frac{\Delta_{\phi}-1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{D}}d^{2}\alpha(1-\alpha\overline{\alpha})^{2\Delta_{\phi}-2}\,\overline{\psi_{1}(\alpha)}\psi_{2}(\alpha),

(36)

where
\mathbb{D}
is the unit disk
|\alpha|<1
, and the multiplicative constant
(\Delta_{\phi}-1)/\pi
is chosen such that the lowest-weight state
\psi(\alpha)=1
has unit norm. At the same, the multiplicative constant
C_{\Delta_{\phi},d}^{1/2}
entering the relation between
\Psi(x)
and
\psi(\alpha)
in (
32
) ensures that the scalar product (
36
) descends exactly from the canonical scalar product of the AdS field:
\langle\Psi_{1}|\Psi_{2}\rangle=\langle\psi_{1}|\psi_{2}\rangle
.

Note that although the wavefunction
\psi(\alpha)
is parameterized by
\alpha
in the unit disk
\mathbb{D}
, and there is an action of
\mathfrak{so}(2,1)
on
\mathbb{D}
, there isn’t a natural embedding of
\mathbb{D}
into
\mathrm{AdS}_{d,1}
that respects this action. For example,
L_{0}=\tfrac{1}{2}\overline{\tau}
necessarily generates translations along the non-compact time direction. Relatedly, even though the anti-Hermitian combinations of
L_{n}
act by hyperbolic isometries on
\mathbb{D}
, on the Hilbert space the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{so}(2,1)
exponentiates to the universal cover
\widetilde{\mathrm{SO}}(2,1)\subseteq\widetilde{\mathrm{SO}}(2,d)
, rather than the hyperbolic isometry group
\mathrm{SO}(2,1)
of
\mathbb{D}
.

We now consider multi-particle states, which are obtained through the usual Fock space construction as symmetrized tensor products of the single-particle states,
\mathcal{H}_{N}=(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\otimes N})^{S_{N}}
. For the leading-twist states we find

\displaystyle\Psi(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N})=\psi(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n})\prod_{i=1}^{N}C_{\Delta_{\phi},d}^{1/2}\frac{e^{-i\Delta_{\phi}t_{i}}}{(\cosh\rho_{i})^{\Delta_{\phi}}},

(37)

where the wavefunction
\Psi
is defined as

\displaystyle\Psi(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N})=\langle 0|\overline{\Phi}(x_{1})\cdots\overline{\Phi}(x_{N})|\Psi\rangle.

(38)

Bose symmetry implies that
\psi(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n})
is symmetric in its arguments.

Defining the spin
J
as
J=-iM_{12}=\tfrac{\overline{\tau}-\tau}{2}
, we find that it acts on
\psi(\alpha)
by

\displaystyle(J\psi)(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{N})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}\partial_{\alpha_{i}}\psi(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{N}).

(39)

In other words, states with definite spin
J
are homogeneous polynomials in
\alpha_{i}
of total degree
J
. It can be verified that such
\psi
are the highest-weight vectors in the traceless-symmetric spin-
J
representation of
\mathrm{SO}(d)
. We will use
\mathcal{H}_{N,J}\subset\mathcal{H}_{N}
to denote the Hilbert space of lowest-twist states at spin
J
.

In terms of
\psi
, the inner product on the
N
-particle states becomes simply

\displaystyle\langle\psi_{1}|\psi_{2}\rangle=\frac{(\Delta_{\phi}-1)^{N}}{\pi^{N}N!}\int_{\mathbb{D}}d^{2N}\alpha\prod_{k=1}^{N}(1-\alpha_{k}\overline{\alpha}_{k})^{\Delta_{\phi}-2}\,\overline{\psi_{1}(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n})}\psi_{2}(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}).

(40)

Primary states

Due to (
27
), the lowest-twist states are primary if and only if they are annihilated by
K^{+}\propto L_{+}
. This happens precisely when

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\partial_{\alpha_{i}}\psi(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{N})=0,

(41)

see (
34
). We therefore find that the lowest-twist traceless-symmetric spin-
J
primaries in the
N
-particle Hilbert space are in one-to-one correspondence with wavefunctions
\psi(\alpha_{1},\cdots\alpha_{N})
that are symmetric, homogeneous of degree
J
, and translation-invariant polynomials in the
\alpha_{i}
. We will denote the vector space of such wavefunctions by
\mathcal{H}_{N,J}^{\text{primary}}\subset\mathcal{H}_{N,J}
.

For example, the unique lowest-twist primary in the 1-particle Hilbert space is given by
\psi(\alpha)=1
and has spin-
0
. The two-particle Hilbert space has a unique primary state at every even spin
J
, given by

\displaystyle\psi(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})^{J}.

(42)

To enumerate the
N
-particle primary states, we define the translation-invariant combinations

\displaystyle\beta_{i}=\alpha_{i}-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\alpha_{k}.

(43)

A basis of states at spin
J
is then given by the wavefunctions

\displaystyle\psi(\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{N})=f_{m}(\alpha):=e_{2}(\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{N})^{m_{2}}\cdots e_{N}(\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{N})^{m_{N}},

(44)

where the non-negative integers
m_{k}
satisfy
\sum_{k=2}^{N}km_{k}=J
and
e_{n}
are the elementary symmetric polynomials. Note that
e_{1}(\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{n})=\sum_{i}\beta_{i}=0
and thus does not appear.

The number of lowest-twist states at spin
J
is therefore equal to the number of partitions of
J
into integers from
2
to
N
. We have the generating function

\displaystyle\sum_{J=0}^{\infty}\left(\dim\mathcal{H}_{N,J}^{\text{primary}}\right)x^{J}=\prod_{k=2}^{N}(1-x^{k})^{-1}.

(45)

Explicit expressions for a given
N
can be obtained by computing

\displaystyle\dim\mathcal{H}_{N,J}^{\text{primary}}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|x|=\frac{1}{2}}dxx^{-J-1}\prod_{k=2}^{N}(1-x^{k})^{-1}

(46)

via the residue theorem as the integration contour is deformed to infinity. It is not hard to check that the leading term at large
J
comes from the residue at
x=1
and gives

\displaystyle\dim\mathcal{H}_{N,J}^{\text{primary}}=\frac{J^{N-2}}{N!(N-2)!}+O(J^{N-3}).

(47)

In particular, for
N=3
we find

\displaystyle\dim\mathcal{H}_{3,J}^{\text{primary}}=J/6+O(1),

(48)

where the
O(1)
term only depends on
J\!\!\mod 6
.

2.4
Effective pair potential

We now consider the problem of finding the leading correction from the potential (
20
) to the energies of the leading-twist states with
N

\Phi
-particles. If we wanted to compute the correction to the energy of a state
|\Psi\rangle
that is non-degenerate in the free theory, it would be as simple as computing the expectation value
\langle\Psi|V|\Psi\rangle
. However, as discussed above, the energies of
N
-particle states are highly degenerate at large spin
J
. Therefore, we need to use the degenerate perturbation theory, which instructs us to diagonalize the restriction of
V
to the lowest-twist degenerate
N
-particle subspace
\mathcal{H}_{N,J}
, or more generally to
\mathcal{H}_{N}
.

The simplest way to characterize this restriction is via the matrix elements

\displaystyle\langle\Psi_{1}|V|\Psi_{2}\rangle,\quad\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2}\in\mathcal{H}_{N}.

(49)

Due to our choice of
V
, given by (
20
), the only non-trivial calculation to do is in the case
N=2
. We will find that in terms of wavefunctions
\psi
the matrix elements are given by (c.f. (
40
))

\displaystyle\langle\Psi_{1}|V|\Psi_{2}\rangle=\frac{(\Delta_{\phi}-1)^{2}}{\pi^{2}2!}\int_{\mathbb{D}}d^{4}\alpha\prod_{k=1}^{2}(1-\alpha_{k}\overline{\alpha}_{k})^{\Delta_{\phi}-2}\overline{\psi_{1}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})}\psi_{2}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})U_{2}(s_{12}),

(50)

where

\displaystyle\quad s_{12}:=\sinh^{2}\tfrac{\mathbf{d}_{12}}{2}=\frac{(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})(\overline{\alpha}_{1}-\overline{\alpha}_{2})}{(1-\alpha_{1}\overline{\alpha}_{1})(1-\alpha_{2}\overline{\alpha}_{2})}\in[0,\infty),

(51)

\mathbf{d}_{12}
is the hyperbolic distance between
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
in
\mathbb{D}
, and the function
U_{2}(s)
is given in (
62
) below. Note that when
\mathbf{d}_{12}
is large, it is double the (extremal) geodesic distance between the codimension-two surfaces of constant
\alpha
in
\mathrm{AdS}_{d,1}
.

In the case of general
N
, the matrix elements are given simply by the sum over the pairwise interactions,

\displaystyle\langle\Psi_{1}|V|\Psi_{2}\rangle=\frac{(\Delta_{\phi}-1)^{N}}{\pi^{N}N!}\int_{\mathbb{D}}d^{2N}\alpha\prod_{k=1}^{N}(1-\alpha_{k}\overline{\alpha}_{k})^{\Delta_{\phi}-2}\overline{\psi_{1}(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n})}\psi_{2}(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n})\sum_{i<j}U_{2}(s_{ij}).

(52)

To derive the form of
U_{2}(s)
, we begin with the explicit expression for the matrix element that follows from the definitions (
20
) of
V
and (
38
) of the wave-functions
\Psi(x)
,

\displaystyle\langle\Psi_{1}|V|\Psi_{2}\rangle=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\int d^{d+1}x_{1}\sqrt{-g}d^{d+1}x_{2}\sqrt{-g}K_{\Sigma}(x_{1},x_{2})\delta(t_{1})\Psi_{2}(x_{1},x_{2})\overline{\Psi_{1}(x_{1},x_{2})}.

(53)

As the matrix elements are
t
-independent, we set
t=0
. Plugging in the lowest-twist wavefunctions (
37
), we get

\displaystyle\langle\Psi_{1}|V|\Psi_{2}\rangle=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\int d^{d+1}x_{1}\sqrt{-g}d^{d+1}x_{2}\sqrt{-g}\frac{C_{\Delta_{\phi},d}^{2}K_{\Sigma}(x_{1},x_{2})\delta(t_{1})}{(\cosh\rho_{1})^{2\Delta_{\phi}}(\cosh\rho_{2})^{2\Delta_{\phi}}}\psi_{2}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\overline{\psi_{1}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})}.

(54)

We therefore find

\displaystyle\langle\Psi_{1}|V|\Psi_{2}\rangle=\frac{(\Delta_{\phi}-1)^{2}}{\pi^{2}2!}\int\prod_{k=1}^{2}d^{2}\alpha_{k}(1-\alpha_{k}\overline{\alpha}_{k})^{\Delta_{\phi}-2}\psi_{2}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\overline{\psi_{1}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})}F(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}),

(55)

where the function
F
is defined by

\displaystyle F(\alpha_{0,1},\alpha_{0,2})\equiv\frac{\pi^{2}\lambda^{2}C_{\Delta_{\phi},d}^{2}}{(\Delta_{\phi}-1)^{2}}\int\prod_{k=1}^{2}\frac{d^{d+1}x_{k}\sqrt{-g}\,\delta^{2}(\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{0,k})}{(1-\alpha_{k}\overline{\alpha}_{k})^{\Delta_{\phi}-2}(\cosh\rho_{k})^{2\Delta_{\phi}}}K_{\Sigma}(x_{1},x_{2})\delta(t_{1}).

(56)

The key point is that the function
F
is
\mathfrak{so}(2,1)
-invariant, where
\mathfrak{so}(2,1)
acts on
\alpha
’s by hyperbolic isometries of
\mathbb{D}
. Indeed, the factor

\displaystyle\frac{\delta^{2}(\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{0,k})}{(\cosh\rho_{k})^{2\Delta_{\phi}}(1-\alpha_{k}\overline{\alpha}_{k})^{\Delta_{\phi}-2}}

(57)

can be verified to be
\mathfrak{so}(2,1)
-invariant by an explicit calculation, and
K_{\Sigma}(x_{1},x_{2})
is invariant under the full
\widetilde{\mathrm{SO}}(d,2)
. The only suspect factor is
\delta(t_{1})
, which is not invariant. For example, if we compute the variation of (
56
) under
L_{0}
, it is going to be proportional to

\displaystyle-i\frac{\pi^{2}\lambda^{2}C_{\Delta_{\phi},d}^{2}}{2(\Delta_{\phi}-1)^{2}}\int\prod_{k=1}^{2}\frac{d^{d+1}x_{k}\sqrt{-g}\,\delta^{2}(\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{0,k})}{(1-\alpha_{k}\overline{\alpha}_{k})^{\Delta_{\phi}-2}(\cosh\rho_{k})^{2\Delta_{\phi}}}K_{\Sigma}(x_{1},x_{2})\delta^{\prime}(t_{1}).

(58)

Note that
\delta(t_{1})
in (
56
) has been replaced by
\mathcal{L}_{0}\delta(t_{1})=-i\delta^{\prime}(t_{1})/2
, where
\mathcal{L}_{0}
is the Killing vector corresponding to
L_{0}
. We can compute this integral in
(t,\rho,\phi,\psi,\widehat{n})
coordinates. The integral over the times
t_{k}
takes the form

\displaystyle\int dt_{1}dt_{2}f(t_{2}-t_{1})\delta^{\prime}(t_{1})=\int dt_{2}f^{\prime}(t_{2})=0.

(59)

where the function
f
of
t_{2}-t_{1}
comes from
K_{\Sigma}(x_{1},x_{2})
, while all the other factors in (
58
) are
t_{k}
-independent. Therefore, the
L_{0}
variation (
58
) vanishes. The same is true for
L_{+}
and
L_{-}
variations due to
\mathcal{L}_{+}\delta(t_{1})=-i\overline{\alpha}_{1}\delta^{\prime}(t_{1})/2
and
\mathcal{L}_{-}\delta(t_{1})=-i\alpha_{1}\delta^{\prime}(t_{1})/2
. We conclude that
F(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=U_{2}(s_{12})
for some function
U_{2}
.
9
9

9

We have essentially shown that the matrix elements of
V
on
\mathcal{H}_{2,J}
are invariant under the
\mathfrak{so}(2,1)
transformations of the free theory. The computation was slightly non-trivial since, much like
\tau
, the
\mathfrak{so}(2,1)
generators receive corrections. Let’s say
Q
is one of the free theory generators. Then we have
[Q,\tau]=[Q+\delta Q,\tau+V]=0
, where
\delta Q
is the correction to
Q
. At the leading order, this implies only that
[Q,V]=[\tau,\delta Q]
. Thus, the operator
V
is not invariant under
Q
, but
\langle\Psi_{1}|[Q,V]|\Psi_{2}\rangle=\langle\Psi_{1}|[\tau,\delta Q]|\Psi_{2}\rangle=0
if
\Psi_{1}
and
\Psi_{2}
are
\tau
-eigenstates with the same eigenvalue.

It is thus enough to compute
F(\alpha_{1},0)
. For this, we rewrite
F
as

\displaystyle F(\alpha_{0,1},0)=\frac{\pi^{2}\lambda^{2}C_{\Delta_{\phi},d}^{2}}{(\Delta_{\phi}-1)^{2}}\int\frac{d^{d+1}x_{1}\sqrt{-g}\,\delta^{2}(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{0,1})}{(1-\alpha_{1}\overline{\alpha}_{1})^{\Delta_{\phi}-2}(\cosh\rho_{1})^{2\Delta_{\phi}}}\delta(t_{1})\,I(x_{1}),

(60)

where

\displaystyle I(x_{1})=\int d^{d+1}x_{2}\sqrt{-g}K_{\Sigma}(x_{1},x_{2})\frac{\delta^{2}(\alpha_{2})}{(\cosh\rho_{2})^{2\Delta_{\phi}}}.

(61)

Since
K_{\Sigma}
is the Green’s function of the Klein-Gordon equation,
I(x_{1})
is the solution with the source
{\delta^{2}(\alpha)/}{(\cosh\rho)^{2\Delta_{\phi}}}
. Therefore, instead of computing the above integral, we can simply solve the Klein-Gordon equation directly. This can be done using separation of variables and is detailed in appendix
B.1
. Substituting the resulting solution (
316
) into (
60
) yields (see appendix
B.3
)

\displaystyle U_{2}(s)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}b_{n}\,k_{\Delta_{\sigma}+2n}\left(\frac{1}{s+1}\right),

(62)

where

\displaystyle k_{2h}(z)

\displaystyle=z^{h}{}_{2}F_{1}(h,h;2h;z),

(63)

\displaystyle b_{0}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{16\pi^{d/2}}\frac{\Gamma(\Delta_{\sigma}/2)^{2}}{\Gamma(\Delta_{\sigma}-d/2+1)}\frac{\Gamma(\Delta_{\phi}+\Delta_{\sigma}/2-d/2)^{2}}{\Gamma(\Delta_{\phi}+\Delta_{\sigma}/2-1)^{2}}\frac{\Gamma(\Delta_{\phi}-1)^{2}}{\Gamma(\Delta_{\phi}-d/2+1)^{2}},

(64)

\displaystyle b_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{b_{0}}{n!}\frac{(d/2-1)_{n}}{(\Delta_{\sigma}-d/2+1)_{n}}\frac{(\Delta_{\sigma}/2-\Delta_{\phi}+1)_{n}^{2}}{(\Delta_{\sigma}/2+\Delta_{\phi}-1)_{n}^{2}}\frac{(\Delta_{\sigma}-1)_{n}}{(\Delta_{\sigma})_{n}}\frac{\Delta_{\sigma}-1+2n}{\Delta_{\sigma}-1}(\Delta_{\sigma}/2)_{n}.

(65)

Note that the expansion (
62
) is effectively an expansion at large
s
since each
k_{2h}(1/(s+1))
is suppressed by
s^{-h}
. For example, the leading behaviour at large
s
is

\displaystyle U_{2}(s)=b_{0}s^{-\Delta_{\sigma}/2}(1+O(s^{-1})).

(66)

When
J
is large, the typical separation
s
between the two particles will be large as well. We will therefore often only need the leading term (
66
).

2.5
Decay of two-particle and
N
-particle potentials

The result (
66
) shows exponential decay of
U_{2}(s)
in the hyperbolic distance
\mathbf{d}\sim\log s
. While ultimately this decay should be due to the large-distance asymptotic of the bulk-to-bulk propagator
K_{\Sigma}
in (
56
), the details of this are not immediately obvious due to the Lorentzian region over which
x_{1},x_{2}
are integrated, which includes null and time-like separations.

A simple way to resolve such difficulties would be a Wick rotation to Euclidean
\mathrm{AdS}_{d+1}
. However, this seems impossible due to the presence of the delta-functions
\delta^{2}(\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{0,k})
in (
56
) which are non-analytic in the global
\mathrm{AdS}_{d,1}
time. Fortunately, the definition (
30
) of
\alpha
shows that these delta-functions are time-independent and do not obstruct the Wick rotation if we work in the coordinates
(t,\rho,\varphi^{\prime},\cdots)
. The Wick rotation in these coordinates is possible since the generator of time translations is the twist
\tau
, which is non-negative definite.

It is interesting to note that since the metric in
(t,\rho,\varphi^{\prime},\cdots)
coordinates contains an off-diagonal term proportional to
dtd\varphi^{\prime}
(see (
8
)), it becomes complex after the Wick rotation. It is easy to check that the resulting complex metric is allowable in the sense of
[
23
]
.
10
10

10

This is true more generally: if, in some coordinate system, translations in time are isometries of a real Lorentzian metric (and are, of course, time-like), then the Wick rotation in this time gives an allowable complex metric
[
24
]
.

At a more practical level, this Wick rotation can be viewed as a contour deformation in (
56
) after which we are integrating over
t_{E}=it
; non-negativity of the twist
\tau
ensures that
K_{\Sigma}
does not have singularities that would prevent this deformation. The purpose of doing this is that we hope that the asymptotics of the integral are easier to determine with the new integration contour.

To see that this is indeed the case, we first note that
K_{\Sigma}(x_{1},x_{2})
decays at large
\zeta
as
\zeta^{-\Delta_{\sigma}}
, where

\displaystyle\zeta=

\displaystyle-1+\cos t_{12}\cosh\rho_{1}\cosh\rho_{2}

\displaystyle+\left(\cos\theta_{1}\cos\theta_{2}\cos(t_{12}+\varphi^{\prime}_{12})+\sin\theta_{1}\sin\theta_{2}\widehat{n}_{1}\cdot\widehat{n}_{2}\right)\sinh\rho_{1}\sinh\rho_{2},

(67)

with
t_{12}=t_{1}-t_{2}
,
\varphi^{\prime}_{12}=\varphi^{\prime}_{1}-\varphi^{\prime}_{2}
, and
\zeta
is related to the
\mathrm{AdS}_{d,1}
geodesic distance
\rho_{12}
by
\zeta=2\sinh^{2}\rho_{12}/2
. Recall also that having a large hyperbolic distance between
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
requires at least one of
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
approach the boundary of the unit disk.

Suppose that at least
\alpha_{1}=e^{i\varphi_{1}}\cos\theta_{1}\tanh\rho_{1}
is pushed to the boundary of the unit disk. This forces
\rho_{1}
to be large and
\theta_{1}
to be small, yielding

\displaystyle\zeta\approx\frac{e^{\rho_{1}}}{2}\left(\cos t_{12}\cosh\rho_{2}-\cos\theta_{2}\cos(t_{12}+\varphi^{\prime}_{12})\sinh\rho_{2}\right).

(68)

Prior to the Wick rotation, this expression can oscillate in sign depending on
t_{12}
, and therefore is not guaranteed to be large.

On the other hand, after the Wick rotation this becomes

\displaystyle\zeta\approx\frac{e^{\rho_{1}}\cosh t_{E,12}\cosh\rho_{2}}{2}\left(1-|\alpha_{2}|(\cos\varphi^{\prime}_{12}+i\sin\varphi^{\prime}_{12}\tanh t_{E,12})\right),

(69)

which can only become small if
\varphi^{\prime}_{2}\to\varphi^{\prime}_{1}
, and
\alpha_{2}
is pushed to the boundary of the unit disk as well. Since
F(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
is invariant under
\mathfrak{so}(2,1)
transformations of
\alpha
’s, we can compute it with
\alpha_{1}=-\alpha_{2}
, and then this condition is never satisfied in the integral (
56
). Therefore, large hyperbolic distance between
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
guarantees that
\zeta
is large for all points on the integration contour. This then guarantees the decay of
F
and thus
U
at large hyperbolic distances.

To see the rate of the decay, note for
\alpha_{2}=-\alpha_{1}
we have

\displaystyle\zeta\propto e^{\rho_{1}}e^{\rho_{2}}\propto(1-|\alpha_{1}|^{2})^{-1/2}(1-|\alpha_{2}|^{2})^{-1/2}\propto s^{1/2}.

(70)

The decay
K_{\Sigma}\sim\zeta^{-\Delta_{\sigma}}
then leads to
F(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=U_{2}(s)\sim s^{-\Delta_{\sigma}/2}
.

Higher-particle interactions

This logic allows us to estimate the decay of effective potentials corresponding to higher-particle interactions. Let us first discuss briefly the role of higher-particle interactions in our model.

\Phi
\Phi
\Sigma
\Phi
\Phi
\Phi

Figure 6
:
Some diagrams contributing to the effective potential between
\Phi
particles. Left: the leading contribution to the two-particle potential. Right: a contribution to the three-particle potential.

The potential
U_{2}(s)
that we computed is basically due to the leading
\Sigma
-exchange diagram shown in left panel of figure
6
. More complicated diagrams such as that in the right of figure
6
would contribute to potentials which involve
n\geq 3

\Phi
-particles at once. Their projections onto the lowest twist subspace can be characterized by potentials

\displaystyle U_{n}(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}).

(71)

We would like to understand how quickly
U_{n}
decays in typical multi-particle states at large spin
J
.

A subtlety is that in our model such potentials appear only at higher orders in the perturbation theory, in which case it is no longer justified to project the interactions to the lowest-twist subspace, and mixing effects with higher twists must be taken into account. However, we expect that taking the projection is still valid at large spin, when the interactions are weak due to large
\mathrm{AdS}
separations rather than weak coupling.

Returning to the decay of the potentials
U_{n}
, we expect that at least at the leading orders they can be computed by generalizations of (
56
),

\displaystyle U(\alpha_{0,1}\cdots\alpha_{0,n})\propto\int\prod_{k=1}^{n}\frac{d^{d+1}x_{k}\sqrt{-g}\,\delta^{2}(\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{0,k})K_{\Sigma}(x_{k},y_{k})}{(1-\alpha_{k}\overline{\alpha}_{k})^{\Delta_{\phi}-2}(\cosh\rho_{k})^{2\Delta_{\phi}}}\Gamma(y_{1}\cdots y_{n})\delta(t_{1}),

(72)

where
\Gamma(y_{1},\cdots,y_{n})
is some vertex function supported (after the Wick rotation) when all
y_{k}
are close together on the order of
\mathrm{AdS}
scale.

We will see in the following sections that the typical configurations on which
N
-particle wavefunctions have support satisfy
1-|\alpha_{i}|^{2}\sim J^{-1}
, with the phases of
\alpha_{i}
relatively evenly distributed over the unit circle. Following the logic above, this gives

\displaystyle\zeta_{k}\propto e^{\rho_{k}}\propto(1-|\alpha_{k}|^{2})^{-1/2}\propto J^{1/2}

(73)

for the arguments of the
K_{\Sigma}
, leading to the decay

\displaystyle U_{n}\sim J^{-n\Delta_{\sigma}/2}.

(74)

In particular, all higher-particle potentials
U_{n\geq 3}
are suppressed at large
J
by at least
J^{-\Delta_{\sigma}/2}
relative to the contribution of
U_{2}(s)
. One could imagine constructing a model where the exchanges contributing to
U_{2}
would be restricted compared to those contributing to
U_{3}
, leading to
U_{3}
dominating over
U_{2}
. We haven’t been able to devise a simple and natural (i.e. where all interactions allowed by symmetries exist) model of this kind, but we did not explore this question systematically.

2.6
Toeplitz operators

In section
2.4
we derived the expression (
52
) for matrix elements
\langle\Psi_{1}|V|\Psi_{2}\rangle
, together with the expression (
62
) for the effective pair potential
U_{2}(s)
. These matrix elements define some operator
V_{\text{eff}}:\mathcal{H}_{N}\to\mathcal{H}_{N}
on the lowest-twist Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{N}
. Formally, it can be described as

\displaystyle V_{\text{eff}}

\displaystyle=P_{\mathcal{H}_{N}}V|_{\mathcal{H}_{N}},

\displaystyle\langle\Psi_{1}|V_{\text{eff}}|\Psi_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\Psi_{1}|V|\Psi_{2}\rangle,\quad\forall\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2}\in\mathcal{H}_{N},

(75)

where
P_{\mathcal{H}_{N}}:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}_{N}
is the orthogonal projector from the full Hilbert space to the lowest-twist
N
-particle states.
As discussed above, our goal in the leading order perturbation theory is to diagonalize
V_{\text{eff}}
.

Equation (
75
) and the matrix elements (
52
) fully define the operator
V_{\text{eff}}
, albeit somewhat implicitly. Here, we would like to point out that the resulting
V_{\text{eff}}
belongs to the class of so-called Toeplitz operators
[
25
,
26
]
.

Indeed, according to section
2.3
, the minimal-twist subspace
\mathcal{H}_{N}
was identified with the Hilbert space of holomorphic functions on the polydisk
\mathbb{D}^{N}
with respect to the inner product (
40
). The Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{N}
can be viewed as a closed subspace of the larger space
L^{2}(\mathbb{D}^{N})
of all square-integrable functions with the same inner product. The total energy
\sum_{i<j}U_{2}(s_{ij})
appearing in (
52
) can be understood as a (unbounded) multiplication operator acting from
\mathcal{H}_{N}
to
L^{2}(\mathbb{D}^{N})
. It is then easy to check that
V_{\text{eff}}
can be described as

\displaystyle V_{\text{eff}}=P_{\text{hol}}\sum_{i<j}U_{2}(s_{ij}),

(76)

where
P_{\text{hol}}:L^{2}(\mathbb{D}^{N})\to\mathcal{H}_{N}
is the orthogonal projector onto the subspace of holomorphic functions. That is, if we want to compute the action of
V_{\text{eff}}
on a wavefunction
\psi(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{N})
, we first compute

\displaystyle\sum_{i<j}U_{2}(s_{ij})\psi(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{N}).

(77)

This is not a holomorphic function of
\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{N}
anymore, and thus does not live in
\mathcal{H}_{N}
. We can fix this by applying the orthogonal projection
P_{\text{hol}}
to
\mathcal{H}_{N}
inside of
L^{2}(\mathbb{D}^{N})
, which yields the desired holomorphic wavefunction
(V_{\text{eff}}\psi)(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{N})
.

Operators that act in this way are known as Toeplitz operators, and the function
\sum_{i<j}U_{2}(s_{ij})
is called the symbol
11
11

11

More specifically, it is the covariant symbol as defined by Berezin
[
27
]
. Since we do not introduce any other kind of symbol for Toeplitz operators in this work, we can and will omit this qualifier without ambiguity.
of the Toeplitz operator
V_{\text{eff}}
. In a well-defined sense,
V_{\text{eff}}
can be viewed as a quantization of
\sum_{i<j}U_{2}(s_{ij})
. There exists an extensive literature on Toeplitz operators and in particular on their semiclassical behavior, see
[
28
,
29
,
30
]
and references therein. We will find this useful later when we analyze the large-
J
spectrum of
V_{\text{eff}}
.

2.7
Two-body binding energies and Lorentzian inversion formula

Using (
50
) and (
62
), it is straightforward to compute the two-particle binding energy. Indeed, given the unique primary two-particle state at spin
J
:

\displaystyle\psi_{J}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})^{J},

(78)

the binding energy is simply

\displaystyle\gamma_{2,J}=\frac{\langle\psi_{J}|V_{\text{eff}}|\psi_{J}\rangle}{\langle\psi_{J}|\psi_{J}\rangle}.

(79)

To compute the inner product and the matrix element, we can consider a more general integral of a generic function
f(s)
:

\displaystyle\langle f\rangle_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}}\equiv\int d^{4}\alpha(1-|\alpha_{1}|^{2})^{\Delta_{1}-2}(1-|\alpha_{2}|^{2})^{\Delta_{2}-2}f(s_{12}).

(80)

Using (see appendix
C.1
)

\displaystyle\int d^{4}\alpha(1-|\alpha_{1}|^{2})^{\Delta_{1}-2}(1-|\alpha_{2}|^{2})^{\Delta_{2}-2}\delta(s-s_{12})

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{2}}{\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-1}{}_{2}F_{1}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2};\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2};-s),

(81)

we find

\displaystyle\langle f\rangle_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}}=\frac{\pi^{2}}{\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}dsf(s)\,{}_{2}F_{1}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2};\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2};-s).

(82)

It is now easy to check from (
40
) and (
52
) that the two-body binding energies take the form

\displaystyle\gamma_{2,J}=\frac{\langle s^{J}U_{2}(s)\rangle_{\Delta_{\phi}+J,\Delta_{\phi}+J}}{\langle s^{J}\rangle_{\Delta_{\phi}+J,\Delta_{\phi}+J}}.

(83)

For example, using the expansion (
66
) of
U_{2}
at large separation, we get the leading large-spin asymptotics

\displaystyle\gamma_{2,J}\sim b_{0}\frac{\langle s^{J-\Delta_{\sigma}/2}\rangle_{\Delta_{\phi}+J,\Delta_{\phi}+J}}{\langle s^{J}\rangle_{\Delta_{\phi}+J,\Delta_{\phi}+J}}\sim b_{0}\frac{\Gamma(\Delta_{\phi}+\Delta_{\sigma}/2-1)^{2}}{\Gamma(\Delta_{\phi}-1)^{2}}J^{-\Delta_{\sigma}}.

(84)

We can also obtain finite-
J
results by fully resumming (
62
) and plugging it into (
83
).

The same binding energies can be obtained by computing the leading correction to the boundary four-point function
\langle\phi\phi\overline{\phi}\overline{\phi}\rangle
and examining the resulting conformal block expansion. The most convenient way of doing this is using the Lorentzian inversion formula (LIF). It is well-known that since the LIF is not sensitive to double-twist exchanges, the anomalous dimensions can be obtained by applying LIF to the
t
-channel conformal block for the exchange of
\sigma
. The resulting correction is
(
31
, section 2.3)

\displaystyle\gamma_{LIF}=-2\frac{C_{\phi\phi\sigma}^{2}\Gamma(\Delta_{\sigma})}{\Gamma(\Delta_{\sigma}/2)^{2}}\frac{\sin^{2}\pi(\Delta_{\phi}-\Delta_{\sigma}/2)}{\sin^{2}\pi\Delta_{\phi}}\frac{\int_{0}^{1}\frac{dz}{z^{2}}\left(\frac{z}{1-z}\right)^{\Delta_{\phi}}k_{2\Delta_{\phi}+2J}(z)k_{\Delta_{\sigma}}^{(d)}(1-z)}{\int_{0}^{1}\frac{dz}{z^{2}}\left(\frac{z}{1-z}\right)^{\Delta_{\phi}}k_{2\Delta_{\phi}+2J}(z)},

(85)

where we defined

\displaystyle k_{2h}^{(d)}(z):=z^{h}{}_{2}F_{1}(h,h,2h-d/2+1,z),\quad k_{2h}^{(2)}\equiv k_{2h},

(86)

and the OPE coefficient
C_{\phi\phi\sigma}
at leading order in
\lambda
is given by (see appendix
C.2
)

\displaystyle C_{\phi\phi\sigma}=\lambda\frac{\pi^{d/2}}{2}\Gamma\left(\frac{2\Delta_{\phi}+\Delta_{\sigma}-d}{2}\right)\frac{C_{\Delta_{\phi},d}}{\Gamma(\Delta_{\phi})^{2}}\frac{C_{\Delta_{\sigma},d}^{1/2}}{\Gamma(\Delta_{\sigma})}\Gamma\left(\frac{\Delta_{\sigma}}{2}\right)^{2}\Gamma\left(\Delta_{\phi}-\frac{\Delta_{\sigma}}{2}\right)+O(\lambda^{2}),

(87)

where
C_{\Delta,d}
was defined in (
32
). It is well known that the large-spin expansion stems from the
z\rightarrow 1
limit of the double-discontinuity in the inversion formula. In the case of (
85
), inserting the leading-order asymptotics
k_{\Delta_{\sigma}}^{(d)}=(1-z)^{\Delta_{\sigma}/2}(1+O(1-z))
yields

\gamma_{LIF}\sim-2\frac{C_{\phi\phi\sigma}^{2}\Gamma(\Delta_{\sigma})}{\Gamma(\Delta_{\sigma}/2)^{2}}\frac{\Gamma(\Delta_{\phi})^{2}}{\Gamma(\Delta_{\phi}-\Delta_{\sigma}/2)^{2}}J^{-\Delta_{\sigma}}.

(88)

At finite spin, the integrals in (
85
) can be expressed as a linear combination of two
{}_{4}F_{3}
hypergeometric functions using
(
31
, eqs. (2.33),(2.44))
.

Comparing
b_{0}
in (
62
) and
C_{\phi\phi\sigma}
in (
87
), we find that the leading large-spin asymptotics of
\gamma_{2,J}
in (
84
) and
\gamma_{LIF}
in (
88
) are identical. More generally, we prove in appendix
C
the exact equality
\gamma_{2,J}=\gamma_{LIF}
as analytic functions of spin.

2.8
Numerical diagonalization at finite spin

The Toeplitz operator (
75
) acts as a linear operator on the finite-dimensional subspace
\mathcal{H}_{N,J}^{\mathrm{primary}}\subset\mathcal{H}_{N}
. If its matrix elements in a basis can be computed explicitly, then we can extract the exact eigenvalues from numerical diagonalization.

At leading order in perturbation theory, the symbol of the Toeplitz operator
V_{\mathrm{eff}}
is the symmetrization of a pair potential
U_{2}(s_{12})
. In this section, for any Toeplitz operator of this form, we devise a general method to compute matrix elements of
V_{\mathrm{eff}}
in the basis (
44
) of
\mathcal{H}_{N,J}^{\mathrm{primary}}
. The same kind of numerical diagonalization problem was recently studied in
(
20
, section 4)
. These authors computed the matrix elements in a different basis, using the decomposition of
N
-twist states into iterated double-twist states in
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\otimes N}
.

To compute the matrix elements of
V_{\mathrm{eff}}
, our strategy is to first determine the eigenvalues and a basis of eigenvectors for the pair potentials
P_{\mathrm{hol}}U_{2}(s_{ij})
. We then compute the basis-change matrix from these pair potential eigenvectors to the functions in (
44
). The basis-change matrices and the pair potential eigenvalues fully determine the matrix elements of
V_{\text{eff}}
.

Without loss of generality, we consider the Toeplitz operator
P_{\mathrm{hol}}U_{2}(s_{12})
of the
(12)
pair. Since the pair potential breaks the
S_{N}
symmetry down to
S_{2}\times S_{N-2}
, the latter is actually an operator on
\mathcal{H}_{2}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{N-2}
, represented by holomorphic square-integrable functions
\psi(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\dots,\alpha_{N})
that are symmetric under
\alpha_{1}\leftrightarrow\alpha_{2}
and permutations of
(\alpha_{3},\dots,\alpha_{N})
. The eigenspaces of
P_{\mathrm{hol}}U_{2}(s_{12})
inside
\mathcal{H}_{2}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{N-2}
can be characterized using the representation theory of
\mathfrak{so}(2,1)
. In this context, recall that
\mathcal{H}_{1}\equiv\mathcal{H}_{1}(\Delta_{\phi}/2)
is an irreducible lowest-weight representation with of lowest weight
\overline{h}=\Delta_{\phi}/2
, as follows from the action (
34
) of the generators. Then
\mathcal{H}_{2}\equiv\mathcal{H}_{2}(\Delta_{\phi}/2)
is the symmetrized tensor product of two such lowest-weight representations, which is known to decompose into an infinite direct sum of lowest-weight representations with lowest-weight
\Delta_{\phi}+\ell
, where
\ell
is an even spin label:

\mathcal{H}_{2}(\Delta_{\phi}/2)\cong\bigoplus_{\ell\in 2\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{H}_{1}(\Delta_{\phi}+\ell).

(89)

Each vector space in the direct sum is spanned by the action of the raising operator
(L_{1}+L_{2})_{-}
on the lowest-weight vector
\psi_{2,\ell}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})^{\ell}
. Since
U_{2}(s_{12})
is invariant under
\mathfrak{so}(2,1)
, its corresponding Toeplitz operator commutes with all
\mathfrak{so}(2,1)
generators, such that it must act as a constant on the irreducible subspaces. By acting on the lowest-weight vector, we deduce that
\mathcal{H}_{1}(\Delta_{\phi}+\ell)
are the eigenspaces of
P_{\mathrm{hol}}U_{2}(s_{12})
with eigenvalues
\gamma_{2,\ell}
corresponding to the two-body binding energies computed in section
2.7
.

Now, just like we saw previously, the subspace
(\mathcal{H}_{2}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{N-2})_{J}^{\mathrm{primary}}
of spin-
J
primaries is obtained by imposing that its wavefunctions
\psi
are translation-invariant and homogeneous of degree
J
. The tensor product decomposition (
89
) then implies that
P_{\mathrm{hol}}U_{2}(s_{12})
has eigenspaces
(\mathcal{H}_{1}(\Delta_{\phi}+\ell)\otimes\mathcal{H}_{N-2})_{J}^{\mathrm{primary}}
with eigenvalues
\gamma_{2,\ell}
. To find an explicit basis of functions
\psi_{\ell}^{(12)}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{N})
for these eigenspaces, we use the quadratic Casimir operator for the action of
\mathfrak{so}(2,1)
on
\mathcal{H}_{2}\subset\mathcal{H}_{2}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{N-2}
. As a quadratic form in the generators, it is given by

L_{ij}:=\mathcal{C}^{2}(L_{i}+L_{j}),\quad\mathcal{C}^{2}(L):=L_{0}(L_{0}-1)-L_{-}L_{+}.

(90)

Moreover, it shares the same eigenspaces
\mathcal{H}_{1}(\Delta_{\phi}+\ell)\subset\mathcal{H}_{2}
with eigenvalues
(\Delta_{\phi}+\ell)(\Delta_{\phi}+\ell-1)
. It is convenient to express the action of
L_{12}
in terms of the following translation-invariant variables:

r:=\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2},\quad s:=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}-2\frac{\alpha_{3}+\dots+\alpha_{N}}{N-2},\quad\gamma_{k}:=\alpha_{k}-\frac{\alpha_{3}+\dots+\alpha_{N}}{N-2},

(91)

where
k=1,\dots,N
and
\gamma_{3}+\dots+\gamma_{N}=0
. We can always find a translation gauge where
\alpha_{1,2}=(r\pm s)/2
and
\alpha_{3,\dots,N}=\gamma_{3,\dots,N}
. Denoting
\psi(\alpha):=\chi(r,s,\gamma)
, it is then easy to check that the quadratic Casimir acts as

(L_{12}\psi)\left(\frac{r+s}{2},\frac{r-s}{2},\gamma\right)=r^{2-\Delta_{\phi}}(\partial_{r}^{2}-\partial_{s}^{2})r^{\Delta_{\phi}}\chi(r,s,\gamma).

(92)

Since the above differential operator is completely independent of
\gamma_{3},\dots,\gamma_{N}
, it admits a basis of eigenfunctions on
(\mathcal{H}_{2}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{N-2})_{J}^{\text{primary}}
with the factorized form

\psi_{\ell,m}^{(12)}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{N}):=P_{\ell,J-|m|}(r,s)\,e_{m}(\gamma_{3},\dots,\gamma_{N}),

(93)

where the first factor is an eigenfunction of the Casimir operator (
92
), while the second factor is a product of elementary symmetric polynomials

e_{m}(\gamma):=\prod_{k=2}^{N-2}e_{k}(\gamma)^{m_{k}},\quad|m|:=\sum_{k=2}^{N-2}k\,m_{k}\leq J.

(94)

Note that
P_{\ell,J-|m|}
must be homogeneous of degree
J-|m|
in
(r,s)
for
\psi_{\ell,m}^{(12)}
to be homogeneous of degree
J
in
\alpha
. This constraint reduces the eigenvalue equation (
92
) with eigenvalue
(\Delta_{\phi}+\ell)(\Delta_{\phi}+\ell-1)
to an ODE in
r/s
with polynomial solution

P_{\ell,j}(r,s)=r^{\ell}s^{j-\ell}\,{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\frac{1+\ell-j}{2},\frac{\ell-j}{2},\Delta_{\phi}+\ell+\frac{1}{2};\frac{r^{2}}{s^{2}}\right).

(95)

Now that we have determined the eigenvalues
\gamma_{2,\ell}
and a basis of eigenvectors
\psi_{\ell,m}^{(12)}
for the two-particle operators
U_{2}(s_{12})
, we can find the action of
U_{2}(s_{12})
in the basis
f_{m}(\alpha)
given by (
44
). For this, we determine the transformation matrices between the basis
f_{m}
and
\psi_{\ell,m}^{(12)}
and conjugate by them the diagonal matrix that represents
U_{2}(s_{12})
in the
\psi_{\ell,m}^{(12)}
basis.

A subtlety is that the transition matrices are not square since the basis
\psi_{\ell,m}^{(12)}
is not
S_{N}
-invariant, but rather only
S_{2}\times S_{N-2}
-invariant. The matrix that maps from the
S_{2}\times S_{N-2}
-invariant wavefunctions to
S_{N}
-invariant wavefunctions is therefore obtained by first applying
S_{N}
symmetrization.

3
Three-body problem at large spin

In the previous section, we saw in our toy model that the subspace
\mathcal{H}_{N}
of leading-twist
N
-particle states can be described by the wavefunctions
\psi(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{N})
, holomorphic in
\alpha_{i}
, which range over the hyperbolic disk
\mathbb{D}
equipped with the inner product (
40
). We also saw that the twist Hamiltonian restricted to this subspace is given by a Toeplitz operator with the symbol

\displaystyle\tau=N\Delta_{\phi}+\sum_{i<j}U_{2}(s_{ij}),

(96)

where the effective potential is given in (
62
). Equivalently
\tau=N\Delta_{\phi}+V
, with the matrix elements of
V
given by (
52
). For
N=2
, we explicitly verified that this twist Hamitonian exactly reproduces the Lorentzian inversion formula result for the
t
-exchange of the scalar
\sigma
.

In the rest of this paper, we will analyze the spectral problem of the above type for
N\geq 3
particles, focusing on
N=3
in this section. We will only be interested in the large-spin limit, i.e. the spectrum of
\tau
on
\mathcal{H}_{N,J}
with
J\gg 1
. The methods we develop will not rely on the specific form of the twist Hamiltonian
\tau
. While we will keep using (
96
) in examples for concreteness, the same methods apply to the more general twist Hamiltonian with the symbol

\displaystyle\tau=N\Delta_{\phi}+U_{N}(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{N}),

(97)

as long as the (non-holomorphic)
N
-body potential
U_{N}
has suitable behavior at large distances. Although we have no proof of this, we expect that quite generally the multi-twist operators in CFT can be described by a model of type (
97
) at large spin, with suitably chosen
U_{N}
. In fact, as we discuss in section
2.5
, generically one should expect the two-body interactions of the type (
96
) to dominate at large spin.

The anomalous dimension operator
\gamma=\tau-N\Delta_{\phi}
is the Toeplitz operator with symbol
U_{N}
, schematically

\displaystyle\gamma=U_{N}(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{N}).

(98)

We will usually discuss the spectrum of
\gamma
rather than
\tau
, which is more convenient due to the absence of the constant
N\Delta_{\phi}
shift.

The key to understanding the spectrum of
\gamma
at large spin
J
is to realize that the problem becomes semi-classical with
\hbar=J^{-1}
. We do not know of a simple and rigorous way of demonstrating this. In particular, the classical system obtained in the
J\to\infty
limit is not easy to construct directly. In the context of planar conformal gauge theories, the semiclassical nature of the large-spin limit was derived explicitly using methods from integrability
[
32
,
33
,
34
,
35
,
36
,
37
]
.

Heuristically, we can look at the classical problem of
N
particles in constant magnetic field in the hyperbolic disk
\mathbb{D}
that was discussed in the introduction. Spin
J
is just a charge associated to one of the generators of
\mathfrak{so}(2,1)
isometries of the hyperbolic disc
\mathbb{D}
. To make
J
more explicit, we can perform the symplectic reduction
[
38
]
of the classical phase space with respect to these isometries.
12
12

12

Note that after the restriction to the LLL, the effective classical phase space becomes
\mathbb{D}^{N}
in which the Landau centers move.
One can then check that the reduced Poisson bracket
\{f,g\}
becomes proportional to
J^{-1}
as
J\to\infty
. Since the quantization condition is
[f,g]\approx i\hbar\{f,g\}
, this is equivalent to having a small
\hbar\sim J^{-1}
. This semiclassical behavior is not apparent prior to the symplectic reduction, partly because the spin
J
is implicit, and partly because the trivial “center-of-mass motion” that is modded out by the reduction is not semiclassical.
We do not reproduce this calculation in detail since it is in any case non-rigorous and would require an otherwise unnecessary discussion of symplectic reduction.

Nevertheless, the idea of performing some sort of reduction with respect to the isometries of
\mathbb{D}
will be useful in our fully quantum problem. We will carry it out in the next subsections. We will see that the Hamiltonian of the reduced problem is still a Toeplitz operator, albeit now in a more general setting of Berezin-Toeplitz quantization with
\hbar=J^{-1}
.

3.1
Line bundles on
{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{N-2}
and Berezin-Toeplitz quantization

The analogue of symplectic reduction in our quantum problem is simply the restriction of
\tau
to the subspace of primary wavefunctions with spin
J
, i.e. to
\mathcal{H}^{\text{primary}}_{N,J}
. In terms of the wavefunctions
\psi(\alpha)
, this means that
\psi
is a homogeneous degree-
J
symmetric polynomial that is also translationally-invariant, see section
2.3
.

For the moment, let us ignore the restriction that
\psi
is symmetric. Translation invariance means that
\psi
is, in effect, a function on
\mathbb{C}^{N-1}
. If
\psi
were homogeneous of degree
0
, i.e. invariant under the action of the multiplicative group
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
on
\mathbb{C}^{N-1}
, this would imply that
\psi
can be seen as a function on
{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{N-2}=\mathbb{C}^{N-1}/\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. However,
\psi
is homogeneous with generally non-zero degree
J
. It is well-known that rather than being functions on
{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{N-2}
, such
\psi
describe holomorphic sections of the holomorphic line bundle
{\mathcal{O}}(J)
on
{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{N-2}
.
13
13

13

In this notation, the tautological line bundle of
{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{N-2}
is
{\mathcal{O}}(-1)
and the canonical line bundle (the bundle of holomorphic
(N-2)
-forms) is
{\mathcal{O}}(-(N-1))
. The line bundle
{\mathcal{O}}(m)
is dual to
{\mathcal{O}}(-m)
, and
{\mathcal{O}}(0)
is the trivial bundle.
We denote
\mathcal{L}={\mathcal{O}}(1)
, so that
{\mathcal{O}}(J)=\mathcal{L}^{\otimes J}
.

Restoring the permutation invariance, we can write

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\text{primary}}_{N,J}\simeq\Gamma({\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{N-2},\mathcal{L}^{\otimes J})^{S_{N}}.

(99)

In words, we have identified
\mathcal{H}^{\text{primary}}_{N,J}
with the space of
S_{N}
-invariant holomophic sections of the line bundle
\mathcal{L}^{\otimes J}
over
{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{N-2}
. Somewhat related is the fact that the symplectic reduction of the classical phase space discussed earlier also yields a phase space homeomorphic to
{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{N-2}
.

Inspecting our expressions (
40
) and (
52
) for the inner product on
\mathcal{H}^{\text{primary}}_{N,J}
and for the matrix elements of
V_{\text{eff}}
, we see that they are not written as integrals over
{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{N-2}
, but rather as integrals over the higher-dimensional
\mathbb{D}^{N}
. In other words, four real integrations in (
40
) and (
52
) can be performed using the homogeneity and translation-invariance of the wavefunctions
\psi
, leaving only the integration over
{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{N-2}
.

After this procedure, we expect the inner product to take the form

\displaystyle\langle\psi_{1}|\psi_{2}\rangle=\int_{{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{N-2}}d\mu_{J}h_{J}(\psi_{1},\psi_{2}),

(100)

where
d\mu_{J}
is some (in general,
J
-dependent) measure on
{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{N-1}
and
h_{J}=h_{1}^{\otimes J}
, where
h_{1}
is a Hermitian inner product on
\mathcal{L}
. Similarly, for the matrix elements of
V_{\text{eff}}
we expect

\displaystyle\langle\psi_{1}|\gamma|\psi_{2}\rangle=\int_{{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{N-2}}d\mu_{J}h_{J}(\psi_{1},\psi_{2})\mathcal{U}_{N,J},

(101)

where
\mathcal{U}_{N,J}
is a function on
{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{N-2}
.

We will find that the measure
d\mu_{J}
and the effective potential
\mathcal{U}_{N,J}
have good series expansions in powers of
J^{-1}
. This turns our problem, in the formulation of equations (
99
), (
100
) and (
101
), into a Berezin-Toeplitz quantization setup
[
39
,
40
]
with
\hbar=J^{-1}
. This interpretation allows us to immediately identify the classical problem that arises in the large-
J
limit. In particular, the classical Hamiltonian is simply the leading term in
\mathcal{U}_{N,J}
, while the classical symplectic form is given by the Chern curvature of
h_{1}
. We discuss this in more detail in section
3.3
, after computing
d\mu_{J}
and
\mathcal{U}_{N,J}
in section
3.2
.

A caveat to the above discussion is that this procedure works literally only for
N=3
. For
N\geq 4
and at large
J
, the classical phase space localizes onto an infinitesimal neighborhood of a positive-codimension locus in
{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{N-2}
and the discussion becomes more subtle. We will discuss the case of general
N
in section
4
using an alternative description of the Hilbert space. However, the general strategy will remain the same. For the rest of this section we mostly specialize to
N=3
, although we often keep
N
as a parameter to clarify where it enters. Some of the discussion applies to
N\geq 4
and will be reused in section
4
.

3.2
Computation of
d\mu_{J}
,
h_{1}
and
\mathcal{U}_{N,J}

In order to cast (
40
) into the form (
100
), we need to perform the integration over the orbits of
\mathbb{C}^{\times}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
that acts on
\alpha
by complex translations and rescalings. Since
\mathbb{C}^{\times}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
is not unimodular, there is a difference between left- and right-invariant measures, making the procedure somewhat subtle. Let us first consider a slightly more general and abstract version of the problem.

Specifically, we focus on the integral

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\mu(x)A(x)B(x),

(102)

where
d\mu
is some measure on
\mathcal{M}
, and there is an action of a group
G
on
\mathcal{M}
. We assume

\displaystyle B(gx)=\chi(g)^{2J}B(x),\quad d\mu(gx)=\chi(g)^{2N}d\mu(x),\quad\forall g\in G

(103)

for some multiplicative character
\chi
of
G
, while
A(x)
does not have any nice transformation properties. We would like to rewrite the integral over
\mathcal{M}
as an integral over
G
-orbits. To parameterize the
G
-orbit integral explicitly, we introduce a gauge-fixing function
F(x)
such that
F(x)=0
precisely once on each
G
-orbit. We then write
14
14

14

In general,
F
is valued in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for some
n
and the delta-function in this equation should be the
n
-dimensional delta-function.

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{F}(x)^{-1}=\int_{G}dg\,\delta(F(gx)),

(104)

where
dg
is the right-invariant measure. By construction,
\mathcal{D}_{F}(gx)=\mathcal{D}_{F}(x)
. A simple manipulation now yields

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\mu(x)A(x)B(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{G}dg\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\mu(x)A(x)B(x)\mathcal{D}_{F}(x)\delta(F(gx))

\displaystyle=\int_{G}dg\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\mu(g^{-1}x)A(g^{-1}x)B(g^{-1}x)\mathcal{D}_{F}(g^{-1}x)\delta(F(x))

\displaystyle=\int_{G}dg\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\mu(x)\chi(g)^{-2J-2N}A(g^{-1}x)B(x)\mathcal{D}_{F}(x)\delta(F(x))

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\mu(x)\widetilde{A}(x)B(x)\mathcal{D}_{F}(x)\delta(F(x)),

(105)

where

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}(x)=\int_{G}dg\,\chi(g)^{-2J-2N}A(g^{-1}x).

(106)

The integral (
105
) is a gauge-fixed integral over orbits, so we have succeeded. The subtlety alluded to above is that (
106
) contains
A(g^{-1}x)
integrated with the right-invariant measure
dg
. If we try to simplify
\widetilde{A}(hx)
for
h\in G
, this will involve comparing
d(hg)
with
dg
which are related by the modular function of
G
. In particular, the product
d\mu(x)\widetilde{A}(x)B(x)
is not formally invariant under
G
but transforms according to the modular function of
G
. Although it is easy to derive, we will not need the explicit transformation rule.

In the case at hand, we have
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{C}^{2N}
,
G=\mathbb{C}^{\times}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
. We parameterize
g=(\lambda,\beta)
with
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
and
\beta\in\mathbb{C}
. The composition law is
(\lambda_{1},\beta_{1})(\lambda_{2},\beta_{2})=(\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2},\lambda_{1}\beta_{2}+\beta_{1})
and the action on
\mathbb{C}^{N}
is
(g\alpha)_{i}=\lambda\alpha_{i}+\beta
for
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. We will often abuse notation by writing this formally as
g\alpha=\lambda\alpha+\beta
. The right-invariant measure is

\displaystyle dg=d^{2}\lambda d^{2}\beta|\lambda|^{-2}.

(107)

We identify

\displaystyle d\mu(\alpha)=d^{2N}\alpha,\quad B(\alpha)=\overline{\psi}(\alpha)\psi(\alpha),\quad\chi((\lambda,\beta))=|\lambda|.

(108)

It is convenient to define
(x)^{a}_{+}=x^{a}\theta(x)
, so that we can extend the integration in (
40
) over
\alpha_{i}
from
\mathbb{D}
to
\mathbb{C}
,

\displaystyle\langle\psi_{1}|\psi_{2}\rangle=\frac{(\Delta_{\phi}-1)^{N}}{\pi^{N}N!}\int d^{2N}\alpha\prod_{k=1}^{N}(1-|\alpha_{k}|^{2})_{+}^{\Delta_{\phi}-2}\overline{\psi_{1}(\alpha)}\psi_{2}(\alpha).

(109)

We can now identify
A
as

\displaystyle A(\alpha)=\frac{(\Delta_{\phi}-1)^{N}}{\pi^{N}N!}\prod_{k=1}^{N}(1-|\alpha_{k}|^{2})_{+}^{\Delta_{\phi}-2}.

(110)

Following (
106
), we then define

\displaystyle M_{J}(\alpha)=\widetilde{A}(\alpha)=\frac{(\Delta_{\phi}-1)^{N}}{\pi^{N}N!}\int d^{2}\lambda d^{2}\beta|\lambda|^{-2-2N-2J}\prod_{k=1}^{N}(1-|\lambda^{-1}(\alpha_{k}-\beta)|^{2})_{+}^{\Delta_{\phi}-2}.

(111)

It is easy to check that
M_{J}(\alpha)
is translation-invariant and satisfies

\displaystyle M_{J}(\lambda\alpha)=|\lambda|^{2-2N-2J}M_{J}(\alpha).

(112)

The shift by
2
from the naive value
-2N-2J
in the exponent of
|\lambda|
comes from the modular function of
\mathbb{C}^{\times}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
, as discussed above. Using (
105
), we get

\displaystyle\langle\psi_{1}|\psi_{2}\rangle=\int d^{2N}\alpha\mathcal{D}_{F}(\alpha)\delta(F(\alpha))M_{J}(\alpha)\overline{\psi_{1}(\alpha)}\psi_{2}(\alpha),

(113)

which is now an integral over
{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{N-2}
(we leave the choice of the gauge-fixing function
F
implicit for now). However, the integrand has not yet been fully factorized into the form (
100
).

To proceed, let
r(\alpha)
