\partial_{z_{ij}}=\partial_{z_{i}}-\partial_{z_{j}}
. Since
U_{\mathrm{Cas}}(L)
is not a polynomial, the operator
H_{N}(z,\partial_{z})
is not a differential operator. However, the large-
L
expansion of
U_{\mathrm{Cas}}(L)
with exponents in
-\Delta_{\sigma}/2+\mathbb{N}
makes it a pseudodifferential operator in the sense of Hörmander
[
57
]
. For this class of operators, the integration by parts of
H(z,\partial)
does generalize to the action of (
239
) on
\tilde{M}_{J}
, leading to the equality of matrix elements

\langle{\tilde{\psi}}_{1}|H_{N}(z,\partial_{z}){\tilde{\psi}}_{2}\rangle=\langle{\tilde{\psi}}_{1}|\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{N,J}(z){\tilde{\psi}}_{2}\rangle,\quad\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{N,J}(z)\tilde{M}_{J}(z)={}^{t}\tilde{H}_{N}(z,\partial_{z})\tilde{M}_{J}(z),

(240)

for any
{\tilde{\psi}}_{1,2}\in\mathcal{H}_{N,J}
. In conclusion,
H_{N}(z,\partial_{z})
is a Toeplitz operator with the symbol
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{N,J}
.

4.4
Large-spin expansion and pseudodifferential operators

Having reduced the
N
-body Hamiltonian in momentum space to a Toeplitz operator for a function on
{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{N-2}
, we can now study the large-spin limit as a semiclassical expansion in the framework of Berezin-Toeplitz quantization, introduced in section
3.3
. To this end, we compute the large-spin expansion of the measure
d\tilde{\mu}_{J}
, the Hermitian form
\tilde{h}_{J}
, and the symbol
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{N,J}
of the Toeplitz operator.

Let’s start with
\tilde{M}_{J}
, defined by the integral (
236
), which determines the measure and Hermitian form via (
237
). At large spin, the factor of
|\lambda|^{-2J}
ensures that the integral is dominated by the small
\lambda
region of the integrand, which corresponds to the large-argument limit of the Bessel-Clifford functions:

\mathcal{K}_{1-\Delta_{\phi}}(x^{2})=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2}x^{\Delta_{\phi}-\frac{3}{2}}e^{-2x}\left(1+O(x^{-1})\right).

(241)

Plugging this expansion into (
236
) yields a Gamma function integral in
|\lambda|
, from which we obtain the leading large-spin expansion of
\tilde{M}_{J}
:

\tilde{M}_{J}(z)=C^{\prime}_{\tilde{M}}(\Delta_{\phi},J)\left(\sum_{k=1}^{N}|z_{k}|\right)^{2(1-N-J)}\prod_{k=1}^{N}\left(\frac{|z_{k}|}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}|z_{\ell}|}\right)^{\Delta_{\phi}-\frac{3}{2}}\left(1+O(J^{-1})\right),

(242)

where
C^{\prime}_{\tilde{M}}(\Delta,J)=\pi^{N/2}2^{2-(\Delta+3/2)N-2J}\Gamma(2J+N(\Delta+1/2)-2)C_{\tilde{M}}(\Delta,J)
is another multiplicative constant that will not matter for physical observables. Higher-order corrections can also be systematically computed from the large-argument expansion of the Bessel function, but we will not need them in this work.

Based on (
242
) we define

\tilde{h}_{J}({\tilde{\psi}}_{1},{\tilde{\psi}}_{2})=e^{-J\mathcal{K}}\overline{{\tilde{\psi}}_{1}}{\tilde{\psi}}_{2},\quad\mathcal{K}(z):=2\log\sum_{k=1}^{N}|z_{k}|.

(243)

This is chosen so that
e^{J\mathcal{K}}\tilde{M}_{J}
does not have factors exponential in
J
. Note that
\tilde{h}_{J}=\tilde{h}_{1}^{\otimes J}
and, as discussed in section
3
, the classical symplectic form in Berezin-Toeplitz quantization is identified with the curvature of then Chern connection of
\tilde{h}_{1}
. In other words,
\mathcal{K}
can be viewed as the Kähler potential on the phase space,
22
22

22

Strictly speaking,
\mathcal{K}
is not a function on
{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{N-2}
because it is not homogeneous in
z_{k}
. However, if we choose a local holomorphic embedding of
\varphi:{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{N-2}\to\mathbb{C}^{N}
, the pullback
\varphi^{*}\mathcal{K}
defines a function locally on
{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{N-2}
. It is easy to check that if
\chi
is a different holomorphic embedding, then
\chi^{*}\mathcal{K}=\varphi^{*}\mathcal{K}+f+\overline{f}
, where
f
is a holomorphic function. Therefore,
\partial\overline{\partial}\mathcal{K}
is independent of the embedding.
and the symplectic form is given by

\displaystyle\omega=i\partial\overline{\partial}\mathcal{K}.

(244)

The large-spin expansion of
d\tilde{\mu}_{J}
is then determined from (
237
) and (
242
):

\displaystyle d\tilde{\mu}_{J}=C^{\prime}_{\tilde{M}}(\Delta_{\phi},J)\frac{d^{2N}z}{\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}\mathbb{C}^{\times}}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{N}|z_{k}|\right)^{2(1-N)}\prod_{k=1}^{N}\left(\frac{|z_{k}|}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}|z_{\ell}|}\right)^{\Delta_{\phi}-\frac{3}{2}}\left(1+O(J^{-1})\right).

(245)

We would now like to compute the large-spin expansion of
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{N,J}(z)
in (
240
). Note that
\tilde{M}_{J}=\mathrm{const}\times e^{-J\mathcal{K}}f
, where
f
has an expansion in power of
1/J
. Concretely,

\displaystyle f(z)=\left(\sum_{k=1}^{N}|z_{k}|\right)^{2(1-N)}\prod_{k=1}^{N}\left(\frac{|z_{k}|}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}|z_{\ell}|}\right)^{\Delta_{\phi}-\frac{3}{2}}\left(1+O(J^{-1})\right).

(246)

We can then rewrite (
240
) as

\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{N,J}(z)f(z)=\sum_{i<j}U_{\mathrm{Cas}}\left({}^{t}L_{ij}(z,\partial_{z}-J\partial_{z}\mathcal{K})\right)f(z),

(247)

where have effectively conjugated by
\exp(-J\mathcal{K})
. At large
J
, each two-particle Casimir will scale like
J^{2}
, such that we can replace
U_{\mathrm{Cas}}
by its large-argument expansion. In general, the leading term is of the form

U_{\mathrm{Cas}}(L)=b_{\mathrm{Cas},0}\,L^{-\Delta_{\sigma}/2}\left(1+O(L^{-1})\right).

(248)

For the anomalous dimension of the AdS toy model, we have specifically
b_{\mathrm{Cas},0}=b_{0}\,\Gamma(\Delta_{\phi}+\Delta_{\sigma}/2-1)^{2}\Gamma(\Delta_{\phi}-1)^{-2}
, where
b_{0}
is given by (
64
).

Our computation therefore reduces to the large-spin expansion of
{}^{t}L_{ij}(z,\partial_{z}-J\partial_{z}\mathcal{K})^{-\Delta_{\sigma}/2}f(z)
. This expansion is well-defined on any domain of
{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{N-2}
where
{}^{t}L_{ij}(z,-J\partial_{z}\mathcal{K})>0
. Indeed, since
{}^{t}L_{ij}
is a second-order differential operator, its expansion takes the form

{}^{t}L_{ij}(z,\partial_{z}-J\partial_{z}\mathcal{K})=J^{2}a_{0}(z,\partial_{z}\mathcal{K})+J\mathcal{D}_{1}(z,\partial_{z}\mathcal{K},\partial_{z}^{2}\mathcal{K},\partial_{z})+\mathcal{D}_{2}(z,\partial_{z}),

(249)

for some function
a_{0}
and some first- and second-order differential operators
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}
, respectively. In this case, the action of
{}^{t}L_{ij}(z,\partial_{z}-J\partial_{z}\mathcal{K})^{-\Delta_{\sigma}/2}
on
f
to subleading order is

{}^{t}L_{ij}(z,\partial_{z}-J\partial_{z}\mathcal{K})^{-\Delta_{\sigma}/2}f=J^{-\Delta_{\sigma}}\left(a_{0}^{-\Delta_{\sigma}/2}-\tfrac{\Delta_{\sigma}}{2}J^{-1}a_{0}^{-\Delta_{\sigma}/2-1}\mathcal{D}_{1}+O(J^{-2})\right)f.

(250)

Using this strategy, we obtain the large-spin expansion of
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{N,J}
to subleading order (the precision of (
242
) is enough for this). Defining the symbol
H_{\mathrm{symb}}:=J^{\Delta_{\sigma}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{N,J}
, we find

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{symb}}

\displaystyle=H^{(0)}_{\text{symb}}+J^{-1}H_{\mathrm{symb}}^{(1)}+O(J^{-2}),

(251)

\displaystyle H^{(0)}_{\text{symb}}

\displaystyle=b_{\mathrm{Cas},0}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq N}\left(\frac{2|z_{i}z_{j}|-z_{i}\overline{z}_{j}-\overline{z}_{i}z_{j}}{(|z_{1}|+\dots+|z_{N}|)^{2}}\right)^{-\frac{\Delta_{\sigma}}{2}}.

(252)

We omit the explicit expression for the subleading term
H_{\mathrm{symb}}^{(1)}
, which will only enter the calculation of the ground state energy. While we will not use them in this work, higher orders can be systematically computed using the calculus of pseudodifferential operators, summarized in appendix
E
.

In conclusion, we have assembled all the ingredients to apply semiclassical methods in Berezin-Toeplitz quantization: the Hermitian form (
243
), the measure (
245
), and the symbol (
251
) of the Hamiltonian. In particular, the classical limit is specified by the Kähler potential
\mathcal{K}
and the Hamiltonian
H_{\mathrm{symb}}^{(0)}
.

4.5
Geometry of the classical phase space

In the previous sections we derived a phase space of complex dimension
N-2
given by the hypersurface
z_{1}+\dots+z_{N}=0
in
{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{N-1}
, with Kähler potential
\mathcal{K}=2\log(|z_{1}|+\dots+|z_{N}|)
. We will denote it by
\mathcal{M}
. A useful approach to this Kähler manifold is to view it as a quotient of the projective null cone

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}=\{W\in{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{N-1}|W^{2}=0\},

(253)

where
W
is the vector of homogeneous coordinates on
{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{N-1}
. We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{C}/(\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{N}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\text{diag}}),

(254)

where
(s_{1},\cdots,s_{N})\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{N}
acts on
\mathcal{C}
by
W_{k}\mapsto s_{k}W_{k}
. The element
(-1,-1,\cdots,-1)\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{N}
acts trivially on
\mathcal{C}
since
W\sim-W
, hence the quotient by the diagonal
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\text{diag}}
.

The manifold
\mathcal{C}
is a Kähler manifold equipped with the Kähler potential
\mathcal{K}=2\log||W||^{2}
which is
4\times
the standard Kähler potential
\frac{1}{2}\log||W||^{2}
induced from
{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{N-1}
. In fact,
\mathcal{C}
is isomorphic to the real Grassmannian of oriented two-planes in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, which is a Hermitian symmetric space associated to
\mathrm{SO}(N)
:

\mathcal{C}\cong\mathrm{Gr}(2,N)\cong\frac{\mathrm{SO}(N)}{\mathrm{SO}(2)\times\mathrm{SO}(N-2)}\cong\left\{W\in\mathbb{C}^{N}\,|\,W\sim\lambda W,\,W^{2}=0\right\}.

(255)

To see this, set
e_{1}=\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}W
,
e_{2}=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}W\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
and note that
W^{2}=0
is equivalent to
|e_{1}|=|e_{2}|
and
e_{1}\cdot e_{2}=0
. By positive rescalings of
W
we can set
|e_{1}|=|e_{2}|=1
, such that the pair
(e_{1},e_{2})
forms and oriented orthonormal basis of a two-plane in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. After modding out by the remaining phase rotations of
W
, we lose the information about the choice of basis and only the two-plane with its orientation remain. Note also that the Kähler potential
\mathcal{K}
is invariant under the action of
\mathrm{SO}(N)
.

To summarize, we now view the phase space
\mathcal{M}
as
\mathrm{Gr}(2,N)/(\mathbb{Z}^{N}_{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\text{diag}})
.

We will say that
z\in\mathcal{M}
is regular if all its components
z_{k}
are non-zero and have distinct phases; almost all elements of
\mathcal{M}
are regular. We say that
W\in\mathrm{Gr}(2,N)
is regular if it projects to a regular element in
\mathcal{M}
. Equivalently, if all
W_{k}
are non-zero and none are a real multiple of another. Each regular
z\in\mathcal{M}
defines a cyclic ordering of integers
\{1,\cdots,N\}
which is obtained by reading
z_{k}/|z_{k}|
counterclockwise on the unit circle. We denote by
\mathcal{M}_{(a\cdots b)}
the open set of regular elements with the cyclic ordering
(a\cdots b)
.

We claim that every
\mathcal{M}_{(a\cdots b)}
is isomorphic to the positive Grassmannian
\mathrm{Gr}_{+}(2,N)
. The positive Grassmannian
\mathrm{Gr}_{+}(2,N)
is the subset of
\mathrm{Gr}(2,N)
on which the Plücker coordinates
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(W_{j}\overline{W}_{i})
satisfy

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(W_{j}\overline{W}_{i})>0,\quad\forall i,j\text{ such that }i<j.

(256)

Note that this condition is invariant under complex rescalings of
W
and that
\mathrm{Gr}_{+}(2,N)
contains only regular elements.

It is enough to prove our claim that
\mathcal{M}_{(a\cdots b)}\simeq\mathrm{Gr}_{+}(2,N)
for the standard cyclic ordering
(1\cdots N)
since every
\mathcal{M}_{(a\cdots b)}
can be related to
\mathcal{M}_{(1\cdots N)}
by a
S_{N}
permutation of
z_{k}
’s.

Let
\pi:\mathrm{Gr}(2,N)\to\mathcal{M}
be the canonical projection. We want to show that for every
z\in\mathcal{M}_{(1\cdots N)}
,
\pi^{-1}(z)
contains precisely one point from
\mathrm{Gr}_{+}(2,N)
, and that
\pi(\mathrm{Gr}_{+}(2,N))=\mathcal{M}_{(1\cdots N)}
. First, given a
z\in\mathcal{M}_{(1\cdots N)}
we can assume that
z_{1}=1
and define
W_{k}=\sqrt{z_{k}}
, where we place the branch cut of the square root just below the real axis. Note that
W\in\pi^{-1}(z)
. It is easy to check that
W_{k}=r_{k}\,e^{i\phi_{k}}
with
r_{k}>0
and
\phi_{k}\in[0,\pi)
strictly increasing, which immediately implies (
256
) and thus
W\in\mathrm{Gr}_{+}(2,N)
. All other
W^{\prime}\in\pi^{-1}(z)
are obtained from
W
by non-trivial elements
(s_{1},\cdots,s_{N})\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{N}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\text{diag}}
. If the element
(s_{1},\cdots,s_{N})
is non-trival, then there is a pair
s_{i},s_{j}
such that
s_{i}s_{j}=-1
, which means
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(W^{\prime}_{j}\overline{W}^{\prime}_{i})=-\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(W_{j}\overline{W}_{i})<0
and so
W^{\prime}\not\in\mathrm{Gr}_{+}(2,N)
.

Second, if
W\in\mathrm{Gr}_{+}(2,N)
then (assuming, as we can,
W_{1}=1
) equation (
256
) implies that
W_{k}=r_{k}\,e^{i\phi_{k}}
with
r_{k}>0
and
\phi_{k}\in[0,\pi)
strictly increasing. This implies that
z_{k}=W_{k}^{2}
is a regular element with the cyclic ordering
(1\cdots N)
, i.e.
z\in\mathcal{M}_{(1\cdots N)}
as required. This finishes the proof of our claim.

As we will soon see, the classical Hamiltonian blows up near the boundaries of the sets
\mathcal{M}_{(a\cdots b)}
. Therefore, only the set
\mathcal{M}^{\text{reg}}
of regular elements is classically accessible . Note that
\mathcal{M}^{\text{reg}}
is the disjoint union of the sets
\mathcal{M}_{(a\cdots b)}
for all cyclic orderings
(a\cdots b)
. The permutation group
S_{N}
generally acts by permuting the connected components
\mathcal{M}_{(a\cdots b)}
. However, some permutations can preserve a cycling ordering
(a\cdots b)
. In particular the group
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
of cyclic permutations of
(1\cdots N)
preserves
\mathcal{M}_{(1\cdots N)}
and has a unique fixed point
z\in\mathcal{M}_{(1\cdots N)}
where
z_{k}=e^{2i\pi k/N}
. Equivalently, the fixed point is at
W\in\mathrm{Gr}_{+}(2,N)
with
W=e^{i\pi k/N}
.

Let us consider the example
N=3
in more detail. In this case we obtain a construction of the
N=3
classical phase space that is different from the one described in section
3.4
. Let us first consider the manifold
\mathrm{Gr}(2,3)
. We can parameterize it as
23
23

23

The map between
W\in\mathrm{Gr}(2,3)
and
w\in{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{1}
is one-to-one. Indeed, from
W_{1},W_{2}
we can determine
w_{1}^{2}
and
w_{2}^{2}
. Out of
2^{2}
choices of signs for
w_{1},w_{2}
, only two are consistent with
W_{3}
, and these two are related by
w\to-w\sim w
.

\displaystyle W_{1}=w_{1}^{2}-w_{2}^{2},\quad W_{2}=iw_{1}^{2}+iw_{2}^{2},\quad W_{3}=2w_{1}w_{2},

(257)

which explicitly solves the constraint
W^{2}=0
and establishes the isomorphism of
\mathrm{Gr}(2,3)
and
{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{1}
with homogeneous coordinates
w
. The Kähler potential becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}=4\log\|w\|^{2}+\text{const},

(258)

which is 8
\times
the standard
{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{1}
Kähler potential
\frac{1}{2}\log\|w\|^{2}
that defines the Fubini-Study metric. The symplectic volume of
\mathrm{Gr}(2,3)
is therefore
\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}\mathrm{Gr}(2,3)=8\pi
, and the volume of
\mathcal{M}
is
\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}\mathcal{M}=2^{-2}\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}\mathrm{Gr}(2,3)=2\pi
, in agreement with section
3.4
.

A somewhat surprising point is that the phase space
\mathcal{M}
constructed here has positive curvature, whereas the phase space in section
3
is negatively-curved. However, we do not have any reason to expect that the Hermitian or complex structures of the two spaces should agree, and by Darboux’s theorem there are no local symplectic invariants. Indeed, an explicit symplectomorphism is given by

\displaystyle z=\frac{y-\overline{y}^{3}}{\overline{y}(1-y\overline{y})},

(259)

where
y=w_{2}/w_{1}
and
z
is the coordinate used in section
3
, see figure
7
. In terms of
y
,
\mathrm{Gr}_{+}(2,3)
is the set
\{y\in\mathbb{C}|\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}y<0,\,\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}y<0,\,|y|<1\}
. The three natural boundary components of
\mathrm{Gr}_{+}(2,3)
are mapped to the points
z\in\{0,1,\infty\}
, and the interior is mapped to the acute region with
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z>0
in figure
7
. This symplectomorphism can also be checked to correctly map the classical Hamiltonians (
252
) and (
146
) in the two pictures one to another.

4.6
Relation to a classical LLL phase space

Before we proceed to the semiclassical expansion of the spectrum, it is helpful to make contact with the position space analysis of section
3
. There is of course no direct transformation between the momentum space
z_{k}
and position space
\alpha_{k}
. Nevertheless, it is reasonable to expect that the classical systems obtained in both cases should be related. In this subsection, we will show (rather schematically) how the phase space
\mathcal{M}
discussed above can be formally obtained from a classical position space picture of the kind discussed in
1.1
.

We begin by expressing the points
\alpha_{k}
on the hyperbolic disk as the stereographic projections of vectors on the future-directed hyperboloid in
\mathbb{R}^{2,1}
:

X_{k}=\frac{2}{1-|\alpha_{k}|^{2}}\left(\frac{1+|\alpha_{k}|^{2}}{2},\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\alpha_{k},\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\alpha_{k}\right),\quad(X_{k}^{0})^{2}-(X_{k}^{1})^{2}-(X_{k}^{2})^{2}=1.

(260)

If we assume that
\alpha
parameterize the phase space
\mathbb{D}^{N}
with the symplectic form on each
\mathbb{D}
given by the standard hyperbolic volume form (this is the classical phase space for LLL centres), then
\sum_{k=1}^{N}X_{k}
is the momentum map for the
\mathrm{SO}(2,1)
action. Symplectic reduction by
\mathrm{SO}(2,1)
can be performed by restricting to
\sum_{k=1}^{N}X_{k}=(J,0,0)
and modding out by the stabilizer of
(J,0,0)
.

As we discussed previously, we expect that the important limit at large
J
is when all
\alpha_{k}
are close to the unit circle. Let us therefore parameterize

\displaystyle\alpha_{k}=(1-J^{-1}p_{k}^{-1})e^{i\theta_{k}},

(261)

in terms of which we find

\displaystyle X_{k}=Jp_{k}(1,\cos\theta_{k},\sin\theta_{k})+O(1).

(262)

The condition
\sum_{k=1}^{N}X_{k}=(J,0,0)
becomes

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{N}p_{k}=1,\quad\sum_{k=1}^{N}p_{k}e^{i\theta_{k}}=0,

(263)

and the symplectic quotient is obtained by modding out shifts in
\theta_{k}
. The symplectic form induced from
\mathbb{D}^{N}
is

\displaystyle\omega_{\text{LLL}}=J\sum_{k=1}^{N}dp_{k}\wedge d\theta_{k}+O(1).

(264)

This effective large-spin phase space can be identified with
\mathcal{M}
via

\theta_{k}=\arg(z_{k}),\quad p_{k}=\frac{|z_{k}|}{|z_{1}|+\dots+|z_{N}|},\quad k=1,\dots,N.

(265)

In particular, this identifies
J^{-1}\omega_{\text{LLL}}
with the symplectic form on
\mathcal{M}
.

4.7
Leading density of states

The classical Hamiltonian in the momentum space picture is given in (
252
). Note that the factor

\displaystyle 2|z_{i}z_{j}|-z_{i}\overline{z}_{j}-\overline{z}_{i}z_{j}\geq 0

(266)

vanishes precisely when
z_{i}
and
z_{j}
are collinear. Thus for
\Delta_{\sigma}>0
, the Hamiltonian
H^{(0)}_{\text{symb}}
blows up as we approach the non-regular points on the boundaries of the regions
\mathcal{M}_{(a\cdots b)}
. This means that, as mentioned previously, the classically accessible phase space is the set
\mathcal{M}^{\text{reg}}
of regular points, which is a disjoint union of the connected components
\mathcal{M}_{(a\cdots b)}
over all cyclic orderings
(a\cdots b)
. These regions correspond to the particles arranged on a large circle in
\mathbb{D}
in various cyclic orderings.

Of course, when the particles are identical as in our toy model, we need to quotient
\mathcal{M}^{\text{reg}}
by the permutation group
S_{N}
. This is equivalent to restricting to just one region
\mathcal{M}_{(1\cdots N)}
and modding out by the residual
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
cyclic permutations that act on
\mathcal{M}_{(1\cdots N)}
.

Since each region
\mathcal{M}_{(a\cdots b)}
is isomorphic to the positive Grassmannian
\mathrm{Gr}_{+}(2,N)
, it is useful to consider
H^{(0)}_{\text{symb}}
as a function on
\mathrm{Gr}_{+}(2,N)
. It takes the form

H_{\mathrm{symb}}^{(0)}(W)=b_{\mathrm{Cas},0}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq N}\left(2\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}W_{j}\overline{W}_{i}}{||W||^{2}}\right)^{-\Delta_{\sigma}},

(267)

where, by definition,
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}W_{j}\overline{W}_{i}>0
on
\mathrm{Gr}_{+}(2,N)
. The unique minimum of the Hamiltonian is the unique
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
fixed point of the positive Grassmannian, at
W_{k}=e^{\pi ik/N}
. In terms of the variables (
265
), this corresponds to the classical configuration where the points on the circle form a regular polygon at angles
\theta_{k}=2\pi ik/N
, as anticipated in
[
20
]
. As the energy increases, the equal-energy slices move away from the minimum and toward the singular locus
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}W_{j}\overline{W}_{i}=0
for some
j>i
, where the Hamiltonian diverges.

Now that we have a good understanding of the phase space and the classical Hamiltonian, we can study the semiclassical approximation of the integrated density of states
n_{E}
, that is to say the number of states with energy below
E
. As in section
3
, let
U_{E}
be the set in
\mathcal{M}^{\text{reg}}
where
H^{(0)}_{\text{symb}}<E
. At leading order in
\hbar=J^{-1}
,
n_{E}
is the symplectic volume enclosed by
U_{E}
in units of phase space volume
(2\pi\hbar)^{N-2}
:

n_{E}^{\text{all}}=\left(\frac{J}{2\pi}\right)^{N-2}\int_{U_{E}}\frac{\omega^{\wedge(N-2)}}{(N-2)!}+O(J^{N-3}).

(268)

Here we are counting all states, i.e. not only
S_{N}
-invariant states. When we are dealing with identical particles, we have to restrict to
S_{N}
-invariant states. The analysis of this parallels the discussion in section
3.6
for
N=3
and is discussed in detail in the next subsection.

For now, we expect the leading density of
S_{N}
-invariant states to be obtained from the volume in the quotient
\mathcal{M}^{\text{reg}}/S_{N}
. Equivalently, it is enough to compute the volume in
\mathrm{Gr}_{+}(2,N)/\mathbb{Z}_{N}
. In other words, if we denote by
U_{N}^{+}
the set in
\mathrm{Gr}_{+}(2,N)
where
H^{(0)}_{\text{symb}}<E
, then the number of
S_{N}
-invariant states below energy
E
is

\displaystyle n_{E}=\frac{1}{N}\left(\frac{J}{2\pi}\right)^{N-2}\int_{U_{E}^{+}}\frac{\omega^{\wedge(N-2)}}{(N-2)!}+O(J^{N-3}).

(269)

Here, recall that
\omega=i\partial\overline{\partial}\mathcal{K}
is the symplectic form corresponding to the Kähler potential
\mathcal{K}=2\log||W||^{2}
on the Grassmannian
\mathrm{Gr}(2,N)
.

In particular, the total number of semiclassical states is given by

\displaystyle n_{\infty}=\left(\frac{J}{2\pi}\right)^{N-2}\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}\mathcal{M}}{N!}+O(J^{N-3})=\left(\frac{J}{2\pi}\right)^{N-2}\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}\mathrm{Gr}(2,N)}{2^{N-1}N!}+O(J^{N-3}).

(270)

The symplectic volume of
\mathrm{Gr}(2,N)
can be computed as follows. We previously identified
\mathrm{Gr}(2,N)
as the degree-
2
variety in
{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{N-1}
defined by
W^{2}=0
. This implies

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{Gr}(2,N)}\alpha^{\wedge(N-2)}=2,

(271)

where
\alpha
is the multiple of
\omega=2i\partial\overline{\partial}\log\|W\|^{2}
that represents the generator of
H^{2}({\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{N-1},\mathbb{Z})
, i.e.
\int_{{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{1}}\alpha=1
.
24
24

24

Here we are relying on the fact that the integral cohomology ring of
{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{N-1}
is generated by
\alpha
, and thus
\alpha^{\wedge(N-2)}
gives
1
when paired with
{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{N-2}
.
It is easy to check that
\alpha=\omega/4\pi
, and thus

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}\mathrm{Gr}(2,N)=\int_{\mathrm{Gr}(2,N)}\frac{\omega^{\wedge(N-2)}}{(N-2)!}=\frac{2(4\pi)^{N-2}}{(N-2)!}.

(272)

We conclude

\displaystyle n_{\infty}=\left(\frac{J}{2\pi}\right)^{N-2}\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}\mathrm{Gr}(2,N)}{2^{N-1}N!}+O(J^{N-3})=\frac{J^{N-2}}{N!(N-2)!}+O(J^{N-3}).

(273)

This number agrees precisely with the expansion (
47
) of
\dim\mathcal{H}_{N,J}^{\text{primary}}
to leading order at large
J
. Consequently, we expect that the fraction of states well approximated by semiclassics goes to
1
as
J\to\infty
.

At generic energies, we do not know how to compute the integral in (
269
) analytically. However, it is easy to compute it numerically using Monte-Carlo integration. For this, we generate random pairs of orthogonal unit vectors, each of which defines a plane in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, and therefore a point on
\mathrm{Gr}(2,N)
. We then use the
S_{N}\ltimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{N}
action to map these points into the positive Grassmannian. Computing the fraction of the resulting points that satisfy
H^{(0)}_{\text{symb}}<E
and multiplying by
\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}\mathrm{Gr}_{+}(2,N)
then yields an approximation to the integral in (
269
).

Figure 16
:
Energy as a function of mode number at
N=4
,
(\Delta_{\phi},\Delta_{\sigma})=(1.234,0.6734)
. Blue points are the exact eigenvalues of
J^{\Delta_{\sigma}}H_{N}
at
J=120
from numerical diagonalization, while the red curve is an interpolation of the Monte Carlo approximation of
n_{E}
in (
269
) with
10^{5}
sample points.

In figure
16
we compare the leading-order result for
n_{E}
with the exact diagonalization data at
J=120
, finding good agreement at low energies. The distinct feature in the exact data starting at
n_{E}\approx 270
seemingly corresponds to the three-body states of the form
[\phi\phi\phi^{2}]_{J}
. In figure
17
we make the same comparison, now as a function of
J
for a few sample values of
E
. We see that the numerics support the leading-order result (
269
).

Figure 17
:
Proportion of the number of semiclassical states below fixed energies
E
as a function of inverse spin. The energies
E=12,14,\dots,26,28
, covering roughly eighty percent of the total symplectic volume, are indicated by a color gradient from blue (lower
E
) to red (higher
E
). Data points correspond to the exact counting function at even spins
J\leq 120
with the parameters
(\Delta_{\phi},\Delta_{\sigma})
of figure
16
. The solid lines represent linear fits that are conditioned to reproduce the leading term (
269
) at
J=\infty
. Despite the non-smoothness in
n_{E}
, these fits show that the numerical data is consistent with (
269
).

It would be interesting to compute subleading
1/J
terms in the expansion (
269
) for
n_{E}
. However, one must keep in mind that already the subleading term in (
269
) cannot be easily determined in general systems. Indeed, the exact function
n_{E}
is not smooth. Instead, it is piecewise-constant with jumps at the energy eigenvalues
E_{k}
. In general, this “discrete” behavior can show up already at the subleading order, as is evident in the example of an isotropic harmonic oscillator with
N-2
degrees of freedom. To see this, note that the energy level
E_{k}\sim\hbar k=k/J
has
O(k^{N-3})
degeneracy, which implies that
n_{E}
is of the order
J^{N-2}
while discontinuities in
n_{E}
are of the order
J^{N-3}
.

The Gutzwiller trace formula shows that the discontinuous behavior of
n_{E}
is controlled by the periodic orbits of the classical Hamiltonian—see for instance
[
58
,
59
]
.
25
25

25

One way to interpret the (subleading) Bohr-Sommerfeld condition in systems with one degree of freedom is that periodic trajectories are easy to control, and their contribution can be resummed.
Therefore, one may hope to obtain sensible subleading corrections under the assumption that the Hamiltonian is sufficiently generic and does not have too many periodic orbits. For instance, it is possible to obtain a subleading term in the Weyl law for the Laplace-Beltrami operator under similar assumptions
[
60
]
. We are not aware of a readily-available result for the semiclassical limit of Berezin-Toeplitz quantization, but one can likely be obtained using the methods of
[
61
,
62
]
(see also
[
63
]
).

4.8
Semiclassical states under the action of
S_{N}

In this section we examine in more detail the behavior of semiclassical states under
S_{N}
.

The set
U_{E}\subset\mathcal{M}^{\text{reg}}
has in general
(N-1)!
connected components
U_{E,(a\cdots b)}=U_{E}\cap\mathcal{M}_{(a\cdots b)}
, and the semiclassical states can localize to any of these connected components. This gives a total of
(N-1)!
semiclassically-degenerate states for each energy level. As per usual, this degeneracy may be broken by instanton corrections. We have verified that, for example, the approximate six-fold degeneracy is indeed present in the exact
N=4
spectrum of all (not necessarily
S_{4}
-invariant) states.

For a given energy eigenstate, the semiclassical state localized in
\mathcal{M}_{(1\cdots N)}
transforms in some representation
\rho=e^{2\pi im/N}
of
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
. Similarly to section
3.6
, the representation of
S_{N}
that acts on the full set of
(N-1)!
nearly-degenerate energy levels is then the induced representation
\mathrm{Ind}^{S_{N}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{N}}\rho
. The multiplicity of the trivial representation of
S_{N}
in
\mathrm{Ind}^{S_{N}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{N}}\rho
is one when
\rho
is trivial and zero otherwise. This follows immediately from Frobenius reciprocity.

More generally, the
S_{N}
representation content of
\mathrm{Ind}^{S_{N}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{N}}\rho
can be determined using theorems 8.8 and 8.9 in
[
64
]
: the irreducible representation of
S_{N}
with Young diagram
\mu
appears with multiplicity equal to the number of standard Young tableaux
T
of shape
\mu
with major index
\mathrm{maj}(T)
satisfying
\mathrm{maj}(T)\equiv m\mod N
.

Recall that a standard Young tableau
T
of shape
\mu
is an assignment of integers from
1
to
N
to the cells of
\mu
such that the integers in each row and each column are strictly increasing. An integer
i
in
T
is called a descent if
i+1
appears strictly below
i
in
T
. The major index
\mathrm{maj}(T)
of
T
is the sum of all of its descents. It satisfies
\mathrm{maj}(T)\in\{0,\cdots,\binom{N}{2}\}
.

For instance, at
N=4
, it is easy to check that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ind}^{S_{4}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}e^{2\pi im/4}=\begin{cases}\,{\tiny\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt\vbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt}}\hskip 0.0pt}\,_{1}\oplus\,{\tiny\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt\vbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt}}\hskip 0.0pt}\,_{2}\oplus\,{\tiny\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt\vbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt}}\hskip 0.0pt}\,_{3}&,\quad m=0\\
\,{\tiny\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt\vbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt}}\hskip 0.0pt}\,_{3}\oplus\,{\tiny\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt\vbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt}}\hskip 0.0pt}\,_{3}&,\quad m=1\\
\,{\tiny\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt\vbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt}}\hskip 0.0pt}\,_{1}\oplus\,{\tiny\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt\vbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt}}\hskip 0.0pt}\,_{2}\oplus\,{\tiny\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt\vbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt}}\hskip 0.0pt}\,_{3}&,\quad m=2\\
\,{\tiny\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt\vbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt}}\hskip 0.0pt}\,_{3}\oplus\,{\tiny\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt\vbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt}}\hskip 0.0pt}\,_{3}&,\quad m=3\end{cases}

(274)

where we labeled each irrep of
S_{4}
by its dimension. Note that for each
m
the dimensions add up to
6
. Just like in section
3.6
, the states belonging to one irrep are protected from splitting due to instanton corrections. In our numerics for
N=4
, we indeed observed the splitting of the 6 nearly degenerate levels into
3+3
or
1+2+3
, depending on the energy level.

4.9
The lowest-lying states

In this section, we derive the analogue of the result of section
3.8
for low-lying states in the case of general
N
.

Recall that in the semiclassical limit
J\to\infty
, the low-lying states localize around the minimum of the classical Hamiltonian, near which the potential can be approximated by that of
N-2
coupled harmonic oscillators. We first discuss the spectrum of all (not necessarily
S_{N}
-invariant) states, and later discuss their
S_{N}
representation content based on the previous subsection.

It is enough to focus on the region
\mathcal{M}_{(1\cdots N)}
or, equivalently, the positive Grassmannian
\mathrm{Gr}_{+}(2,N)
. As discussed at the end of section
3.8
, the energy eigenvalues for small mode numbers
k_{1},\dots,k_{N-2}\ll J
take the form

E_{k}=H_{\text{norm}}|_{P}+J^{-1}\sum_{a=1}^{N-2}\Omega_{a}\left(k_{a}+\frac{1}{2}\right)+O\left(J^{-3/2}\right),

(275)

where
P\in\mathrm{Gr}_{+}(2,N)
is the position of the minimum of
H^{(0)}_{\text{symb}}
. The characteristic frequencies
\Omega_{a}
are defined in section
3.8
, around equation (
210
).

The first term in (
275
) can be straightforwardly computed and is given by

\displaystyle H_{\text{norm}}|_{P}

\displaystyle=b_{\mathrm{Cas},0}\,\varepsilon_{0}(N,\Delta_{\sigma})\left(1-\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{\sigma}(N\Delta_{\phi}-1)J^{-1}\right)+O(J^{-2}),

(276)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{0}(N,\Delta_{\sigma})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{2}{N}\right)^{-\Delta_{\sigma}}\sum_{q=1}^{N-1}(N-q)(\sin\tfrac{\pi q}{N})^{-\Delta_{\sigma}}.

(277)

In particular,
\varepsilon_{0}(3,\Delta_{\sigma})=3^{1+\Delta_{\sigma}/2}
and
\varepsilon_{0}(4,\Delta_{\sigma})=2^{1+3\Delta_{\sigma}/2}(2+2^{-\Delta_{\sigma}/2})
. The leading in
J
term comes from
H^{(0)}_{\text{symb}}
in (
267
) and agrees with the prediction in
[
20
]
. Recall also that the coordinates of
P
are given by

\displaystyle W_{k}=e^{\pi ik/N}.

(278)

The subleading term is obtained using (
138
) and the subleading symbol
H^{(1)}_{\text{symb}}
, which is computed following section
4.4
.

It remains to determine the frequencies
\Omega_{1},\dots,\Omega_{N-2}
by expanding the Hamiltonian to quadratic order around the minimum and separating the corresponding quadratic form into a sum of decoupled harmonic oscillators, as described in section
3.8
. This can be simplified by using the action of
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
on
\mathrm{Gr}_{+}(2,N)
. We define the coordinates
X_{0},\cdots,X_{N-1}
as

X_{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{N}W_{k}^{2}\,e^{-2\pi ikn/N},

(279)

such that the cyclic action
W_{k}\to W_{k+1}
now becomes
X_{n}\to e^{2\pi in/N}X_{n}
. Note that the condition
W^{2}=0
implies
X_{0}=0
, and the coordinates of the minimum (
278
) become
X_{1}=N
and all other
X_{n}=0
.

Based on the variables
X_{n}
, we can now define affine coordinates

\displaystyle x_{n}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{X_{n+1}}{X_{1}},\quad n=1,\cdots,N-2.

(280)

Notice that
x_{n}
has charge
n
under
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
and the minimum is at
x_{n}=0
. In terms of the
x_{n}
, the symplectic form at
P
is

\displaystyle\omega|_{P}=i\sum_{n=1}^{N-2}dx_{n}\wedge d\overline{x}_{n}.

(281)

Recall that in section
3.8
we defined the quadratic form
\Omega(x)
as

\displaystyle H^{(0)}_{\text{symb}}=H^{(0)}_{\text{symb}}|_{P}+\Omega(x)+O(x^{3}).

(282)

Using
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
symmetry, we find that the general form of
\Omega(x)
is given by

\displaystyle\Omega(x)=\sum_{n=1}^{N}A_{n}x_{n}\overline{x}_{n}+\sum_{n=2}^{\lfloor N/2\rfloor}\left(B_{n}x_{n}x_{N-n}+\overline{B}_{n}x_{n}x_{N-n}\right).

(283)

We therefore deduce that
\Omega(x)
separates into three types of blocks.

1.

A_{1}x_{1}\overline{x}_{1}
, which gives the frequency
\Omega_{1}=A_{1}
.

2.

A_{n}x_{n}\overline{x}_{n}+A_{N-n}x_{N-n}\overline{x}_{N-n}+B_{n}x_{n}x_{N-n}+\overline{B}_{n}\overline{x}_{n}\overline{x}_{N-n}
, for
1<n<N/2
. In this case we get two frequencies which we can assign as

\displaystyle\Omega_{n}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\left(\frac{A_{n}+A_{N-n}}{2}\right)^{2}-|B_{n}|^{2}}+\frac{A_{n}-A_{N-n}}{2},

(284)

\displaystyle\Omega_{N-n}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\left(\frac{A_{n}+A_{N-n}}{2}\right)^{2}-|B_{n}|^{2}}+\frac{A_{N-n}-A_{n}}{2}.

(285)

3.

A_{N/2}x_{N/2}\overline{x}_{N/2}+B_{N/2}x_{N/2}^{2}+\overline{B}_{N/2}\overline{x}_{N/2}^{2}
when
N
is even. In this case the frequency is

\displaystyle\Omega_{N/2}=\sqrt{A_{N/2}^{2}-4|B_{N/2}|^{2}}.

(286)

The frequencies for each type of block have been determined following the discussion in section
3.8
. We note that the separated variables
u_{a}
,
a=1,\cdots,N-2
can be constructed so that the
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
charges are the same as those of
x_{a}
. This is possible because both the symplectic form and the classical Hamiltonian are
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
-invariant.

For example, at
N=3
we find

\displaystyle\frac{\Omega_{1}}{b_{\mathrm{Cas},0}\,\varepsilon_{0}(N,\Delta_{\sigma})}=\Delta_{\sigma}(\Delta_{\sigma}+2)/2,

(287)

in agreement with section
3.8
. For
N=4
we find

\displaystyle\frac{\Omega_{1}}{b_{\mathrm{Cas},0}\,\varepsilon_{0}(4,\Delta_{\sigma})}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\sigma}\left(\frac{2\Delta_{\sigma}}{2+2^{-\Delta_{\sigma}/2}}+1\right),

(288)

\displaystyle\frac{\Omega_{2}}{b_{\mathrm{Cas},0}\,\varepsilon_{0}(4,\Delta_{\sigma})}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\Delta_{\sigma}}{2+2^{-\Delta_{\sigma}/2}}\sqrt{(1+\Delta_{\sigma}/2)(2+2^{-\Delta_{\sigma}/2}(1+\Delta_{\sigma}))}.

(289)

Though we do not have an analytic formula for all frequencies for general
N
, the above discussion can be straightforwardly applied to compute
\Omega_{1},\dots,\Omega_{N-2}
at any fixed value of
(N,\Delta_{\sigma})
.

Let us now determine the
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
charge of the states in terms of the mode numbers
k_{1},\dots,k_{N-2}
. Similarly to section
3.6
, one can check that the
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
charge gets a contribution
J
from the line bundle
L
. We expect the eigenfunctions of the harmonic oscillator to have the multiplicative structure

\displaystyle\psi\sim u_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots u_{N-2}^{k_{N-2}}.

(290)

Of course, the coordinates
u_{1}
are not holomorphic functions on
\mathrm{Gr}_{+}(2,N)
and so this cannot be understood literally. Instead, this product should be viewed as an operator acting on the ground state wavefunction. Nevertheless, we can still read off the charge due to the excitations and conclude that the total
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
charge is

\displaystyle J+\sum_{a=1}^{N-2}a\,k_{a}\mod N.

(291)

We now recall that each state on
\mathrm{Gr}_{+}(2,N)
gives rise to
(N-1)!
approximately degenerate states on
\mathcal{M}
. The representation of
S_{N}
on these states has been computed in section
4.8
in terms of the
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
charge. In particular, if we are interested in
S_{N}
-invariant states, we need to impose

\displaystyle J+\sum_{a=1}^{N-2}a\,k_{a}=0\mod N.

(292)

Figure 18
:
The ratio
(E_{k}-E_{0}^{\mathrm{HO}})/E_{0}^{\mathrm{HO}}
as a function of spin for
(\Delta_{\phi},\Delta_{\sigma})=(1.234,1.386)
. The blue dots are the values from exact diagonalization, while the horizontal lines correspond to the energies
E_{k}^{\text{HO}}
. The expressions for
\nu_{a}:=\Omega_{a}/\left(b_{\mathrm{Cas},0}\,\varepsilon_{0}(4,\Delta_{\sigma})\right)
are given by (
288
),(
289
). The vertical grid lines are at
J\equiv 0\mod 4

Figure 19
:
Same as figure
18
, but as function of
1/J
. The red curves represent fits of the numerical data points at
J\geq 80
by a quadratic function
a+b/J+c/J^{2}
.

Let
E^{\text{HO}}_{k}
be the eigenstate energies in the harmonic oscillator approximation at
O(J^{-1})
,

\displaystyle E_{k}^{\text{HO}}\equiv H^{(0)}_{\text{symb}}|_{P}+J^{-1}H^{(1)}_{\text{norm}}|_{P}+J^{-1}\sum_{a=1}^{N-2}\Omega_{a}\left(k_{a}+\frac{1}{2}\right),

(293)

with the selection rule (
292
) understood. In figures
18
and
19
we show a comparison of the energies
E_{k}^{\text{HO}}
for
N=4
with the exact numerical spectrum at
(\Delta_{\phi},\Delta_{\sigma})=(1.234,1.386)
. In these figures, we plot
(E_{k}-E_{0}^{\text{HO}})/E_{0}^{\text{HO}}
. These ratios are
J
-independent in the harmonic oscillator approximation and appear as horizontal lines. They arrange into groups of
1,2,3,\cdots
due to the breaking of the degeneracy present for
\Omega_{1}=\Omega_{2}
(in our example, the difference between
\Omega_{1}
and
\Omega_{2}
is
\approx 10\%
). The fits in figure
19
indicate a clear convergence of the numerics towards
E_{k}^{\text{HO}}
with a correction that scales like
1/J^{2}
, as expected
26
26

26

Recall that the
O(J^{-3/2})
correction in (
275
) can only exist if there is a degeneracy at leading order, as explained in
(
49
, Sec. 5.1)
.
. Figure
18
can be examined to confirm the selection rule (
292
); for instance, the
k_{1}=k_{2}=0
states only exist at
J\equiv 0\mod 4
.

5
Discussion

In this paper we have analyzed the large-
J
limit of the leading-twist
N
-particle states in
\mathrm{AdS}
. We found that the quantum-mechanical problem that describes them becomes semiclassical for the majority of states, with
\hbar=1/J
. We have developed methods for studying the semiclassical spectrum, relying on existing results from Berezin-Toeplitz quantization. We found that all our analytical results are in good agreement with the exact numerics.

There are many questions that we have not addressed in this work. It will be important to understand more general interactions between the individual particles. For instance, in a multi-twist state with both
\phi^{2}
and
\phi
constituents, an exchange of
\phi
can swap the positions of
\phi^{2}
and
\phi
. If our methods can be applied to general CFTs, this would be important for the
[\sigma\epsilon\epsilon]_{J}
triple-twist operators in 3d Ising CFT, where
\epsilon\sim\sigma^{2}
. A related perturbative problem is
[\phi\phi\phi\phi]_{J}
quadruple-twist states in Wilson-Fisher theory at one loop, where the only non-zero anomalous dimensions correspond to states of the form
[\phi^{2}\phi\phi]_{J}
.

Another interesting problem is to understand if, and under which conditions, one can derive subleading corrections to the density of states (
269
) for
N\geq 4
. Although we have not explored this question with any degree of rigour, this might be feasible to do, at least formally, using the path integral methods of
[
61
,
62
]
.

Similarly, it would be interesting to derive systematic corrections to the harmonic oscillator approximation discussed in sections
3.8
and
4.9
. One can see from figure
19
that the leading term does not work very well, even at
J=120
, but that the next correction would likely remedy this.

We have focused on the limit where
J
is the largest parameter in the problem. There are several other natural limits to explore. Perhaps the most intriguing one is the double-scaling limit
N^{2}\sim J\gg 1
. Recall from section
1.1
that the lowest-twist states can be interpreted as the lowest Landau level (LLL) states on the hyperbolic disk. The ratio
N^{2}/(2J)
can be interpreted as the filling fraction of the LLL. The physics of interacting fermions in the LLL in flat space at finite filling fraction is well studied in the field of fractional quantum Hall effect (FQHE)
[
65
,
66
]
.
27
27

27

In fact, early in this project we drew a lot of intuition from the Laughlin state which is often discussed in FQHE context.
It seems clear that in principle the FQHE setup can be obtained in an appropriate limit of our problem (swapping scalars for fermions and taking large-
\mathrm{AdS}
radius limit). It is therefore interesting to ask whether some remnant of FQHE physics can be observed in the large-spin multi-twist spectra of various CFTs. Interpreting
N
as a
U(1)
charge
Q
, we note that the regime
J\sim Q^{2}
is between the Regge phase and the giant vortex phase studied in
[
67
]
. One issue with this limit is that the inter-particle distances do not grow and we do not expect two-body potentials to dominate, or the interactions to be universal. However, it is possible that some universal features of FQHE can still be observed. At the very least, one can try engineering a FQHE setup in holographic theories such as planar
\mathcal{N}=4
Super Yang-Mills.

Another interesting limit is to consider fixed
N
with large
\Delta_{\phi}\sim J
. In this case the semiclassical limit is given by the classical LLL particles on the hyperbolic disk, and somewhat more general dynamics are allowed. Independently of spin, large scaling dimensions correspond to large
\mathrm{AdS}
radii, and the physics of the classical
N
-body problem in flat space can be recovered. Can anything interesting be said about this problem from this point of view? In this context, note that the relation between near-threshold and large-spin expansions of flat space amplitudes was studied in
[
68
]
from the Froissart-Gribov formula.

Finally, perhaps the most important open question is to derive the quantum-mechanical problems of the kind considered in this work in general CFTs and without appealing to the
\mathrm{AdS}
picture. One approach is to try to formally derive the interaction Hamiltonian for
N=3
from crossing equations for six-point functions. Besides the technical complexity, one aspect of this approach that differs from
N=2
is that large inter-particle separation (and hence universality of interactions) is guaranteed by the large
J
limit at
N=2
, irrespective of the state. For
N=3
, only a subclass of states has large inter-particle separations, however large this subclass is. Therefore, such a derivation would have to rely on some assumption about the states, and we hope that our analysis will be useful in identifying their key features.

An alternative approach would be to construct (perhaps using ideas from
[
69
]
) an effective large-spin theory in which the appropriate Wilson coefficients can be matched to various limits of CFT correlation functions. Based on the intuition in section
1.1
, we expect that the appropriate theory should be phrased in terms of fields that second-quantize the LLL. We leave these and other questions for future study.

Appendix A
Conformal algebra and embedding space conventions

In Euclidean signature,
\mathrm{AdS}_{d+1}
can be identified with the locus of solutions to the equation
X^{2}=-1
, where
X\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1,1}
. We label different components of
X
by
X^{A}
with
A=-1,0_{E},1,\cdots,d
, while the metric is taken to be
\eta_{AB}=\mathrm{diag}\{-1,+1,\cdots,+1\}
. We use the index
0_{E}
to stress that this is a Euclidean component. Using
\mathbf{e}_{A}
to denote the standard basis vectors of
\mathbb{R}^{d+1,1}
, we define the generators
L_{AB}
of
\mathfrak{so}(d+1,1)
by

\displaystyle L_{AB}\mathbf{e}_{C}=\eta_{BC}\mathbf{e}_{A}-\eta_{AC}\mathbf{e}_{B}.

(294)

For fixed
A,B
, the function
X\mapsto L_{AB}X
defines a vector field in
\mathbb{R}^{d+1,1}
that is tangent to
\mathrm{AdS}_{d+1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d+1,1}
. By restriction, it defines a vector field on
\mathrm{AdS}_{d+1}
that we denote by
\mathcal{L}_{AB}
. The action of
L_{AB}
on a local operator in
\mathrm{AdS}_{d+1}
then takes the simple form

\displaystyle[L_{AB},{\mathcal{O}}(x)]=(\mathcal{L}_{AB}{\mathcal{O}})(x).

(295)

The generators
L_{AB}
can be identified with the standard conformal generators as (
\mu,\nu\in\{1,\cdots,d\}
)

\displaystyle L_{0_{E},-1}=D,\quad L_{0_{E},\mu}=-\frac{P_{\mu}+K_{\mu}}{2},\quad L_{-1,\mu}=\frac{P_{\mu}-K_{\mu}}{2},\quad L_{\mu\nu}=M_{\mu\nu}.

(296)

The Wick rotation to Lorentzian signature is achieved by setting
X^{0}=-iX^{0_{E}}
,
X_{0}=iX^{0_{E}}
and similarly for all other tensors. In particular,
L_{0\mu}=iL_{0_{E},\mu}
. The Wick-rotated metric in the resulting
\mathbb{R}^{d,2}
becomes
\mathrm{diag}\{-1,-1,+1,\cdots,+1\}
. In terms of the global coordinates,
\mathrm{AdS}_{d,1}
is embedded as

\displaystyle X^{-1}

\displaystyle=\cos t\cosh\rho,

(297)

\displaystyle X^{0}

\displaystyle=-\sin t\cosh\rho,

(298)

\displaystyle X^{\mu}

\displaystyle=n^{\mu}\sinh\rho,

(299)

where
\mu=1,\cdots,d
and
n
is a unit vector in
S^{d-1}
,
n\cdot n=1
. Note that in terms of the Euclidean time
t_{E}=it
this becomes

\displaystyle X^{-1}

\displaystyle=\cosh t_{E}\cosh\rho,

(300)

\displaystyle X^{0_{E}}

\displaystyle=-\sinh t_{E}\cosh\rho,

(301)

\displaystyle X^{\mu}

\displaystyle=n^{\mu}\sinh\rho.

(302)

It is easy to check that in these coordinates the vector field
\mathcal{D}
corresponding to
D
is
-i\partial_{t}
. In other words,
[D,{\mathcal{O}}(x)]=-i\partial_{t}{\mathcal{O}}(x)
and thus
D
plays the role of the Hamiltonian for global time translations on
\mathbb{R}\times S^{d-1}
.

The Euclidean boundary Poincaré patch can be identified using
X^{\pm}=X^{-1}\pm X^{0_{E}}
and rescaling to
X^{+}=1
. Then
X^{\mu}=x^{\mu}
with
x^{\mu}
(
\mu=1,\cdots,d
) being the standard coordinates on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. In this Poincaré patch, the generators
D,P_{\mu},K_{\mu},M_{\mu\nu}
become the standard conformal transformations. Furthermore, it is easy to check that the unit sphere
|x|=1
is embedded as
(X^{+},X^{-},X^{\mu})=(1,1,x^{\mu})
, which coincides with the boundary (
\rho\to\infty
) limit of the
t=t_{E}=0
slice of the
\mathrm{AdS}
space.

Appendix B
Derivation of the effective pair potential

In this appendix, we derive the expansion (
62
) of
U_{2}(s)
, starting from its integral expression
U_{2}(|\alpha_{1}|^{2}/(1-|\alpha_{1}|^{2}))=F(\alpha_{1},0)
, where
F(\alpha_{1},0)
is given by (
60
). We will be working in
(t,\alpha,q)
coordinates of AdS
d+1
, where
t
is the global time and
(\alpha,q)\in\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-2}
are related to global coordinates by

\alpha=e^{i(\varphi-t)}\cos\theta\tanh\rho,\quad q^{i}=\sinh\rho\,n^{i},\,\,\,i=3,\dots,d.

(303)

In these coordinates,
F
reduces to an integral over
q_{1}
:

\displaystyle F(\alpha_{1},0)=\frac{\pi^{2}\lambda^{2}C_{\Delta_{\phi},d}^{2}}{(\Delta_{\phi}-1)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d-2}}d^{d-2}q_{1}\,(1+q_{1}^{2})^{1-\Delta_{\phi}}I(0,\alpha_{1},q_{1}).

(304)

By its definition (
61
), the function
I(x_{1})
is the unique solution to the following scalar AdS wave equation with source:

(\nabla^{2}-\Delta_{\sigma}(\Delta_{\sigma}-d))I(t,\alpha,q)=\frac{\delta^{2}(\alpha)}{(1+q^{2})^{\Delta_{\phi}}}.

(305)

The derivation proceeds in three steps: first, we solve the homogeneous wave equation away from
\alpha=0
with a separation of variables ansatz. Second, we fix all undetermined constants in the separated solution by requiring that it reproduce the source term at
\alpha=0
. Having determined its exact form, we finally integrate
I(0,\alpha_{1},q_{1})
over
q_{1}
in (
304
) to obtain the expansion in lightcone blocks
k_{\Delta_{\sigma}+2n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

B.1
Separation of variables for AdS Klein-Gordon equation

Since the source in (
305
) is invariant under translations of
t
, phase rotations of
\alpha
and
SO(d-2)
rotations of
q^{a}
, we can restrict the dependence of
I
to
I(t,\alpha,q)\equiv I_{0}(s,|q|)
, where
s=s_{12}(\alpha,0)=|\alpha|^{2}(1-|\alpha|^{2})^{-1}
. In this case, the action of the AdS Laplacian reduces to

\nabla^{2}I_{0}(s,|q|)=\mathcal{D}_{|q|}I(s,|q|)+4(1+|q|^{2})^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{s}I(s,|q|),

(306)

where the second-order, one-variable differential operators
\mathcal{D}_{|q|}
,
\mathcal{D}_{s}
are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{s}(s,\partial_{s})=s(1+s)\partial_{s}^{2}+(1+2s)\partial_{s},

(307)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{|q|}(q,\partial_{q})=(1+q^{2})\partial_{q}^{2}+\left((d+1)q+(d-3)q^{-1}\right)\partial_{q}.

(308)

Consequently, the wave equation
(1+q^{2})(\nabla^{2}-\Delta_{\sigma}(\Delta_{\sigma}-d))I(s,|q|)=0
away from the source at
\alpha=0
separates into a sum of two one-variable differential equations in
s
and
|q|
, respectively. To motivate our ansatz for the full solution, we first look at the particular case of
d=2
.

Solution in two dimensions.

In
d=2
, where there are no transverse directions
q\equiv 0
, the curves
\alpha=\mathrm{const}
are geodesics in AdS
3
. In this case, the function
I(x)
coincides with the function
\varphi^{12}_{\Delta_{\sigma}}
studied in
(
70
, section 3.1)
. After translating their coordinates to ours, the solution is then given by

I_{0}(s)=I_{\Delta_{\sigma},2}(s):=\frac{\Gamma(\Delta_{\sigma}/2)^{2}}{4\pi\Gamma(\Delta_{\sigma})}k_{\Delta_{\sigma}}(1/(s+1)),\quad k_{2h}(z):=z^{h}{}_{2}F_{1}(h,h;2h;z).

(309)

It is easy to check that
4\mathcal{D}_{s}k_{\Delta_{\sigma}}=\Delta_{\sigma}(\Delta_{\sigma}-2)k_{\Delta_{\sigma}}
away from
s=0
, which solves the homogeneous wave equation. To check that the multiplicative prefactor correctly accounts for the source
\delta^{2}(\alpha)
, we can expand the wave equation near
\alpha=0
, where
s=|\alpha|^{2}+O(|\alpha|^{4})=0
. There, the reduced Laplacian
4\mathcal{D}_{s}
tends to the radial part of the Laplacian on the complex
\alpha
-plane in polar coordinates:

4\mathcal{D}_{s}(\epsilon s,\epsilon^{-1}\partial_{s})=4\epsilon^{-1}\partial_{s}s\partial_{s}+O(1)=\epsilon^{-1}|\alpha|^{-1}\partial_{|\alpha|}|\alpha|\partial_{|\alpha|}+O(1).

(310)

At the same time, it follows from
2\pi I_{\Delta_{\sigma},2}(s)=\log(s)+O(s^{0})
that
I_{0}
tends to the rotation-symmetric Green’s function of the Laplacian (
310
) near
|\alpha|=0
. As a result,
I_{\Delta_{\sigma},2}
is the unique solution to the AdS
3
Klein-Gordon equation with geodesic source:

(\nabla^{2}-\Delta_{\sigma}(\Delta_{\sigma}-2))I_{\Delta_{\sigma},2}(|\alpha|^{2}(1-|\alpha|^{2})^{-1})=\delta^{2}(\alpha).

(311)

Solution in higher dimension.

In dimensions
d>2
, we make the separation of variables ansatz

I_{0}(s,|q|)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}c_{n}\,Q_{n}(|q|)\,I_{\Delta_{\sigma}+2n,2}(s),

(312)

where
Q_{n}\,k_{\Delta_{\sigma}+2n}
at
s\neq 0
solves the wave equation for any
n
. Since
4\mathcal{D}_{s}I_{\Delta_{\sigma}+2n,2}=(\Delta_{\sigma}+2n)(\Delta_{\sigma}+2n-2)I_{\Delta_{\sigma}+2n,2}
, this is true if and only if
Q_{n}
solves the following first-order differential equation:

\left(\mathcal{D}_{q}(|q|,\partial_{|q|})+\frac{(\Delta_{\sigma}+2n)(\Delta_{\sigma}+2n-2)}{1+|q|^{2}}-\Delta_{\sigma}(\Delta_{\sigma}-d)\right)Q_{n}(|q|)=0.

(313)

After the change of variables and parameter redefinition

x:=\frac{|q|^{2}-1}{|q|^{2}+1},\quad Q_{n}(|q|)=(1-x)^{\Delta_{\sigma}/2}P_{n}(x),\quad(a,b):=\left(\Delta_{\sigma}-\frac{d}{2},\frac{d}{2}-2\right),

(314)

it is straightforward to check that (
313
) reduces to the Jacobi differential equation with parameters
(n,a,b)
:

(1-x^{2})P_{n}^{\prime\prime}(x)+(b-a-(a+b+2)x)P_{n}^{\prime}(x)-n(n+a+b+1)P_{n}(x)=0.

(315)

There are two independent solutions to this differential equation: the Jacobi polynomials
P_{n}^{(a,b)}
, and another solution with asymptotic behavior
P_{n}(x)\sim(1-x)^{(d-2\Delta_{\sigma})/2}
as
x\rightarrow 1
, which translates to
Q_{n}(|q|)\sim|q|^{\Delta_{\sigma}-d}
as
|q|\rightarrow\infty
. When inserting this latter solution into (
304
), the integral would diverge from the large
|q|
region when
\Delta_{\sigma}
is sufficiently large. On the other hand, the Jacobi polynomials correspond to solutions that decay like
Q_{n}(|q|)\sim|q|^{-\Delta_{\sigma}}
as
|q|\rightarrow\infty
, and therefore provide the correct solutions to the separation of variables ansatz (
312
). As a result, the function
I
can be expressed as

I(t,\alpha,q)=\left(\frac{1-x}{2}\right)^{\Delta_{\sigma}/2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}c_{n}P_{n}^{(a,b)}(x)k_{\Delta_{\sigma}+2n}(1/(s+1)),

(316)

where
s=|\alpha|^{2}(1-|\alpha|^{2})^{-1}
,
P_{n}^{(a,b)}
are the Jacobi polynomials, and
(x,a,b)
are given by (
314
).

B.2
Solving for the source

We would now like to fix the coefficients
c_{n}
in (
316
) via the input of the source in (
305
). The first step is very similar to the
d=2
case: expanding
\nabla^{2}-\Delta_{\sigma}(\Delta_{\sigma}-d)
near
\alpha=0
using (
306
) and (
310
), we again obtain the Laplacian of the complex
\alpha
-plane at leading order, albeit multiplied by a factor of
(1+q^{2})^{-1}=(1-x)/2
. After inserting the solution (
316
) and using the fact that
I_{\Delta_{\sigma}+2n,2}\sim\log|\alpha|^{2}/(2\pi)
near
\alpha=0
, the wave equation (
305
) in the separation of variables ansatz then reduces to

\left(\frac{1-x}{2}\right)^{\Delta_{\sigma}/2+1}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}c_{n}\,P_{n}^{(a,b)}(x)\,\delta^{2}(\alpha)=\left(\frac{1-x}{2}\right)^{\Delta_{\phi}}\delta^{2}(\alpha).

(317)

After factoring out
\delta^{2}(\alpha)
and dividing by the power of
(1-x)/2
on the left-hand side, we can understand this equation as defining the vector with coefficients
c_{n}
in the infinite-dimensional vector space spanned by the Jacobi polynomials
P_{n}^{(a,b)}
. This vector space is equipped with a scalar product for which the Jacobi polynomials form an orthogonal basis:

\langle f,g\rangle:=\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{dx}{2}\left(\frac{1-x}{2}\right)^{a}\left(\frac{1+x}{2}\right)^{b}f(x)g(x).

(318)

We can therefore determine the coefficients
c_{n}
by projecting with respect to this scalar product, leading to

c_{n}\langle P_{n}^{(a,b)},P_{n}^{(a,b)}\rangle=\left\langle P_{n}^{(a,b)},\left((1-x)/2\right)^{\Delta_{\phi}-\Delta_{\sigma}/2-1}\right\rangle.

(319)

These scalar products that determine
c_{n}
are given explicitly by equations 1 and 7 of
(
71
, §7.391)
in Gradshteyn & Ryzhik.

B.3
Expansion into lightcone blocks

Now that we have an explicit solution (
316
) for
I(0,\alpha_{1},q_{1})
, with coefficients
c_{n}
given by (
319
), we can determine
F(\alpha_{1},0)=U_{2}(|\alpha_{1}|^{2}(1-|\alpha_{1}|^{2})^{-1})
by plugging the solution into (
304
). Assuming the integral over
q\in\mathbb{R}^{d-2}
can be swapped with the sum over
n
, we can then identify the coefficients
b_{n}
of the expansion (
66
) as

b_{n}=c_{n}\frac{\pi^{2}\lambda^{2}C_{\Delta_{\phi},d}^{2}}{(\Delta_{\phi}-1)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d-2}}d^{d-2}q\,(1+q^{2})^{1-\Delta_{\phi}-\Delta_{\sigma}/2}P_{n}^{(a,b)}(x(|q|)).

(320)

To compute this integral, we take spherical coordinates for
\mathbb{R}^{d-2}
, integrate over the
S^{d-3}
, and change of variables from
|q|
to
x
. Using the formula

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d-2}}\frac{d^{d-2}q}{(1+q^{2})^{\Delta_{\sigma}}}F(x(|q|))=\frac{\pi^{d/2-1}}{\Gamma(d/2-1)}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{dx}{2}\left(\frac{1-x}{2}\right)^{\Delta_{\sigma}-d/2}\left(\frac{1+x}{2}\right)^{d/2-2}F(x),

(321)

as well as the expression (
319
) for the coefficients
c_{n}
, we find

b_{n}=\frac{\pi^{2}\lambda^{2}C_{\Delta_{\phi},d}^{2}}{(\Delta_{\phi}-1)^{2}}\frac{\pi^{d/2-1}}{\Gamma(d/2-1)}\frac{\left\langle P_{n}^{(a,b)},\left((1-x)/2\right)^{\Delta_{\phi}-\Delta_{\sigma}/2-1}\right\rangle^{2}}{\langle P_{n}^{(a,b)},P_{n}^{(a,b)}\rangle},

(322)

where
\langle f,g\rangle
is defined in (
318
).

Appendix C
Matching the two-body binding energy with the Lorentzian inversion formula

This appendix details the explicit proof that the two-body binding energy
\gamma_{2,J}
in (
79
) is equal to the anomalous dimension
\gamma_{LIF}
in (
85
). The latter gives the contribution of a
\sigma
exchange in the
t,u
channels of
\langle\phi\phi\overline{\phi\phi}\rangle
to the anomalous dimension of the double-twist operator
[\phi\phi]_{0,J}
, as predicted from the Lorentzian inversion formula.

First, in section
C.1
, we prove the formula (
81
) that recasts the two-body binding energy into the form (
83
). The latter can then be rewritten as a ratio of two integrals:

\gamma_{2,J}=\frac{\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\,s^{-\Delta_{\phi}}k_{2\Delta_{\phi}+2J}\left(\frac{s}{s+1}\right)U_{2}(s)}{\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\,s^{-\Delta_{\phi}}k_{2\Delta_{\phi}+2J}\left(\frac{s}{s+1}\right)}=\frac{\int_{0}^{1}\frac{dz}{z^{2}}\left(\frac{z}{1-z}\right)^{2-\Delta_{\phi}}k_{2\Delta_{\phi}+2J}(z)U_{2}\left(\frac{z}{1-z}\right)}{\int_{0}^{1}\frac{dz}{z^{2}}\left(\frac{z}{1-z}\right)^{2-\Delta_{\phi}}k_{2\Delta_{\phi}+2J}(z)},

(323)

where we used
{}_{2}F_{1}(h,h;2h,-s)=(1+s)^{-h}{}_{2}F_{1}(h,h;2h;s/(s+1))
in the first equality and changed variables to
s=z/(1-z)\leftrightarrow z=s/(s+1)
in the last equality. In this form, we see some first similarities with
\gamma_{LIF}
, except that
s^{\Delta_{\phi}}k_{\Delta_{\sigma}}^{(d)}(1-z)
and its multiplicative prefactor are replaced with
s^{2-\Delta_{\phi}}U_{2}(s)
in the numerator, while
s^{\Delta_{\phi}}
is replaced with
s^{2-\Delta_{\phi}}
in the denominator. The multiplicative prefactor is proportional to the square of the OPE coefficient
C_{\phi\phi\sigma}
, which is given by (
87
) to leading order in
\lambda
, as we review in section
C.2
. Since
U_{2}(s)
is defined by an expansion into lightcone blocks
k_{\Delta_{\sigma}+2n}(1-z)
in (
62
), we determine the same expansion for
k_{\Delta_{\sigma}}^{(d)}(1-z)
in section
C.3
. The result is

k_{\Delta_{\sigma}}^{(d)}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{B}_{n}\,k_{\Delta_{\sigma}+2n},\quad\mathcal{B}_{n}=\frac{(d/2-1)_{n}}{n!(\Delta_{\sigma}-d/2+1)_{n}}\frac{(\Delta_{\sigma}/2)_{n}^{2}}{(\Delta_{\sigma}+n-1)_{n}},

(324)

where
(a)_{k}:=\Gamma(a+k)/\Gamma(a)
. After swapping the integrals over
z
with the sum over lightcone blocks, the equality
\gamma_{2,J}=\gamma_{LIF}
then reduces to

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}b_{n}\frac{\Omega_{\Delta_{\phi}+J,\Delta_{\sigma}/2,2-\Delta_{\phi}}}{\Omega_{\Delta_{\phi}+J,0,2-\Delta_{\phi}}}=-2\frac{C_{\phi\phi\sigma}^{2}\Gamma(\Delta_{\sigma})}{\Gamma(\Delta_{\sigma}/2)^{2}}\frac{\sin^{2}\pi(\Delta_{\phi}-\Delta_{\sigma}/2)}{\sin^{2}\pi\Delta_{\phi}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{B}_{n}\frac{\Omega_{\Delta_{\phi}+J,\Delta_{\sigma}/2,\Delta_{\phi}}}{\Omega_{\Delta_{\phi}+J,0,\Delta_{\phi}}},

(325)

where

\Omega_{h,h^{\prime},p}=\Omega_{h^{\prime},h,2-p}=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{dz}{z^{2}}\left(\frac{z}{1-z}\right)^{p}k_{2h}(z)k_{2h^{\prime}}(1-z).

(326)

In fact, the equality (
325
) holds order-by-order in each summand
n=0,1,2,\dots
if the hypergeometric integral (
326
) satisfies the identity

\frac{\Omega_{h_{1},h_{2},p}}{\Gamma(h_{1}+p-1)^{2}\Gamma(h_{2}+1-p)^{2}}=\frac{\Omega_{h_{1},h_{2},2-p}}{\Gamma(h_{1}+1-p)^{2}\Gamma(h_{2}+p-1)^{2}}.

(327)

We prove this identity in section
C.4
.

C.1
Proof of (
81
)

To prove this formula, it will be useful to rewrite the integral in terms of the future-directed unit hyperboloid in Minkowski space:

{\mathbb{H}}^{+}_{:}=\{X\in\mathbb{R}^{2,1}\,|\,X^{2}=-1,\,X^{t}>0\},\quad X^{2}:=-(X^{t})^{2}+(X^{x})^{2}+(X^{y})^{2}.

(328)

The latter is isomorphic to the hyperbolic disk via stereographic projection:

X(\alpha_{k}):=X_{k}=\frac{2}{1-|\alpha_{k}|^{2}}\left(\frac{1+|\alpha_{k}|^{2}}{2},\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\alpha_{k},\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\alpha_{k}\right).

(329)

The Lorentz-invariant measure on the hyperboloid must agree with the
\mathfrak{so}(2,1)
-invariant measure
d^{2}\alpha/(1-|\alpha|^{2})^{2}
on the disk up to a multiplicative constant, which is easily checked to be

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2,1}}\widetilde{dX}_{k}:=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2,1}}d^{3}X\,\delta(X^{2}+1)\,\theta(X^{0})=\int_{\mathbb{D}}\frac{d^{2}\alpha}{(1-|\alpha|^{2})^{2}}.

(330)

Moreover, scalar products on
{\mathbb{H}}^{+}
are related to two-point invariants on the disk as follows:

\displaystyle C(X_{1},X_{2}):=\frac{1-X_{1}\cdot X_{2}}{2}=\frac{(1-\alpha_{1}\overline{\alpha}_{2})(1-\overline{\alpha}_{1}\alpha_{2})}{(1-\alpha_{1}\overline{\alpha}_{1})(1-\alpha_{2}\overline{\alpha}_{2})}:=\cosh^{2}\mathbf{d}_{12}/2,

(331)

\displaystyle S(X_{1},X_{2}):=-\frac{1+X_{1}\cdot X_{2}}{2}=\frac{(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})(\overline{\alpha}_{1}-\overline{\alpha}_{2})}{(1-\alpha_{1}\overline{\alpha}_{1})(1-\alpha_{2}\overline{\alpha}_{2})}:=\sinh^{2}\mathbf{d}_{12}/2,

(332)

where
\mathbf{d}_{12}
is the hyperbolic distance on the disk. After introducing the point
\alpha_{0}:=0
on the disk and the vector
X(0):=X_{0}=(1,0,0)
on the hyperboloid, we can recast the left-hand-side of (
81
) into the following form:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{D}^{2}}\prod_{k=1}^{2}d^{2}\alpha_{k}(1-\alpha_{k}\overline{\alpha}_{k})^{\Delta_{k}-2}\delta(s-s_{12})

\displaystyle=\int_{({\mathbb{H}}^{+})^{2}}\prod_{k=2}^{2}\widetilde{dX}_{k}\,C(X_{k},X_{0})^{-\Delta_{k}}\,\delta(s-S(X_{1},X_{2}))

\displaystyle=:G(s;X_{0}).

(333)

From Lorentz invariance of the integral, it is easy to check that
G(s;X_{0})
is invariant under any Lorentz transformation
X_{0}\mapsto\Lambda X_{0}
,
\Lambda\in SO(1,2)
. Since
{\mathbb{H}}^{+}
is homogeneous for
SO(1,2)
, Lorentz invariance implies that
Y\mapsto G(s;Y)
is a constant function. After integrating against
\widetilde{dY}
, we obtain
28
28

28

Here we define
\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}{\mathbb{H}}^{+}:=\int\widetilde{dX}
.

\displaystyle G(s;X_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}({\mathbb{H}}^{+})}\int_{({\mathbb{H}}^{+})^{2}}\prod_{k=1}^{2}\widetilde{dX}_{k}\,\delta(s-S(X_{1},X_{2}))\int_{{\mathbb{H}}^{+}}\widetilde{dY}\prod_{k=1}^{2}C(X_{k},Y)^{-\Delta_{k}}

(334)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}({\mathbb{H}}^{+})}\int_{({\mathbb{H}}^{+})^{2}}\prod_{k=1}^{2}\widetilde{dX}_{k}\,\delta(s-s(X_{1},X_{2}))\,g(s(X_{1},X_{2})).

(335)

The integral
g(s(X_{1},X_{2}))
is manifestly Lorentz-invariant, and must therefore be a function of its unique non-trivial two-point invariant
X_{1}\cdot X_{2}=-2S-1=1-2C
. We will compute its explicit form shortly. In the meantime, as the integral over
(X_{1},X_{2})
localizes to
s=s(X_{1},X_{2})
, we can factor out
g(s)
and compute

\frac{G(s;X_{0})}{g(s)}=\frac{1}{\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}({\mathbb{H}}^{+})}\int_{({\mathbb{H}}^{+})^{2}}\prod_{k=1}^{2}\widetilde{dX}_{k}\,\delta(s-s(X_{1},X_{2})).

(336)

There always exists a Lorentz transformation
\Lambda
such that
X_{2}=\Lambda X_{0}
. After the change of variables
X_{1}\rightarrow\Lambda X_{1}
, we can then factor out the integral over
X_{2}
into
\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}({\mathbb{H}}^{+})
to obtain

\frac{G(s;X_{0})}{g(s)}=\int_{{\mathbb{H}}^{+}}\widetilde{dX}_{1}\,\delta(s-s(X_{1},X_{0}))=\int_{\mathbb{D}}\frac{d^{2}\alpha_{1}}{(1-|\alpha_{1}|^{2})^{2}}\,\delta\left(s-\frac{|\alpha_{1}|^{2}}{1-|\alpha_{1}|^{2}}\right)=\pi.

(337)

We now finish the proof by computing
g(s)
. After introducing the stereographic projection
Y:=X(\beta)
, we can re-express
g(s(X_{1},X_{2}))
as an integral over the disk. Using Lorentz invariance to set
X_{2}=X(0)=X_{0}
, we obtain

g\left(\frac{|\alpha_{1}|^{2}}{1-|\alpha_{1}|^{2}}\right)=(1-\alpha_{1}\overline{\alpha}_{1})^{\Delta_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{D}}d^{2}\beta\,(1-\beta\overline{\beta})^{\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-2}(1-\alpha_{1}\overline{\beta})^{-\Delta_{1}}(1-\overline{\alpha}_{1}\beta)^{-\Delta_{1}}.

(338)

We now expand the factor
(1-\alpha_{1}\overline{\beta})^{-\Delta_{1}}
and its complex conjugate into the binomial series

(1-x)^{-\Delta}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(\Delta)_{k}}{k!}x^{k},\quad|x|<1,\quad(\Delta)_{k}:=\frac{\Gamma(\Delta+k)}{\Gamma(\Delta)},

(339)

and apply the orthogonality relation

\frac{\Delta-1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{D}}d^{2}\beta\,(1-|\beta|^{2})^{\Delta-2}\,\overline{\beta}^{m}\beta^{n}=\delta_{mn}\frac{k!}{(\Delta)_{k}},

(340)

with
\Delta=\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}
, onto each monomial. We obtain in this way

g\left(\frac{|\alpha_{1}|^{2}}{1-|\alpha_{1}|^{2}}\right)=\frac{\pi}{\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-1}(1-|\alpha_{1}|^{2})^{\Delta_{1}}\,{}_{2}F_{1}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{1};\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2};|\alpha_{1}|^{2}).

(341)

Note that we would have obtained the exact same function with
(\alpha_{1},\Delta_{1})\leftrightarrow(\alpha_{2},\Delta_{2})
if we had set
X_{1}=X_{0}
instead of
X_{2}=X_{0}
. This permutation symmetry becomes manifest after applying the Pfaff transformation of the Gauss hypergeometric function:

{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{1};\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2};\frac{z}{z-1}\right)=(1-z)^{\Delta_{1}}{}_{2}F_{1}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2};\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2};z).

(342)

Using
G(s;X_{0})=\pi g(s)
and
|\alpha_{1}|^{2}=s/(s+1)
, we then obtain (
81
).

C.2
Bulk coupling and boundary OPE coefficient

At leading order in perturbation theory, the relation between the cubic bulk coupling constant
\lambda\Phi_{1}\Phi_{2}\Phi_{3}
and the OPE coefficient
C_{\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\phi_{3}}
follows from the tree-level approximation to the CFT three-point function:

C_{\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\phi_{3}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq 3}(-2P_{i}\cdot P_{j})^{-\delta_{ij}}=\lambda\int d^{d+1}x\sqrt{-g}\prod_{i=1}^{3}K_{\Delta_{i}}(X,P_{i})+O(\lambda^{2}),

(343)

where
\delta_{12}:=\frac{\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{3}}{2}
, and
\delta_{23}
,
\delta_{13}
are cyclic permutations thereof. The AdS integral on the right-hand side, corresponding to a scalar three-point contact diagram, was computed explicitly in e.g.
(
72
, eq. (3.7))
. In this paper, the relevant cubic coupling is
\phi_{1}=\phi_{2}=\phi
,
\phi_{3}=\sigma
, such that

C_{\phi\phi\sigma}=\lambda\frac{\pi^{d/2}}{2}\Gamma\left(\frac{2\Delta_{\phi}+\Delta_{\sigma}-d}{2}\right)\frac{C_{\Delta_{\phi},d}}{\Gamma(\Delta_{\phi})^{2}}\frac{C_{\Delta_{\sigma},d}^{1/2}}{\Gamma(\Delta_{\sigma})}\Gamma\left(\frac{\Delta_{\sigma}}{2}\right)^{2}\Gamma\left(\Delta_{\phi}-\frac{\Delta_{\sigma}}{2}\right).

(344)

C.3
Expansion of
k_{\Delta_{\sigma}}^{(d)}
into lightcone blocks

We would like to prove the relation (
324
), which can be written as

\displaystyle{}_{2}F_{1}(h,h;2h-\varepsilon;z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{B}_{n}^{h,\varepsilon}z^{n}{}_{2}F_{1}(h+n,h+n;2h+2n;z),

(345)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{n}^{h,\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{n!}\frac{(\varepsilon)_{n}}{(2h-\varepsilon)_{n}}\frac{(h)_{n}^{2}}{(2h+n-1)_{n}},

(346)

for
h=\Delta_{\sigma}/2
and
\varepsilon=(d-2)/2
. In a power series expansion around
z=0
, it is easy to check that the equality holds at the first orders. To show the equality at all orders, we expand the right-hand side into a double sum:

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{B}_{n}^{h,\varepsilon}z^{n}{}_{2}F_{1}(h+n,h+n;2h+2n;z)=\sum_{n,k=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{B}_{n}^{h,\varepsilon}\frac{(h+n)_{k}^{2}}{k!(2h+2n)_{k}}z^{n+k}.

(347)

We then change the summation variables to
\ell:=n+k\in\mathbb{N}
and
n=0,1,\dots,\ell
. After some manipulation of Pochhammer symbols, the sum over
n
at fixed
\ell
turns into a
{}_{4}F_{3}
and the previous expression simplifies to

\sum_{n,k=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{B}_{n}^{h,\varepsilon}\frac{(h+n)_{k}^{2}}{k!(2h+2n)_{k}}z^{n+k}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\frac{(h)_{\ell}^{2}z^{\ell}}{\ell!\,(2h)_{\ell}}{}_{4}F_{3}\left(-\ell,\varepsilon,2h-1,h+1/2;2h-\varepsilon,2h+\ell,h+1/2;-1\right)

For
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, this last
{}_{4}F_{3}
reduces to

{}_{4}F_{3}\left(-\ell,\varepsilon,2h-1,h+1/2;2h-\varepsilon,2h+\ell,h+1/2;-1\right)=\frac{(2h)_{\ell}}{(2h-\varepsilon)_{\ell}},

(348)

thereby establishing the identity (
345
).

C.4
Hypergeometric identity for pairing of lightcone blocks

In this appendix, we will prove the identity

\frac{\Omega_{h_{1},h_{2},p}}{\Gamma(h_{1}+p-1)^{2}\Gamma(h_{2}+1-p)^{2}}=\frac{\Omega_{h_{1},h_{2},2-p}}{\Gamma(h_{1}+1-p)^{2}\Gamma(h_{2}+p-1)^{2}},

(349)

where
\Omega_{h,h^{\prime},p}
was defined in eq. (
326
). This relation can be seen as a generalization of the
a\leftrightarrow b
permutation symmetry for the Euler representation of the Gauss hypergeometric function:

\int_{0}^{1}\frac{dz}{z(1-z)}\frac{z^{b}(1-z)^{c-b}}{\mathrm{B}(b,c-b)}(1-xz)^{-a}=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{dz}{z(1-z)}\frac{z^{a}(1-z)^{c-a}}{\mathrm{B}(a,c-a)}(1-xz)^{-b}.

(350)

Here, we begin with a similar Euler-type integral:

\displaystyle\frac{\Omega_{h_{1},h_{2},p}}{\mathrm{B}(h_{1}+p-1,h_{2}+1-p)}=

(351)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\frac{dz}{z(1-z)}\frac{z^{h_{1}+p-1}(1-z)^{h_{2}+1-p}}{\mathrm{B}(h_{1}+p-1,h_{2}+1-p)}{}_{2}F_{1}\begin{bmatrix}h_{1},h_{1}\\
2h_{1}\end{bmatrix}(z)\,\,{}_{2}F_{1}\begin{bmatrix}h_{2},h_{2}\\
2h_{2}\end{bmatrix}(1-z).

Expanding each of the two Gauss hypergeometric functions into a power series, we obtain the following double-sum:

\frac{\Omega_{h_{1},h_{2},p}}{\mathrm{B}(h_{1}+p-1,h_{2}+1-p)}=\prod_{i=1}^{2}\sum_{n_{i}=0}^{\infty}\frac{(h_{i})_{n_{i}}^{2}}{n_{i}!(2h_{i})_{n_{i}}}\frac{(h_{1}+p-1)_{n_{1}}(h_{2}+1-p)_{n_{2}}}{(h_{1}+h_{2})_{n_{1}+n_{2}}}.

(352)

This same double-sum admits an alternative but equivalent integral representation:

\displaystyle\frac{\Omega_{h_{1},h_{2},p}}{\mathrm{B}(h_{1}+p-1,h_{2}+1-p)}=

(353)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\frac{dz}{z(1-z)}\frac{z^{h_{1}}(1-z)^{h_{2}}}{\mathrm{B}(h_{1},h_{2})}{}_{2}F_{1}\begin{bmatrix}h_{1}+p-1,h_{1}\\
2h_{1}\end{bmatrix}(z)\,\,{}_{2}F_{1}\begin{bmatrix}h_{2}+1-p,h_{2}\\
2h_{2}\end{bmatrix}(1-z).

After applying te Euler transformation of the Gauss hypergeometric function,

{}_{2}F_{1}\begin{bmatrix}a,b\\
c\end{bmatrix}(z)=(1-z)^{c-a-b}{}_{2}F_{1}\begin{bmatrix}c-a,c-b\\
c\end{bmatrix}(z),

(354)

we can rewrite the equality (
353
) as

\displaystyle\frac{\Omega_{h_{1},h_{2},p}\,\mathrm{B}(h_{1},h_{2})}{\mathrm{B}(h_{1}+p-1,h_{2}+1-p)^{2}}=

(355)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\frac{dz}{z(1-z)}\frac{z^{h_{1}+p-1}(1-z)^{h_{2}+1-p}}{\mathrm{B}(h_{1}+p-1,h_{2}+1-p)}{}_{2}F_{1}\begin{bmatrix}h_{1}+1-p,h_{1}\\
2h_{1}\end{bmatrix}(z)\,\,{}_{2}F_{1}\begin{bmatrix}h_{2}+p-1,h_{2}\\
2h_{2}\end{bmatrix}(1-z).

We obtain yet another double-sum after expanding each Gauss hypergeometric series, only now the coefficients are manifestly symmetric under
h_{1}\leftrightarrow h_{2}
, or equivalently under
p\leftrightarrow 2-p
:

\frac{\Omega_{h_{1},h_{2},p}\,\mathrm{B}(h_{1},h_{2})}{\mathrm{B}(h_{1}+p-1,h_{2}+1-p)^{2}}=\prod_{i=1}^{2}\sum_{n_{i}=0}^{\infty}\frac{(h_{i})_{n_{i}}(h_{i}+p-1)_{n_{i}}(h_{i}+1-p)_{n_{i}}}{n_{i}!(2h_{i})_{n_{i}}}.

(356)

The identity (
349
) then follows from the invariance of this expression under
p\rightarrow 2-p
.

Appendix D
Large-spin expansion of the integrals over orbits

In this appendix, we perform the explicit computation of
M_{J}(\alpha)
in (
111
) and
\mathcal{U}_{N,J}
in (
118
) to leading orders at large spin
J
in the case of
N=3
. After recalling below some general properties of these integrals at large
J
, we detail the derivation of (
122
), (
127
) for the acute region and (
131
), (
130
) for the obtuse region in the subsequent subsections.

First, note that if
U_{N}(e^{i\varphi}\alpha)=U_{N}(\alpha)
for
\varphi\in[0,2\pi)
, then the integrals are invariant under phase shifts of
\lambda
, such that we can integrate out
\arg(\lambda)
into a factor of
2\pi
and reduce the domain of integration to
(|\lambda|,\beta)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{C}
. Next, due to the factor of
|\lambda|^{-2J}
, it is easy to see that the integral at large
J
is dominated by
(|\lambda|,\beta)=(r(\alpha),b(\alpha))
, where
r
and
b
are the radius and center of the smallest enclosing circle of
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})
in the complex plane. Below, we will show that an expansion of the integrands as

(|\lambda|,\beta)=(r(\alpha),b(\alpha))+\epsilon(\delta|\lambda|,\delta\beta)+O(\epsilon^{2}),

(357)

translates into the large-spin expansion of the integrals for
\epsilon\sim 1/J
. This reproduces the formulas of section
3.2
.

The smallest circle depends on whether the three points form an acute or obtuse triangle, which separates
{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{P}}^{1}
into two regions. We will analyze the acute and obtuse regions separately, and show that they produce different large-spin expansions.

D.1
Acute region: leading order

If
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})
form an acute triangle, then all points lie on the smallest circle. The
\mathbb{C}^{\times}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
transformation
\alpha_{k}\rightarrow\xi_{k}
, where

\xi_{k}=\frac{\alpha_{k}-b(\alpha)}{r(\alpha)}=1/\overline{\xi}_{k},

(358)

maps each point to the unit circle. One retrieves the functions
M_{J},\mathcal{U}_{3,J}
in a generic acute configuraton from their values on the unit circle by the covariance relations

M_{J}(\alpha)=r(\alpha)^{2-2N-2J}M_{J}\left(\xi\right),\quad\mathcal{U}_{3,J}(\alpha)=\mathcal{U}_{3,J}\left(\xi\right).

(359)

Now, if
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})
lie on the smallest circle, then
(r(\xi),b(\xi))=(1,0)
