by definition and the quantities
r_{i}^{\prime}:=1-\lambda^{-2}|\xi_{i}-\beta|^{2}
are small in the neighborhood of
(|\lambda|,\beta)=(1,0)
. This yields a linear map between
(\delta|\lambda|,\delta\beta,\delta\overline{\beta})
in (
357
) and
(r_{1}^{\prime},r_{2}^{\prime},r_{3}^{\prime})
:

r_{i}^{\prime}=\epsilon(2\delta|\lambda|+\xi_{i}^{-1}\delta\beta+\xi_{i}\,\delta\overline{\beta})+O(\epsilon^{2}),\quad i=1,2,3.

The linear map is straightforward to invert, and we obtain in particular

\delta|\lambda|=\frac{\epsilon}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{3}r_{k}^{\prime}R_{k}(\xi)+O(\epsilon^{2}),

(360)

where
R_{k}
is given by (
123
). The Jacobian for the measure turns into

d|\lambda|d^{2}\beta=\epsilon^{3}d\delta|\lambda|d^{2}\delta\beta+O(\epsilon^{4})=\frac{1}{4}\left|\frac{\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\xi_{3}}{\xi_{12}\xi_{23}\xi_{31}}\right|d^{3}r^{\prime}(1+O(\epsilon)).

(361)

Together, formulas (
360
) and (
361
) recast
M_{J},\mathcal{U}_{3,J}
into integrals over
r_{i}^{\prime}
. Starting with
M_{J}
in (
111
), we can apply the change of variables to obtain

\displaystyle M_{J}(\xi)=\frac{(\Delta_{\phi}-1)^{3}}{12\pi^{2}}\left|\frac{\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\xi_{3}}{\xi_{12}\xi_{23}\xi_{31}}\right|\int d^{3}r^{\prime}\,\prod_{i=1}^{3}r^{\prime\Delta_{\phi}-2}_{i}\left(1+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{3}r_{i}^{\prime}R_{i}(\xi)+O(\epsilon^{2})\right)^{-(2J+2N+1)}.

If the expansion parameter of the integrand goes like
r_{i}^{\prime}\sim\epsilon\sim J^{-1}
, then the integral reduces to a product of Gamma functions after a rescaling of
r_{i}^{\prime}
by
J
, and we obtain the formula (
122
) for
M_{J}(\xi)
in the acute region.

Moving on to
\mathcal{U}_{3,J}
defined by (
118
), note that the expansion of the integrand at
r_{i}^{\prime}=O(\epsilon)
induces a large-distance expansion of the potential
U_{N}
. We assume that its leading large-distance behavior is a symmetrized sum of pair potentials
U_{2}(s_{ij})
with leading asymptotics
U_{2}(s\gg 1)=b_{0}\,s^{-\Delta_{\sigma}/2}(1+O(s))
. In this case, the
U_{N}
factor in the integrand of (
118
) can be expanded as

U_{3}\left(\frac{\xi-\beta}{\lambda}\right)=U_{3}\left(\frac{\xi-\epsilon\,\delta\beta}{1+\epsilon\,\delta|\lambda|+O(\epsilon^{2})}\right)=b_{0}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 3}\frac{(r_{i}^{\prime}r_{j}^{\prime})^{\Delta_{\sigma}/2}}{|\xi_{ij}|^{\Delta_{\sigma}}}+O(\epsilon^{\Delta_{\sigma}+2}),

(362)

such that the overall integral takes the form

\displaystyle M_{J}(\xi)\,\mathcal{U}_{3,J}(\xi)=b_{0}\frac{(\Delta_{\phi}-1)^{3}}{12\pi^{2}}\left|\frac{\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\xi_{3}}{\xi_{12}\xi_{23}\xi_{31}}\right|\int d^{3}r^{\prime}\,\prod_{i=1}^{3}r^{\prime\Delta_{\phi}-2}_{i}e^{-Jr_{i}^{\prime}R_{i}(\xi)}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 3}\frac{(r_{i}^{\prime}r_{j}^{\prime})^{\Delta_{\sigma}/2}}{|\xi_{ij}|^{\Delta_{\sigma}}}\left(1+O(\epsilon)\right).

The factors
(r_{i}^{\prime}r_{j}^{\prime})^{\Delta_{\sigma}/2}
shift two out of the three Gamma function integrals, leading to the expression (
127
) at leading order in the large-spin limit.

D.2
Acute region: first subleading order

From the leading-order analysis of the previous section, we expect that the
1/J
expansion of the functions
M_{J}(\xi)
,
\mathcal{U}_{3,J}(\xi)
can be efficiently computed via the change of variables
(|\lambda|,\beta)\rightarrow(r_{i}^{\prime})_{i=1,2,3}
and the expansion of the integrand as
r_{i}^{\prime}=O(1/J)
. At fixed order, this expansion culminates in a linear combination of Gamma function integrals of the form

\mathcal{I}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{3}y_{i}^{\nu_{i}}\right]:=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{3}}d^{3}y\prod_{i=1}^{3}y_{i}^{\Delta_{\phi}-2+\nu_{i}}e^{-y_{i}R_{i}}=\prod_{i=1}^{3}\Gamma(\Delta_{\phi}+\nu_{i}-1)R_{i}^{-(\Delta_{\phi}-1+\nu_{i})},

(363)

where
y_{i}:=r_{i}^{\prime}J=O(1)
and we introduced for future use the linear functional

\mathcal{I}[f]:=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{3}}d^{3}y\prod_{i=1}^{3}y_{i}^{\Delta_{\phi}-2+\nu_{i}}e^{-y_{i}R_{i}}f(y).

(364)

For the next-to-leading order in
1/J
, we need to invert the relation
r_{i}^{\prime}(|\lambda|,\beta)=1-|\lambda|^{-2}|\xi_{i}-\beta|^{2}
to second order around
(|\lambda|,\beta)=(1,0)
. This yields an expansion of the scale factor and Jacobian of the following form:

\displaystyle|\lambda|=1+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{3}R_{i}r_{i}^{\prime}+\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq 3}\Lambda^{(2)}_{ij}r_{i}^{\prime}r_{j}^{\prime}+O(1/J^{3}),

\displaystyle d|\lambda|d^{2}\beta=\frac{1}{4}\left|\frac{\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\xi_{3}}{\xi_{12}\xi_{23}\xi_{31}}\right|d^{3}r^{\prime}\left(1+\sum_{i=1}^{3}\mathcal{J}^{(1)}_{i}r_{i}^{\prime}+O(1/J^{2})\right).

In terms of this data, the integrand of
M_{J}
will have the expansion

\displaystyle\frac{d|\lambda|d^{2}\beta}{|\lambda|^{2J+2N+1}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}r_{i}^{\prime\Delta_{\phi}-2}=\prod_{i=1}^{3}dr_{i}^{\prime}r_{i}^{\prime\Delta_{\phi}-2}\,e^{-JR_{i}(\xi)r_{i}^{\prime}}\left(1+J^{-1}\mathcal{M}^{(1)}(Jr^{\prime},\xi)+O(J^{-2})\right),

(365)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(1)}(y,\xi)=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\left(\mathcal{J}^{(1)}_{i}(\xi)-4R_{i}(\xi)\right)y_{i}+\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq 3}\left(\frac{1}{4}R_{i}(\xi)R_{j}(\xi)-2\Lambda^{(2)}_{ij}(\xi)\right)y_{i}y_{j}.

As a result, its large-spin expansion to subleading order is

\displaystyle M_{J}(\xi)=\frac{J^{6-3\Delta_{\phi}}(\Delta_{\phi}-1)^{3}}{12\pi^{2}}\left|\frac{\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\xi_{3}}{\xi_{12}\xi_{13}\xi_{23}}\right|\left(\mathcal{I}[1]+J^{-1}\mathcal{I}[\mathcal{M}^{(1)}]+O(J^{-2})\right),

(366)

where the action of the linear functional
\mathcal{I}
on a power series in
y_{i}
follows from (
363
).

The above derivation generalizes readily to the subleading correction of
\mathcal{U}_{3,J}(\xi)
, defined from the integral (
127
). To retrieve this result, note that the corrections to the leading asymptotics of
U_{N}((\xi-\beta)/\lambda)
at
r_{i}^{\prime}\sim 1/J
are of relative order
1/J^{2}
, and therefore subleading to the
1/J
corrections in (
118
). After inserting the leading-order form (
362
) of
U_{3}
at
r_{i}^{\prime}=O(1/J)
, we obtain

\displaystyle M_{J}(\xi)\,\mathcal{U}_{3,J}(\xi)=

\displaystyle\frac{(\Delta_{\phi}-1)^{3}}{12\pi^{2}J^{3\Delta_{\phi}+\Delta_{\sigma}-6}}\left|\frac{\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\xi_{3}}{\xi_{12}\xi_{13}\xi_{23}}\right|\times

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 3}|\xi_{ij}|^{-\Delta_{\sigma}}\left(\mathcal{I}[(y_{i}y_{j})^{\Delta_{\sigma}/2}]+J^{-1}\mathcal{I}[(y_{i}y_{j})^{\Delta_{\sigma}/2}\mathcal{M}^{(1)}]+O(J^{-2})\right),

(367)

where the functionals can again be evaluated from (
363
). Dividing (
367
) by (
366
) and expanding to the subleading order
J^{-\Delta_{\sigma}-1}
, we finally obtain the full formula (
127
).

D.3
Obtuse region: leading order

If
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})
form an obtuse triangle, then only two out of three points lie on the smallest circle; without loss of generality, we assume the latter two are
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
. In this case, the radius and center of the smallest circle are
(r(\alpha),b(\alpha))=(|\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}|,\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})/2
. The
\mathbb{C}^{\times}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
transformation
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})\rightarrow(1,-1,z)
such that
(r,b)\rightarrow(1,0)
is then given by

\alpha_{3}\rightarrow z=\frac{2\alpha_{3}-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}}{\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}}.

(368)

In this gauge, the smallest circle is the unit circle which must enclose the third point, i.e.
|z|<1
. We can again reconstruct the functions at a generic obtuse triangle configuration from
(1,-1,z)
via the covariance relations

\displaystyle M_{J}(\alpha)=\left|\frac{\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}}{2}\right|^{2-2N-2J}M_{J}(1,-1,z),\quad\mathcal{U}_{3,J}(\alpha)=\mathcal{U}_{3,J}(1,-1,z).

(369)

Consider now the integral
M_{J}(1,-1,z)
in (
111
). The integrand is a product of powers
r_{i}^{\prime\Delta_{\phi}-2}
, where

r_{1}^{\prime}=1-\left|\frac{1-\beta}{\lambda}\right|^{2},\quad r_{2}^{\prime}=1-\left|\frac{1+\beta}{\lambda}\right|^{2},\quad r_{3}^{\prime}=1-\left|\frac{z-\beta}{\lambda}\right|^{2}.

(370)

In these expressions, it is only for
i=1,2
that
r_{i}^{\prime}\rightarrow 0
as
(|\lambda|,\beta)\rightarrow(1,0)
, while
r_{3}^{\prime}\rightarrow 1-|z|^{2}
is finite. At leading order in the expansion (
360
), the integrand therefore factorizes as

M_{J}(1,-1,z)=\frac{(\Delta_{\phi}-1)^{3}}{3\pi^{2}}\int\frac{d^{2}\lambda d^{2}\beta}{|\lambda|^{2(J+4)}}\left(1-\left|\frac{1-\beta}{\lambda}\right|^{2}\right)_{+}^{\Delta_{\phi}-2}\left(1-\left|\frac{1+\beta}{\lambda}\right|^{2}\right)_{+}^{\Delta_{\phi}-2}(1-|z|^{2})^{\Delta_{\phi}-2}(1+O(\epsilon)).

To compute the integral in this approximation, we make the change of variables

(\lambda^{-1},\lambda^{-1}\beta):=\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{2}^{\prime}-\alpha_{1}^{\prime},\alpha_{1}^{\prime}+\alpha_{2}^{\prime}),\quad\frac{d^{2}\lambda d^{2}\beta}{|\lambda|^{2(J+N+1)}}=\frac{d^{2}\alpha_{1}^{\prime}d^{2}\alpha_{2}^{\prime}}{4^{J+1}}|\alpha_{1}^{\prime}-\alpha_{2}^{\prime}|^{2(J+1)},

(371)

after which
r_{i}^{\prime}=1-|\alpha_{i}^{\prime}|^{2}
for
i=1,2
. We can then rewrite the leading-order integral as

\displaystyle M_{J}(1,-1,z)

\displaystyle=\frac{(\Delta_{\phi}-1)^{3}}{12\pi^{2}4^{J}}\int_{\mathbb{D}^{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}d^{2}\alpha_{i}^{\prime}(1-|\alpha_{i}^{\prime}|^{2})^{\Delta_{\phi}-2}|\alpha_{1}^{\prime}-\alpha_{2}^{\prime}|^{2(J+1)}(1-|z|^{2})^{\Delta_{\phi}-2}+\dots

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta_{\phi}-1}{12\,4^{J}}\langle\psi_{J+1}|\psi_{J+1}\rangle(1-|z|^{2})^{\Delta_{\phi}-2}+\dots,

where
\psi_{J}(\alpha_{1}^{\prime},\alpha_{2}^{\prime})=(\alpha_{1}^{\prime}-\alpha_{2}^{\prime})^{J}
is the unique two-particle, minimal-twist, primary wavefunction at spin
J
(up to a multiplicative constant). Its norm-squared
\langle\psi_{J}|\psi_{J}\rangle
can be computed using the methods of section
2.7
, where it is mapped to the integral
\langle s^{J}\rangle_{\Delta_{\phi}+J,\Delta_{\phi}+J}
defined by (
82
). In these consecutive changes of variables
(\lambda,\beta)\rightarrow(\alpha_{1}^{\prime},\alpha_{2}^{\prime})\rightarrow s
, we can keep track of the original expansion of the integrand by noting that the two-point invariant
s
diverges as

s=\frac{|\alpha_{1}^{\prime}-\alpha_{2}^{\prime}|^{2}}{(1-|\alpha_{1}^{\prime}|^{2})(1-|\alpha_{2}^{\prime}|^{2})}=\frac{4}{(|\lambda|^{2}-|1-\beta|^{2})(|\lambda|^{2}-|1+\beta|^{2})}=O(1/\epsilon^{2}),

(372)

when
(|\lambda|,\beta)=(1,0)+O(\epsilon)
. We then retrieve the large-
J
expansion of the norm-squared by setting
\epsilon\sim 1/\sqrt{s}\sim 1/J
. Assuming this scaling applies for the full expansion of the integrand, we find

M_{J}(1,-1,z)=\frac{(\Delta_{\phi}-1)^{3}}{12\pi^{2}}4^{-J}\langle s^{J+1}\rangle_{\Delta_{\phi}+J+1,\Delta_{\phi}+J+1}(1-|z|^{2})^{\Delta_{\phi}-2}\left(1+O(1/J)\right).

(373)

The function
\langle s^{J}\rangle_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}}
, defined from the integral (
82
), can be computed explicitly from the Mellin-Barnes representation of the hypergeometric function:

\langle s^{J}\rangle_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}}=\pi^{2}\frac{\Gamma(J+1)\Gamma(\Delta_{1}-J-1)\Gamma(\Delta_{2}-J-1)\Gamma(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-1)}{\Gamma(\Delta_{1})\Gamma(\Delta_{2})\Gamma(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+J-1)}

(374)

Its leading large-spin expression then follows from the Stirling formula for the Gamma functions.

We now use the same procedure to determine
\mathcal{U}_{3,J}(1,-1,z)
in the obtuse region. Specifically, in the integral (
118
), the function
U_{3}((\alpha-\beta)/\lambda)
is expanded around
r_{1}^{\prime},r_{2}^{\prime}=0
and
r_{3}^{\prime}=1-|z|^{2}
in the limit
(|\lambda|,\beta)\rightarrow(1,0)
. In terms of hyperbolic distances, this limit translates to
s_{13},s_{23}=O(\epsilon^{-1})
and
s_{12}=O(\epsilon^{-2})
. If we again assume a decomposition into a sum of pair potentials
U_{2}(s_{ij})
with leading asymptotics
U_{2}(s)=b_{0}s^{-\Delta_{\sigma}/2}(1+O(1/s))
, then the function
U_{N}
takes the form

\displaystyle U_{3}\left(\frac{1-\beta}{\lambda},\frac{-1-\beta}{\lambda},\frac{z-\beta}{\lambda}\right)=

\displaystyle b_{0}\left[\left(r_{1}^{\prime}\frac{1-|z|^{2}}{|1-z|^{2}}\right)^{\Delta_{\sigma}/2}+\left(r_{2}^{\prime}\frac{1-|z|^{2}}{|1+z|^{2}}\right)^{\Delta_{\sigma}/2}\right]

\displaystyle+O(\epsilon^{\Delta_{\sigma}},\epsilon^{\Delta_{\sigma}/2+1}).

(375)

Plugging this back into the integral, we find

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{3,J}(1,-1,z)=

\displaystyle b_{0}\frac{\langle s^{J+1}\rangle_{\Delta_{\phi}+\Delta_{\sigma}/2+J+1,\Delta_{\phi}+J+1}}{\langle s^{J+1}\rangle_{\Delta_{\phi}+J+1,\Delta_{\phi}+J+1}}\left[\left(\frac{1-|z|^{2}}{|1-z|^{2}}\right)^{\Delta_{\sigma}/2}+\left(\frac{1-|z|^{2}}{|1+z|^{2}}\right)^{\Delta_{\sigma}/2}\right]

\displaystyle+O(J^{-\Delta_{\sigma}},J^{-\Delta_{\sigma}/2-2})

(376)

Applying the large-spin expansion of the functions
\langle s^{J}\rangle
in (
374
), we are left with

\frac{\langle s^{J+1}\rangle_{\Delta_{\phi}+\Delta_{\sigma}/2+J+1,\Delta_{\phi}+J+1}}{\langle s^{J+1}\rangle_{\Delta_{\phi}+J+1,\Delta_{\phi}+J+1}}=\frac{\Gamma(\Delta_{\phi}+\Delta_{\sigma}/2-1)}{\Gamma(\Delta_{\phi}-1)}2^{-\Delta_{\sigma}/2}J^{-\Delta_{\sigma}/2}(1+O(1/J)),

(377)

which yields the final expression (
132
) for the leading asmyptotics of the effective potential in the obtuse region.

Appendix E
Calculus of pseudodifferential operators

This appendix reviews aspects of the calculus of pseudodifferential operators relevant for applications to the semiclassical analysis of sections
3
and
4
.

A general definition of pseudodifferential operators is given in section
E.1
, which is then specialized to non-integer powers of differential operators in section
E.2
. Next, we use pseudodifferential calculus to compute the large-spin expansion of the function
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{N,J}(z)=J^{-\Delta_{\sigma}}H_{\mathrm{symb}}(z)
defined by (
240
) in section
E.3
. Finally, section
E.4
outlines a formal derivation of Bohr-Sommerfeld conditions in the case of one degree of freedom.

E.1
Definition and asymptotic expansion

We consider spaces of functions
\psi
on
z=(z_{1},\dots,z_{N})\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
that admit a WKB expansion of the form

\psi(z)=e^{iJS_{0}(z)}f(z),

(378)

Then our definition of a pseudodifferential operator
\mathcal{P}(z,\partial_{z})
, following closely that of Hörmander
[
57
]
, is a linear and holomorphic operator whose action
e^{-iJS_{0}}\mathcal{P}e^{iJS_{0}}f
admits an asymptotic expansion around
J=\infty
. This expansion takes the form

e^{-iJS_{0}}\mathcal{P}e^{iJS_{0}}f=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{P}_{k}[f,S_{0}]\,J^{-\tau_{k}},

(379)

where
(\tau_{k})_{k}
is a monotonically increasing sequence with
\tau_{k}\rightarrow\infty
as
k\rightarrow\infty
. Linearity of
\mathcal{P}
implies that
\mathcal{P}_{k}
is homogeneous of degree
-\tau_{k}
in
S_{0}
and linear in
f
. For any
S_{0},f
, the action (
379
) can be reconstructed from the case

f\equiv 1,\quad g(z)=\langle z,p_{z}\rangle:=z_{1}p_{z_{1}}+\dots+z_{N}p_{z_{N}},\quad(\mathcal{P})_{k}(z,p_{z}):=\mathcal{P}_{k}[1,\langle z,p_{z}\rangle],

(380)

where we call
(\mathcal{P})_{k}
the
k
’th symbol function, and
(\mathcal{P})_{0}
is often called the principal symbol. The explicit formula relating
\mathcal{P}_{k}
and
(\mathcal{P})_{k}
is

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}J^{-\tau_{k}}\mathcal{P}_{k}[f(z),S_{0}(z)]=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\sum_{a\in\mathbb{N}^{N}}\frac{1}{a!}\partial_{p_{z}}^{a}(\mathcal{P})_{\ell}(z,J\partial_{z}S_{0}(z))(-i\partial_{w})^{a}e^{iJh_{z}(w)}f(w)\Bigl|_{w=z},

(381)

where we introduced the multi-index notation

a!:=a_{1}!\dots a_{N}!,\quad\partial_{p_{z}}^{a}:=\partial_{p_{z_{1}}}^{a_{1}}\dots\partial_{p_{z_{N}}}^{a_{N}},

and the function

h_{z}(w):=S_{0}(w)-S_{0}(z)-\sum_{k=1}^{N}(w_{k}-z_{k})\partial_{z_{k}}S_{0}(z).

(382)

To decompose the right-hand side of (
381
) into an asymptotic expansion at
J=\infty
that matches the left-hand side, note that
(\mathcal{P}_{k})^{(a)}
is homogeneous of degree
-\tau_{k}-|a|
in
J
, where
|a|=a_{1}+\dots+a_{N}
. This function multiplies
(-i\partial_{w})^{a}e^{iJh_{z}(w)}f(w)|_{w=z}
. Using the Leibniz rule and the fact that
h_{z}(z)=0
, the latter turns into a polynomial of degree
|a|
in
J
. In fact, since
\partial_{w_{k}}h_{z}(w)=0
at
w=z
by virtue of (
382
), this polynomial is actually of degree at most
|a|/2
in
J
. Consequently, the restriction to
\mathcal{P}_{k}
at order
J^{-\tau_{k}}
on the left-hand side implies a truncation of the sum to
\ell<k
and
|a|\leq 2(\tau_{k}-\tau_{\ell})
on the right-hand side. For this paper, we only use the first two orders in the case where
\tau_{k}=\Delta_{\sigma}+k
, yielding

\mathcal{P}_{0}[f,S_{0}]=(\mathcal{P})_{0}(z,\partial_{z}S_{0})\,f,

(383)

and

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{P}_{1}[f,S_{0}]}{f}=(\mathcal{P})_{1}(z,\partial_{z}S_{0})-\frac{i}{2}\sum_{|a|=2}\partial_{p_{z}}^{a}(\mathcal{P})_{0}(z,\partial_{z}S_{0})\partial_{z}^{a}S_{0}-i\langle\partial_{p}(\mathcal{P})_{0}(z,\partial_{z}S_{0}),\partial_{z}\rangle.

(384)

E.2
Powers of a differential operator

The operators appearing in (
240
) are of the form
({}^{t}L_{ij})^{u}
, where
{}^{t}L_{ij}
is a second-order differential operator and
u
is a non-integer exponent. More generally, the class of pseudodifferential operators corresponding to complex powers
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{D}^{u}
of a differential operator
\mathcal{D}
was studied by Seeley in
[
73
]
. The latter makes sense as a pseudodifferential operator if
(\mathcal{D})_{0}(z,\partial S_{0}(z))\neq 0
on the domain of
f,S_{0}
. In this case, an explicit procedure to extract the symbol functions of
\mathcal{D}^{u}
from the those of
\mathcal{D}
was given in
[
73
]
, based on the residues of the resolvent:

(\mathcal{D}^{u})_{k}(z,p_{z})=-\oint\frac{d\lambda}{2\pi i}\lambda^{u}\left(\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}-\lambda}\right)_{k}(z,p_{z}).

(385)

The symbols of the resolvent can be computed from the formula in
(
57
, Thm. 4.3)
for the symbol functions
(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{Q})_{j}
of a product
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{Q}
of pseudodifferential operators:

(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{Q})_{j}=\sum_{k+\ell+|a|=j}\frac{1}{a!}(\mathcal{Q})_{k}^{(a)}(-i\partial_{z})^{a}(\mathcal{P})_{\ell}.

(386)

If
\mathcal{P}=(\mathcal{D}-\lambda)^{-1}
and
\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{D}-\lambda
, then
(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{Q})_{j}=\delta_{j0}
and we can solve (
386
) order-by-order for the symbol functions of
\mathcal{P}
. Plugging these back into (
385
), the contour integral over
\lambda
is performed by picking up the poles and applying the residue theorem.

We will need explicit formulas for the leading and subleading symbol functions of
\mathcal{D}^{u}
, i.e.
k=0,1
in (
385
). These, in turn, depend on the leading and subleading symbol functions of the resolvent:

\left(\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}-\lambda}\right)_{0}=-\frac{1}{\lambda-(\mathcal{D})_{0}},\quad\left(\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}-\lambda}\right)_{1}=-\frac{(\mathcal{D})_{1}}{(\lambda-(\mathcal{D})_{0})^{2}}+i\frac{\langle\partial_{p_{z}}(\mathcal{D})_{0},\partial_{z}(\mathcal{D})_{0}\rangle}{(\lambda-(\mathcal{D})_{0})^{3}}.

(387)

We thus obtain a leading symbol

(\mathcal{D}^{u})_{0}=(\mathcal{D})_{0}^{u},

(388)

and a subleading symbol

(\mathcal{D}^{u})_{1}=u(\mathcal{D})_{0}^{u-1}(\mathcal{D})_{1}-\frac{i}{2}u(u-1)(\mathcal{D})_{0}^{u-2}\langle\partial_{p_{z}}(\mathcal{D})_{0},\partial_{z}(\mathcal{D})_{0}\rangle.

(389)

E.3
Application to Toeplitz operator in momentum space

We can now reformulate (
240
) in the language of pseudodifferential operators and apply it to the expansion (
251
) of the Toeplitz operator’s symbol. First, the function
\tilde{M}_{J}
admits a large-spin expansion of the form

\tilde{M}_{J}(z)=\text{const}\times e^{iJS_{0}(z)}f(z),\quad S_{0}=i\mathcal{K},

(390)

where
\mathcal{K}
is given by (
243
) and
f
to leading order at large
J
is given by (
245
). Next, the operator
{}^{t}H_{N}(z,\partial_{z})
is the symmetrization of
U_{\mathrm{Cas}}\left({}^{t}L_{ij}\right)
, where
{}^{t}L_{ij}
in (
239
) is the transpose of the quadratic Casimir operator and the function
U_{\mathrm{Cas}}
admits a series expansion of the form

U_{\mathrm{Cas}}(L)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}b_{\mathrm{Cas},n}L^{-\Delta_{\sigma}/2-n}.

(391)

We can therefore determine the action of
{}^{t}H_{N}
on
\tilde{M}_{J}
from the action of
\mathcal{D}^{u}
on
e^{iJS_{0}}f
, where
\mathcal{D}={}^{t}L_{ij}
and
u=-\Delta_{\sigma}/2-n
.

The quadratic Casimir operators
L_{ij}(z,\partial_{z})
in momentum space are given by (
227
). As second-order differential operators, their exponents are
\tau_{k}(L)=2-k
and their asymptotic expansion truncates for
k>2
. The three non-zero symbol functions are

\displaystyle(L_{ij})_{0}(z,p_{z})=z_{i}z_{j}(p_{z_{i}}-p_{z_{j}})^{2},

(392)

\displaystyle(L_{ij})_{1}(z,p_{z})=i\Delta_{\phi}(z_{i}-z_{j})(p_{z_{i}}-p_{z_{j}}),

(393)

\displaystyle(L_{ij})_{2}(z,p_{z})=\Delta_{\phi}(\Delta_{\phi}-1).

(394)

Similarly, the symbol functions of their transpose (
239
) are

\displaystyle\left({}^{t}L_{ij}\right)_{0}(z,p_{z})=z_{i}z_{j}(p_{z_{i}}-p_{z_{j}})^{2}=(L_{ij})_{0}(z,p_{z}),

(395)

\displaystyle\left({}^{t}L_{ij}\right)_{1}(z,p_{z})=i(2-\Delta_{\phi})(z_{i}-z_{j})(p_{z_{i}}-p_{z_{j}}),

(396)

\displaystyle\left({}^{t}L_{ij}\right)_{2}(z,p_{z})=(2-\Delta_{\phi})(1-\Delta_{\phi}).

(397)

From the three functions above, we can fully reconstruct the action of
{}^{t}H_{N}
on (
390
) using the methods of the previous two sections. For calculations in this paper, we will only use the leading
k=0
and subleading
k=1
symbol functions of
{}^{t}H_{N}
, with corresponding exponents
\tau_{k}(H)=\Delta_{\sigma}+k
. The leading symbol is given by

\left({}^{t}H_{N}\right)_{0}(z,p_{z})=b_{\mathrm{Cas},0}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq N}\left(L_{ij}\right)_{0}(z,p_{z})^{-\Delta_{\sigma}/2}.

(398)

Plugging this into (
383
) for
\mathcal{P}={}^{t}H_{N}
, we then obtain the leading term
H_{\mathrm{symb}}^{(0)}(z)
in the expansion (
251
). Since the differential operators
{}^{t}L_{ij}
have leading exponent
\tau_{0}(L)=2
, the subleading symbol of
{}^{t}H_{N}
is still captured by the leading power in the large-argument expansion (
391
) of
U_{\mathrm{Cas}}(L)
:

\left({}^{t}H_{N}\right)_{1}(z,p_{z})=b_{\mathrm{Cas},0}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq N}\left({}^{t}L_{ij}^{-\Delta_{\sigma}/2}\right)_{1}(z,p_{z}).

(399)

The symbol functions on the right-hand side are obtained by application of formula (
389
) with
\mathcal{D}={}^{t}L_{ij}
and
u=-\Delta_{\sigma}/2
. Plugging this into (
384
) for
\mathcal{P}={}^{t}H_{N}
, we obtain the subleading symbol
H_{\mathrm{symb}}^{(1)}(z)
in the expansion (
251
).

The symbols of the Toeplitz operator at higher orders
k>1
will also depend on the coefficients
b_{\mathrm{Cas},n>0}
in (
391
) and the higher-order expansion of
f=e^{J\mathcal{K}}\tilde{M}_{J}(z)
, where
\tilde{M}_{J}
is given by (
236
). Given this data, the pseudodifferential calculus of the two previous sections allows for an algorithmic reconstruction
H_{\mathrm{symb}}^{(k)}
at arbitrary order
k
.

E.4
A formal derivation of Bohr-Sommerfeld conditions

In this section we sketch a formal derivation of the leading and the subleading Bohr-Sommerfeld conditions (
180
) for a special class of Hamiltonians. Specifically, we consider an eigenvalue problem

\displaystyle H\psi=E\psi

(400)

for a holomorphic pseudodifferential operator
\mathcal{P}
acting on holomorphic functions
\psi(z)
of one variable. We assume that the functions
\psi
belong to a Hilbert space with the inner product

\displaystyle\langle\psi_{1}|\psi_{2}\rangle=\int d^{2}z\mu e^{-J\mathcal{K}}\overline{\psi}_{1}\psi_{2},

(401)

for some (non-holomorphic) functions
\mathcal{K}
and
\mu
on
\mathbb{C}
. For simplicity, we assume that the exponents
\tau_{k}
in the large-
J
expansion (
379
) in the case of
H
are given by
\tau_{k}=k
.

We will also assume that
H
is equivalent to a Toeplitz operator via the logic described in section
4.4
. Specifically, we will assume

\displaystyle\langle\psi_{1}|H|\psi_{2}\rangle=\int d^{2}z\mu e^{-J\mathcal{K}}\overline{\psi}_{1}\psi_{2}H_{\text{symb}}

(402)

for some symbol
H_{\text{symb}}=H_{\text{symb}}(z,\overline{z})
. We have

\displaystyle H_{\text{symb}}=\left(\mu^{-1}e^{J\mathcal{K}}\right){}^{t}H\left(\mu e^{-J\mathcal{K}}\right).

(403)

The symbol
H_{\text{symb}}
has an expansion of the form
H_{\text{symb}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}J^{-k}H_{\text{symb}}^{(k)}
, where the symbols
H_{\text{symb}}^{(k)}
can be determined from the symbol functions
(H)_{k}
using the discussion in the preceding subsections. In particular, the leading symbol is given by (see (
383
))

\displaystyle H_{\text{symb}}^{(0)}(z,\overline{z})=({}^{t}H)_{0}(z,i\partial_{z}\mathcal{K}(z,\overline{z}))=(H)_{0}(z,-i\partial_{z}\mathcal{K}(z,\overline{z})).

(404)

More generally, the relationship between the symbol functions of
H
and its transpose
{}^{t}H
is given in
[
57
]
.

Using the WKB ansatz

\displaystyle\psi=fe^{iJS_{0}},

(405)

the leading-order part of (
400
) becomes simply

\displaystyle(H)_{0}(z,\partial_{z}S_{0}(z))=E.

(406)

This equation can be solved for
\partial_{z}S_{0}(z)
, which can then be integrated to give
S_{0}(z)
. Single-valuedness of
\psi
then requires

\displaystyle J\oint_{\Gamma}\partial_{z}S_{0}(z)dz=2\pi k

(407)

along closed contours
\Gamma
. Setting
\Gamma=\Gamma_{E}
the constant-energy contour on which
H_{\text{symb}}^{(0)}=E
and taking (
404
) into account, we find

\displaystyle J\oint_{\Gamma_{E}}\partial_{z}S_{0}(z)dz=-iJ\oint_{\Gamma_{E}}\partial_{z}\mathcal{K}(z,\overline{z})dz=c_{0}(E).

(408)

This immediately leads to the leading-order Bohr-Sommerfeld condition

\displaystyle c_{0}(E)+\cdots=2\pi k.

(409)

Furthermore,
c_{0}(E)
gets the interpretation of the (leading-order) phase picked up by
\psi
along the contour
\Gamma_{E}
.

The subleading Bohr-Sommerfeld condition can be derived by analysing the subleading term in the equation (
400
) using the methods described in this appendix.
29
29

29

We use the expansion scheme in which we define
S_{0}
as the solution to (
406
) for the exact value of
E
. The latter then defines
f
, for which we seek a
1/J
expansion.
Similarly to the above, the subleading term allows one to determine
\partial_{z}\log f
up to
O(1/J)
corrections. The single-valuedness condition for
\psi
is

\displaystyle J\oint_{\Gamma}\partial_{z}S_{0}dz-i\oint_{\Gamma}\partial_{z}\log f=2\pi k.

(410)

We have already shown that for
\Gamma=\Gamma_{E}
the first term reduces to
c_{0}(E)
. Our claim is that the second term reduces to
c_{1}(E)-\pi+O(1/J)
. This can be shown using derivatives of (
404
) and the relation
\partial_{z}H_{\text{symb}}^{(0)}dz+\partial_{z}H_{\text{symb}}^{(0)}d\overline{z}=0
on
\Gamma_{E}
. The detailed calculation is straightforward but tedious, so we omit it here.
